The paper provides a proof-of-concept by measuring the traffic generated per prompt for various open-source LLMs, estimating an average size of 7.593 KB per prompt in 2025. It envisions AI as a potential "killer application," potentially saturating networks similarly to Peer-to-Peer traffic and Video-on-demand in previous decades.  The paper forecasts a significant increase in Internet traffic due to widespread AI agent adoption in the coming years.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Timeliness:** The paper addresses a current and important trend – the growing use of LLMs and GenAI.  Forecasting the implications of these technologies on internet infrastructure is crucial for network operators and service providers.
    *   **Practical Experimentation:** The proof-of-concept experiment provides valuable insights into the actual traffic generated by LLM interactions.  The inclusion of source code enhances reproducibility and allows others to build upon the work.
    *   **Quantitative Analysis:**  The paper offers quantifiable estimates of traffic volumes based on different usage scenarios, providing a tangible basis for network planning.
    *   **Well-Structured:** The paper is logically organized, clearly defining the problem, methodology, results, and potential implications.
    *   **Comprehensive scope**: The paper takes into account the current state of the market of LLMs, a forecast on traffic and a forecast on user trends, making it comprehensive.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplified Model:**  The traffic forecast model, while useful as a starting point, is quite simple. It assumes a single request-response exchange per interaction and relies on average values. It doesn't account for variability in response sizes based on prompt complexity, multimedia data transfer, or the evolution of more efficient LLM architectures.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** The experiment uses a specific dataset (GSM8K) which may not fully represent the diversity of real-world LLM applications. Experiments using different types of tasks could provide a more thorough evaluation.
    *   **Assumptions on Adoption Rates:**  The paper assumes high adoption rates and increasing query frequencies. The real-world scenario may differ. Factors like cost, privacy concerns, and user acceptance could influence adoption.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Countermeasures:** While the paper highlights the potential traffic burden, it only briefly touches upon possible mitigation strategies or network adaptations needed to handle the increased load.
    *   **Limited exploration on traffic mitigation**: The paper lacks the introduction to methods and technologies that would mitigate the traffic increase.
    *   **Limited technical discussion**: The paper shows the importance of its topic, but it has scarce technical discussion about the core aspects of traffic generation, mitigation, and forecasting for LLMs.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the application of quantitative traffic analysis to the emerging field of LLM-driven interactions. While the basic concepts are well-established, the application to LLMs and the resulting forecasts are new. The significance is in alerting network operators to the potential challenges and encouraging proactive planning for a future dominated by AI agents. However, its simplified experiment and analysis somewhat limit the impact of the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:**

The paper could serve as a starting point for more in-depth research into LLM traffic patterns and network optimization strategies. It can also influence network planning and investment decisions by highlighting the need to accommodate the growing demands of AI applications.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper is valuable in raising awareness about a crucial future trend. It offers a foundation for further research and provides initial estimates of traffic volume. However, the simplicity of the model and limited scope of the experimental evaluation prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The lack of a more nuanced forecast, coupled with the absence of technical details about traffic generation and mitigation strategies, reduces the significance of the contribution. However, its clear and concise nature makes it very attractive for readers outside the research community.

- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Jailbreak Tax: How Useful are Your Jailbreak Outputs?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10694v1)
- **Authors**: Kristina Nikolić, Luze Sun, Jie Zhang, Florian Tramèr
- **Abstract**: Jailbreak attacks bypass the guardrails of large language models to produce harmful outputs. In this paper, we ask whether the model outputs produced by existing jailbreaks are actually useful. For example, when jailbreaking a model to give instructions for building a bomb, does the jailbreak yield good instructions? Since the utility of most unsafe answers (e.g., bomb instructions) is hard to evaluate rigorously, we build new jailbreak evaluation sets with known ground truth answers, by aligning models to refuse questions related to benign and easy-to-evaluate topics (e.g., biology or math). Our evaluation of eight representative jailbreaks across five utility benchmarks reveals a consistent drop in model utility in jailbroken responses, which we term the jailbreak tax. For example, while all jailbreaks we tested bypass guardrails in models aligned to refuse to answer math, this comes at the expense of a drop of up to 92% in accuracy. Overall, our work proposes the jailbreak tax as a new important metric in AI safety, and introduces benchmarks to evaluate existing and future jailbreaks. We make the benchmark available at https://github.com/ethz-spylab/jailbreak-tax
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The Jailbreak Tax: How Useful are Your Jailbreak Outputs?":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of a "jailbreak tax" – the degradation in model performance when using jailbreak attacks to circumvent safety alignments in large language models (LLMs). The authors argue that while many jailbreak attacks successfully bypass safety guardrails, they often come at the cost of reduced utility or accuracy in the model's responses.  They develop benchmarks consisting of tasks where model utility can be objectively evaluated (e.g., math problems, multiple-choice questions) that are then made to *appear* as harmful or undesirable. They evaluate several popular jailbreak techniques on these benchmarks, revealing a significant jailbreak tax in some cases, even when jailbreak success rates are high.  The paper emphasizes the importance of considering the jailbreak tax as a key metric in evaluating AI safety and releases their benchmarks to the community.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of a "jailbreak tax" is relatively novel and provides a valuable addition to the current discourse on LLM safety. While prior work has investigated jailbreak success rates, this paper explicitly focuses on the *utility* of the outputs, moving beyond simple "success/failure" of bypassing safeguards. The methodology of creating datasets that mimic harmful scenarios while retaining verifiable ground truth adds a layer of rigor often absent in jailbreak evaluation. The idea of making typically helpful scenarios "appear harmful" is clever.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its call for a more nuanced approach to evaluating jailbreak attacks. It highlights that simply breaking through safety mechanisms isn't enough; the quality and usefulness of the resulting responses must also be considered. The benchmarks provided allow researchers to directly compare the utility degradation caused by different jailbreak methods, fostering a more informed discussion about their relative risks.  The findings demonstrate that not all jailbreaks are equal in their impact on model capabilities, even when they achieve similar success rates in bypassing safeguards. This has important implications for the design and evaluation of future defenses.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Definition and Concept:** The paper clearly defines the jailbreak tax and motivates its importance.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The use of objective evaluation metrics (accuracy on math problems, multiple-choice questions) strengthens the validity of the findings. Creating adversarial datasets with known ground truths mitigates the subjectivity inherent in evaluating harmful content generation.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation covers a range of jailbreak techniques and alignment methods.
    *   **Open Source:**  Releasing the benchmarks ensures reproducibility and facilitates further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Utility:** The benchmarks focus primarily on tasks with easily verifiable correctness (math, multiple-choice questions). While this ensures rigor, it might not fully capture the utility degradation in more open-ended and creative tasks or when using LLMs for complex reasoning. It is hard to know if this idea generalizes to other tasks.
    *   **Reliance on Pseudo-Alignment:** By transforming the tasks instead of explicitly evaluating harmful outputs, the paper introduces a level of abstraction.  The assumption that these transformed tasks accurately represent the model's behavior in genuinely harmful scenarios requires further validation. While the paper attempts to alleviate this concern using UnicornMath, the limitation remains.
    *   **Model Choice and Alignment:** The bulk of the experimental results focus on LLaMA 3.1 models and variations of system prompt/finetuned alignment. Findings may not generalize across significantly different model architectures or alignment strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the direction of AI safety research by prompting a shift towards more comprehensive evaluations of jailbreak attacks. It could encourage the development of new metrics and benchmarks that capture the utility of LLM outputs in various domains. The emphasis on quantifiable, objective measures could also contribute to the development of more robust and reliable defenses against jailbreak attacks.

The approach of creating pseudo-harmful scenarios to study jailbreaks has its limitations, particularly regarding the extent to which the results transfer to genuinely harmful contexts. However, the framework is valuable, and the concept of measuring jailbreak utility, however indirectly, is a significant improvement over simply measuring bypass success. While it could benefit from exploring different tasks, the framework offers a good foundation for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper introduces a novel and useful concept ("jailbreak tax") and provides a rigorous methodology for evaluating it. It highlights a crucial aspect of LLM safety that has often been overlooked. The open-source nature of the benchmark and the comprehensiveness of the evaluation are strong points. However, the reliance on pseudo-alignment and the limited scope of utility limit the generalizability of the findings and their relevance to real-world harmful scenarios, precluding it from receiving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can LLMs Classify CVEs? Investigating LLMs Capabilities in Computing CVSS Vectors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10713v1)
- **Authors**: Francesco Marchiori, Denis Donadel, Mauro Conti
- **Abstract**: Common Vulnerability and Exposure (CVE) records are fundamental to cybersecurity, offering unique identifiers for publicly known software and system vulnerabilities. Each CVE is typically assigned a Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) score to support risk prioritization and remediation. However, score inconsistencies often arise due to subjective interpretations of certain metrics. As the number of new CVEs continues to grow rapidly, automation is increasingly necessary to ensure timely and consistent scoring. While prior studies have explored automated methods, the application of Large Language Models (LLMs), despite their recent popularity, remains relatively underexplored. In this work, we evaluate the effectiveness of LLMs in generating CVSS scores for newly reported vulnerabilities. We investigate various prompt engineering strategies to enhance their accuracy and compare LLM-generated scores against those from embedding-based models, which use vector representations classified via supervised learning. Our results show that while LLMs demonstrate potential in automating CVSS evaluation, embedding-based methods outperform them in scoring more subjective components, particularly confidentiality, integrity, and availability impacts. These findings underscore the complexity of CVSS scoring and suggest that combining LLMs with embedding-based methods could yield more reliable results across all scoring components.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can LLMs Classify CVEs? Investigating LLMs Capabilities in Computing CVSS Vectors" explores the applicability of Large Language Models (LLMs) in automating the generation of CVSS vectors from CVE descriptions. The authors evaluate various prompt engineering strategies for LLMs and compare their performance against embedding-based models that use traditional Machine Learning (ML) for classification. The results indicate that while LLMs show promise in automating CVSS evaluation, embedding-based methods generally outperform them in scoring more subjective components like confidentiality, integrity, and availability.  The paper proposes a hybrid approach combining LLMs and embedding-based methods as a potential solution for improving overall accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its comprehensive analysis of applying general-purpose LLMs to the specific problem of generating CVSS vectors from CVE descriptions. While prior work has explored automated CVSS scoring, the systematic evaluation of LLMs, particularly across different prompt engineering techniques and comparison with traditional embedding-based methods, provides a valuable contribution. Prior work on LLMs in cybersecurity has touched related areas (threat intelligence, vulnerability detection) but hadn't directly tackled this CVSS classification task with the same breadth.
*   **Significance:** Automating CVSS vector generation is a significant problem in cybersecurity because it directly addresses the challenges of manual analysis being time-consuming, subjective, and prone to errors, especially as the number of vulnerabilities increases.  A reliable automated solution can significantly improve vulnerability prioritization and remediation efforts. The paper's findings regarding the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs versus embedding models are crucial for guiding future research and development in this area. The hybrid approach offers a promising avenue for combining the strengths of both methodologies.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a range of LLMs (both proprietary and open-source) and different prompt engineering techniques.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The comparison of LLMs with embedding-based models provides a solid benchmark and insights into the advantages and disadvantages of each approach.
    *   **Hybrid Approach:** The proposition of a hybrid strategy is a practical and potentially effective way to improve overall accuracy by leveraging the strengths of both LLMs and embeddings.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Making the code and implementation publicly available enhances the paper's reproducibility and encourages further research in this area.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Metrics:** The paper primarily focuses on accuracy as the evaluation metric.  A more comprehensive evaluation could include metrics like precision, recall, F1-score (especially for imbalanced classes), and inference time. Error analysis, showing *which* mistakes are made and *why*, would greatly improve the paper.
    *   **Generalization:** While the paper uses a recent set of CVEs for testing, a more rigorous assessment of generalization ability would involve testing on a completely separate dataset from a different time period or even from different sources (beyond just the official CVE repository).
    *   **Subjectivity in Subjectivity:** While the paper acknowledges subjectivity in CVSS scoring, it doesn't deeply explore *why* LLMs struggle with those specific subjective areas. Is it the inherent ambiguity, or a lack of specific training data that exposes those nuances?
    *   **Potential for Bias:**  The LLMs and embedding models might inherit biases present in their training data, leading to skewed or inaccurate CVSS scores for certain types of vulnerabilities or software. The paper lacks a discussion on mitigating or analyzing this potential bias.
    *   **No Error Analysis:** The paper lacks detailed error analysis for the models, which would help to gain insights into the strengths and weaknesses of each approach.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by:

    *   Motivating further research into using LLMs and hybrid approaches for automated CVSS scoring.
    *   Providing a baseline for evaluating new LLMs and techniques in this domain.
    *   Guiding the development of more accurate and reliable automated vulnerability assessment tools.
    *   Highlighting the importance of considering both objective and subjective aspects of CVSS scoring when developing automated solutions.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant investigation into the use of LLMs for automated CVSS vector generation. It provides a comprehensive comparison with embedding-based methods and proposes a promising hybrid approach. However, the evaluation is somewhat limited in scope (primarily focusing on accuracy), and a deeper exploration of the challenges associated with subjective CVSS components and potential bias is needed. While the reproducibility enhances its value, the lack of error analysis and in-depth discussion of biases detracts from the overall impact. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects the paper's important contributions while acknowledging the areas for improvement. The potential influence is substantial, but future work will need to address the limitations identified to fully realize the benefits of automated CVSS scoring.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### SpinMeRound: Consistent Multi-View Identity Generation Using Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10716v1)
- **Authors**: Stathis Galanakis, Alexandros Lattas, Stylianos Moschoglou, Bernhard Kainz, Stefanos Zafeiriou
- **Abstract**: Despite recent progress in diffusion models, generating realistic head portraits from novel viewpoints remains a significant challenge. Most current approaches are constrained to limited angular ranges, predominantly focusing on frontal or near-frontal views. Moreover, although the recent emerging large-scale diffusion models have been proven robust in handling 3D scenes, they underperform on facial data, given their complex structure and the uncanny valley pitfalls. In this paper, we propose SpinMeRound, a diffusion-based approach designed to generate consistent and accurate head portraits from novel viewpoints. By leveraging a number of input views alongside an identity embedding, our method effectively synthesizes diverse viewpoints of a subject whilst robustly maintaining its unique identity features. Through experimentation, we showcase our model's generation capabilities in 360 head synthesis, while beating current state-of-the-art multiview diffusion models.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper "SpinMeRound: Consistent multiview Identity Generation Using Diffusion Models" introduces a novel diffusion-based approach for generating consistent and accurate head portraits from novel viewpoints. The method addresses the challenge of creating realistic 3D head representations, particularly full head portraits, from limited or single input views.  It leverages identity embeddings and multiple input views (when available) to synthesize diverse viewpoints while preserving individual identity characteristics. The model is trained on synthetic data acquired using Panohead and is shown to outperform existing multiview diffusion models in generating high-fidelity, 360-degree head synthesis, with an emphasis on accurate 3D consistency including surface normals. A novel sampling strategy is presented for "in-the-wild" image reconstruction, aiming for complete head coverage, in order to generate fully consistent human head portraitures across all angles.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in several aspects, most of which are incremental.
1.  *Combining Diffusion with Identity Preservation:*  While the concept of using diffusion models for image generation isn't new, the specific application to generating consistent multiview head portraits with a strong emphasis on identity preservation is noteworthy, although this has been explored to varying degrees in prior work (as the paper itself acknowledges). The integration of ArcFace for identity embedding is a crucial aspect, but also not fundamentally new.
2.  *Full Head Generation*: The paper is positioned as an advancement over methods that primarily focus on frontal or near-frontal views. Achieving full 360-degree consistent head generation is a meaningful goal, which adds to the practical significance, but the methods for achieving it appear to be primarily adapted from existing techniques.
3.  *Specific Sampling Strategy:* The proposed three-step sampling strategy for "in-the-wild" images is a valuable contribution, especially addressing camera pose selection. However, it needs to be evaluated if this contribution, alone, significantly distinguishes the approach.
4.  *Surface Normal Generation:*  The simultaneous generation of surface normals alongside the images to enhance 3D consistency is important, and the paper makes a reasonable case that the addition improves consistency and facial feature accuracy. However, surface normal generation is not particularly a novel idea itself, either for reconstruction tasks or in conjunction with generative models.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from its potential to:

1.  *Improve 3D Head Modeling:* The method can advance 3D head modeling and reconstruction, which has applications in various fields, including computer graphics, animation, virtual reality, and teleconferencing.
2.  *Address Data Scarcity:*  The fact that the model is trained on synthetic data is significant because it mitigates the problem of limited availability of large-scale multiview head datasets. This is a practical advantage, however, the performance of synthetically trained data needs to be carefully considered.
3.  *Advance Generative Facial Models:* This furthers the advancement of generative models applied to facial data, which can have significant downstream effects, particularly in applications that require photo-realistic head generation.

**Weaknesses:**

1.  *Dependence on Panohead:* The model's reliance on synthetic data generated by Panohead is a potential limitation. Any biases or artifacts present in the Panohead data will likely be inherited by SpinMeRound. It has structural and qualitative limitations from the Panohead system.
2.  *Incremental Novelty:*  While the combination of techniques is useful, the individual components (diffusion models, identity embeddings, surface normal generation) are not fundamentally new.
3.  *Lack of Quantifiable Evaluation Against Alternatives:* Qualitative comparison with other multiview diffusion models is provided, but more rigorous quantitative comparisons on metrics like identity similarity, multi-view consistency, and normal accuracy would strengthen the paper.
4.  *Limited Focus on Practical Applications:* The paper primarily focuses on the technical aspects of the model. A discussion of potential applications and use cases would enhance its impact. While 3D consistency has been discussed, there is also lack of discussion of how this compares to other means, such as differentiable rendering, 3D priors, etc.
5. The comparison in novel view synthesis comparisons are incomplete and appear to show the proposed method producing reasonable results, albeit with slightly unnatural lighting, geometry, hair etc. Many alternative methods produce similar results, such that the differences are minimal. The impact of not integrating the identity embedding mechanisms also does not seem to justify its performance.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the above assessment, I assign a score of **6** to this paper.

**Rationale:**

*   The paper presents a practically useful system for generating consistent, full-head portraits from limited views using a diffusion model, but the overall novelty of the individual ideas is incremental. The proposed system combines existing components to improve upon the limitations of the other systems.

*   It addresses a significant challenge (consistent multiview head generation) and offers a reasonable, albeit slightly incremental, solution.

*   The synthetic training data mitigates a common problem in this field, but it also introduces a reliance on the quality and biases of the data generator.

*   More rigorous quantitative evaluations comparing the proposed method to existing alternatives, particularly concerning multi-view consistency and identity preservation, would strengthen the paper and potentially justify a higher score.

*   More exploration of real-world application is needed, along with a more comprehensive comparison to other forms of 3D consistency regularization.

Overall, the paper contributes to the field of 3D face modeling and generation, but the impact may be limited due to the incremental nature of the novelty.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### HELIOS: Adaptive Model And Early-Exit Selection for Efficient LLM Inference Serving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10724v1)
- **Authors**: Avinash Kumar, Shashank Nag, Jason Clemons, Lizy John, Poulami Das
- **Abstract**: Deploying large language models (LLMs) presents critical challenges due to the inherent trade-offs associated with key performance metrics, such as latency, accuracy, and throughput. Typically, gains in one metric is accompanied with degradation in others. Early-Exit LLMs (EE-LLMs) efficiently navigate this trade-off space by skipping some of the later model layers when it confidently finds an output token early, thus reducing latency without impacting accuracy. However, as the early exits taken depend on the task and are unknown apriori to request processing, EE-LLMs conservatively load the entire model, limiting resource savings and throughput. Also, current frameworks statically select a model for a user task, limiting our ability to adapt to changing nature of the input queries. We propose HELIOS to address these challenges. First, HELIOS shortlists a set of candidate LLMs, evaluates them using a subset of prompts, gathering telemetry data in real-time. Second, HELIOS uses the early exit data from these evaluations to greedily load the selected model only up to a limited number of layers. This approach yields memory savings which enables us to process more requests at the same time, thereby improving throughput. Third, HELIOS monitors and periodically reassesses the performance of the candidate LLMs and if needed, switches to another model that can service incoming queries more efficiently (such as using fewer layers without lowering accuracy). Our evaluations show that HELIOS achieves 1.48$\times$ throughput, 1.10$\times$ energy-efficiency, 1.39$\times$ lower response time, and 3.7$\times$ improvements in inference batch sizes compared to the baseline, when optimizing for the respective service level objectives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HELIOS: Adaptive Model And Early-Exit Selection for Efficient LLM Inference Serving":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HELIOS, a framework designed to optimize the serving of Early-Exit Large Language Models (EE-LLMs). HELIOS addresses limitations in current EE-LLM serving systems, which often statically select a model and load the entire model into memory, hindering resource savings and adaptability.  HELIOS dynamically selects the optimal model based on input-specific telemetry data, greedily loading only a subset of the most likely to be used layers. HELIOS monitors performance in real-time, adapting the model or loaded layers according to changing input query characteristics and user-specified service-level objectives (SLOs). HELIOS dynamically switches models and/or adjusts the number of layers based on feedback mechanisms, confidence thresholds, and a confidence breach counter (CBC) to balance accuracy and throughput.  The paper demonstrates the benefits of HELIOS through experiments showing improved throughput, energy efficiency, and reduced response time compared to static model selection baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a significant advancement in the realm of LLM inference by integrating dynamic model selection with adaptive early-exit strategies. While the concept of early exits is not entirely new, HELIOS contributes by making the selection of models, and especially the number of layers to load, *adaptive* to both the nature of the request and the overall performance objectives. The adaptive aspect is the key element. Existing early-exit solutions primarily focus on pre-defined exit points and fail to adapt based on the incoming request. The use of the Performance History Table (PHT) to track early-exit behavior during candidate model evaluation and the use of the Confidence Breach Counter (CBC) to dynamically switch models or load additional layers are novel contributions.  However, dynamic model selection has been addressed in previous work, but HELIOS integrates it with early exits in a specific way.

**Significance:** The paper tackles a crucial challenge: improving the efficiency of serving LLMs, a critical factor in their widespread deployment.  The results show substantial performance gains, especially in throughput and response time, making it a practically relevant solution.  The ability to adapt to changing input queries and user SLOs enhances the usability of LLMs in real-world applications, where workloads and performance requirements can vary significantly. The potential for memory savings by selectively loading layers is a crucial contribution, allowing for larger batch sizes and increased throughput.  The insights regarding the persistence of token predictions even when confidence thresholds aren't met initially are valuable observations that inform the design of a more efficient system.

**Strengths:**

*   **Adaptive Strategy:** HELIOS's dynamic selection of both the model and the number of layers based on real-time performance data is a significant strength.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The system's focus on optimizing for SLOs (throughput, latency, accuracy, energy) makes it directly applicable to real-world deployment scenarios.
*   **Clear Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation comparing HELIOS against reasonable baselines, demonstrating substantial improvements in key performance metrics.
*   **Insightful Observations:** The paper identifies and leverages key insights, such as the high probability of unchanged token predictions and the prompt locality.
*   **Comprehensive Design:** The paper provides a detailed description of HELIOS architecture and implementation, including model selection, evaluation methodologies and model management.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Diversity:** The evaluations focus on a limited set of models (OPT-1.3B and OPT-6.7B).  Testing HELIOS with a wider range of model sizes and architectures would strengthen the generality of the findings. The diversity could be improved by testing it with models such as Llama-3, Mistral and Gemini.
*   **Environment Assumption:** The assumption of a resource-constrained environment might not be representative of all deployment scenarios. Some evaluation of how performance and cost tradeoffs vary with plentiful resources is needed.
*   **Complexity:** The HELIOS system appears complex, requiring careful tuning of several parameters (RI, TH, CBCmax). While the paper discusses sensitivity analysis, further guidance on parameter selection for different workloads and hardware configurations would be helpful.
*   **Ground Truth Accuracy:** There's an acknowledgement of the challenge in comparing accuracy due to the lack of ground truths for evaluation prompts. Using reference-free metrics helps, but it's still an indirect measure of accuracy.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to efficient LLM inference serving. The adaptive strategy, practical relevance, and clear evaluation make it a valuable addition to the field. While the study has some limitations regarding model diversity and environment assumptions, the core ideas and demonstrated performance gains warrant a high score. The critical evaluation focuses on improving the practical deployment of the concepts.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Foundation Models for Remote Sensing: An Analysis of MLLMs for Object Localization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10727v1)
- **Authors**: Darryl Hannan, John Cooper, Dylan White, Timothy Doster, Henry Kvinge, Yijing Watkins
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have altered the landscape of computer vision, obtaining impressive results across a wide range of tasks, especially in zero-shot settings. Unfortunately, their strong performance does not always transfer to out-of-distribution domains, such as earth observation (EO) imagery. Prior work has demonstrated that MLLMs excel at some EO tasks, such as image captioning and scene understanding, while failing at tasks that require more fine-grained spatial reasoning, such as object localization. However, MLLMs are advancing rapidly and insights quickly become out-dated. In this work, we analyze more recent MLLMs that have been explicitly trained to include fine-grained spatial reasoning capabilities, benchmarking them on EO object localization tasks. We demonstrate that these models are performant in certain settings, making them well suited for zero-shot scenarios. Additionally, we provide a detailed discussion focused on prompt selection, ground sample distance (GSD) optimization, and analyzing failure cases. We hope that this work will prove valuable as others evaluate whether an MLLM is well suited for a given EO localization task and how to optimize it.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper benchmarks recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with spatial reasoning capabilities on Earth Observation (EO) object localization tasks. It assesses the zero-shot and few-shot performance of models like Molmo, Qwen 2.5-VL, and Llama 3.2 across different datasets (RarePlanes, AAP, xBD) involving tasks like plane, animal, and building detection. The study compares MLLM performance against a standard object detection architecture (Faster RCNN) and investigates the impact of factors like prompt selection, ground sample distance (GSD), and fine-tuning strategies on MLLM accuracy. It also analyzes common failure cases and offers insights on optimizing MLLMs for EO localization. The work aims to provide valuable guidance for researchers and practitioners seeking to leverage MLLMs for EO applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the application of MLLMs to EO data is not entirely new, the paper's novelty lies in its focus on *recent* MLLMs that explicitly incorporate localization capabilities. Previous studies had shown limitations of MLLMs in fine-grained spatial reasoning in EO; this work assesses whether newer architectures have overcome these limitations. The benchmarking of multiple state-of-the-art models and the inclusion of models like Llama 3.2 (which doesn't advertise localization) as a baseline also add novelty. The detailed analyses of prompt engineering, GSD optimization, and failure scenarios provide practical insights that are not commonly found in similar studies. The analysis of fine-tuning using DORA specifically in the EO context also adds to the paper's novel contributions.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing importance of EO data and the potential of MLLMs to automate or augment tasks like object detection and monitoring.  The paper provides a valuable benchmark for assessing the readiness of these models for EO applications. The identification of key factors influencing performance (prompt selection, GSD, fine-tuning) helps to guide future research and development efforts. The discussion of failure cases provides insights for improving model robustness and reliability. The few-shot learning experiments give a good indication for the amount of data needed to surpass certain baselines. Overall, the paper contributes to a better understanding of the capabilities and limitations of MLLMs in the EO domain, paving the way for their wider adoption in practical applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive benchmark with multiple MLLMs and datasets.
    *   Detailed analysis of factors impacting performance (prompting, GSD, fine-tuning).
    *   Practical insights for optimizing MLLMs for EO tasks.
    *   Clear presentation of results with visualizations and failure case analysis.
    *   Rigorous comparison against a traditional object detection model (Faster RCNN).
    *   Addresses a relevant problem in a growing field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The fine-tuning experiments, while valuable, could be expanded with a more thorough exploration of different PEFT techniques and hyperparameters.
    *   The datasets used, while representative, are still limited in scope. A more diverse set of EO datasets would further strengthen the conclusions.
    *   The metric used only detects centre points, while a bounding box approach would enable a more granular evaluation (especially in multiclass problems).
    *   The performance of the models are far from perfect, suggesting a lot of room for improvement.
    *   The analysis is limited to RGB imagery; future work should consider the applicability of MLLMs to other modalities (e.g., SAR, multispectral).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can potentially influence the field by:
    *   Guiding researchers in the selection and optimization of MLLMs for EO tasks.
    *   Providing a benchmark for evaluating new MLLM architectures and training strategies.
    *   Inspiring further research on improving the spatial reasoning capabilities of MLLMs in EO.
    *   Accelerating the adoption of MLLMs in practical EO applications such as environmental monitoring, disaster response, and urban planning.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper offers a valuable and timely contribution to the field by rigorously evaluating recent MLLMs on EO object localization tasks. It provides useful insights into the factors influencing model performance and identifies areas for future research. While the fine-tuning experiments and dataset coverage could be expanded, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a significant contribution that has the potential to influence the development and application of MLLMs in the EO domain. A higher score (8-9) would be justified if there were more novel techniques proposed for overcoming the limitations of MLLMs in EO (beyond parameter optimization), and/or if a more comprehensive exploration of fine-tuning techniques was undertaken.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Frozen Layers: Memory-efficient Many-fidelity Hyperparameter Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10735v1)
- **Authors**: Timur Carstensen, Neeratyoy Mallik, Frank Hutter, Martin Rapp
- **Abstract**: As model sizes grow, finding efficient and cost-effective hyperparameter optimization (HPO) methods becomes increasingly crucial for deep learning pipelines. While multi-fidelity HPO (MF-HPO) trades off computational resources required for DL training with lower fidelity estimations, existing fidelity sources often fail under lower compute and memory constraints. We propose a novel fidelity source: the number of layers that are trained or frozen during training. For deep networks, this approach offers significant compute and memory savings while preserving rank correlations between hyperparameters at low fidelities compared to full model training. We demonstrate this in our empirical evaluation across ResNets and Transformers and additionally analyze the utility of frozen layers as a fidelity in using GPU resources as a fidelity in HPO, and for a combined MF-HPO with other fidelity sources. This contribution opens new applications for MF-HPO with hardware resources as a fidelity and creates opportunities for improved algorithms navigating joint fidelity spaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel fidelity source for hyperparameter optimization (HPO) in deep learning: freezing layers during training. By only training a subset of layers and keeping the rest at their initial random state, the method significantly reduces GPU memory and computational costs.  The authors demonstrate that training with a partially frozen network can still preserve the rank correlation between hyperparameter configurations and full-model performance. This allows for efficient exploration of the hyperparameter space, especially under memory constraints. They also show how this "layer freezing" fidelity source can be combined with other fidelity sources like training duration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of freezing layers as a fidelity source for HPO is a novel contribution. It addresses a key limitation of existing multi-fidelity HPO methods: the inability to reduce memory consumption substantially. Traditional fidelity sources like dataset size or training epochs primarily affect computational cost but have a limited impact on memory footprint. By contrast, freezing layers directly reduces the memory required, opening the door to tuning larger models on hardware with limited memory. The paper is also the first to modify gradient computation and weight update steps within a deep learning (DL) model as a multi-fidelity source.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the practical implications for large-scale deep learning. As models continue to grow, the memory demands of HPO become a bottleneck. This method offers a practical way to overcome this limitation, allowing for more efficient HPO on existing hardware and potentially enabling HPO in scenarios where it was previously infeasible. The demonstrated preservation of rank correlation at lower fidelities makes this approach valuable for guiding the HPO process. Moreover, the method enables efficient tuning of models on memory-constrained hardware and paves the way for HPO algorithms designed to navigate the joint fidelity space of frozen layers and other sources.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:**  The approach is easy to implement and integrate with existing HPO frameworks.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on ResNets and Transformers.
    *   **Memory Savings:** Demonstrates significant memory reduction.
    *   **Rank Correlation Preservation:**  Shows empirically that freezing layers maintains rank correlation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Layer Discretization Strategy:** The paper acknowledges the architecture-dependent nature of the optimal layer splitting strategy. The current heuristics might not be optimal for every model architecture.
    *   **Continuation Mechanism:**  The lack of a smooth continuation mechanism (like warm starting), prevents the use of freeze-thaw methods which could be a potential limitation and should be addressed in future work.
    *   **Limited hyperparameter sweeps:** The experiments are conducted for two architetural families - ResNets and Transformers. A broader evaluation with a wide variety of architectures and HPO algorithms would further strengthen the work.
    *   **Focus on rank correlation:** While rank correlation is essential, the paper could benefit from including a better analysis of the *quality* of final solutions found in the HPO and how do these compare with the full-fidelity optimal hyperparameter configurations, particularly when dealing with low fidelity runs.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the design of future HPO algorithms and workflows, particularly in resource-constrained settings. It may also prompt further research into combining different fidelity sources more effectively. It democratizes access to DL tuning as the constraints on memory are reduced.

**Justification of Score:**

This paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to HPO in deep learning. The memory savings and the preservation of rank correlation are valuable contributions. While some limitations remain (layer discretization, lack of continuation, and the scope of hyperparameter sweeps), the potential impact on the field is significant. The work offers a concrete solution to a pressing problem in large-scale DL.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### How Instruction and Reasoning Data shape Post-Training: Data Quality through the Lens of Layer-wise Gradients
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10766v1)
- **Authors**: Ming Li, Yanhong Li, Ziyue Li, Tianyi Zhou
- **Abstract**: As the post-training of large language models (LLMs) advances from instruction-following to complex reasoning tasks, understanding how different data affect finetuning dynamics remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we present a spectral analysis of layer-wise gradients induced by low/high-quality instruction and reasoning data for LLM post-training. Our analysis reveals that widely-studied metrics for data evaluation, e.g., IFD, InsTag, Difficulty, and Reward, can be explained and unified by spectral properties computed from gradients' singular value decomposition (SVD). Specifically, higher-quality data are usually associated with lower nuclear norms and higher effective ranks. Notably, effective rank exhibits better robustness and resolution than nuclear norm in capturing subtle quality differences. For example, reasoning data achieves substantially higher effective ranks than instruction data, implying richer gradient structures on more complex tasks. Our experiments also highlight that models within the same family share similar gradient patterns regardless of their sizes, whereas different model families diverge significantly. Providing a unified view on the effects of data quality across instruction and reasoning data, this work illuminates the interplay between data quality and training stability, shedding novel insights into developing better data exploration strategies for post-training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how instruction and reasoning data shape the post-training dynamics of large language models (LLMs).  It uses spectral analysis of layer-wise gradients to understand how different data qualities (low/high) impact LLM finetuning. By analyzing the Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) of gradients, the authors connect established data evaluation metrics (IFD, InsTag, Difficulty, Reward) to spectral properties. The key findings are that higher-quality data tend to exhibit lower nuclear norms and higher effective ranks in the layer-wise gradients.  The paper also explores differences between instruction data and reasoning data, model sizes, and model families in terms of gradient behavior, providing a more comprehensive understanding of the data quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a fresh perspective on data quality assessment in LLM training by linking it to the spectral properties of gradients. Analyzing layer-wise gradients using SVD-based metrics to unify data quality metrics for instruction and reasoning tasks is a valuable contribution, providing a mechanistic understanding that goes beyond simply measuring end-task performance. The comparison between instruction and reasoning data, and across different model families, further expands the scope of the analysis. While the core idea of using gradients isn't entirely new (Li et al., 2024c), the specific application to unifying quality metrics and the breadth of the experimental setup are novel.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for developing better data exploration strategies for post-training LLMs. Understanding how different data qualities affect training stability and efficiency could lead to more effective data synthesis and selection methods, improving model performance and resource utilization. The observation that effective rank is more robust and provides better resolution than nuclear norm in capturing quality differences is valuable. The discovery of gradient pattern similarities within the same model family (despite varying sizes) could inform transfer learning approaches.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous empirical analysis across diverse LLM families (Qwen2.5, Llama3, Gemma) and model sizes.
    *   Unification of several existing data quality metrics through the lens of gradient spectral properties.
    *   Comparison of instruction-following and reasoning data, leading to novel insights into reasoning capability.
    *   Development of new metrics (Nuclear Norm, Effective Rank, Same-layer Similarity, Adjacent-layer Similarity) for characterizing gradient behavior.
    *   Comprehensive experimental design with multiple datasets and automated evaluation metrics.
    *   The findings provide valuable insights into the interplay between data quality, training stability, and the development of better data exploration strategies for post-training.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The similarity-based metrics (Same-layer Similarity, Adjacent-layer Similarity) were not effective in reflecting data quality, suggesting they might be capturing something else (or be too sensitive to the specific architectures). This limits the scope and comprehensiveness of the analysis.
    *   The interpretation of the direct connection between the spectra of gradients and data quality/model behavior could benefit from further theoretical grounding.  While the paper establishes empirical correlations, it doesn't fully explain *why* these relationships exist.
    *   The study primarily focuses on supervised fine-tuning.  It would be interesting to see how these gradient dynamics extend to other training paradigms like Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) or Direct Preference Optimization (DPO).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore gradient-based analysis for understanding and improving LLM training.  The unified view on data quality metrics and insights into reasoning data effects are particularly valuable. It could potentially lead to better data selection strategies, optimized data synthesis, and more efficient fine-tuning techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has solid novelty and substantial significance. The findings provide a valuable mechanistic understanding of how data quality affects LLM finetuning. While there is room for improvement with respect to explaining *why* these gradient patterns emerge and why some metrics failed to be effective, the breadth and depth of the empirical analysis, coupled with the unified view on data quality, warrant a high score. The potential impact on data selection strategies and fine-tuning techniques in the field is significant.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Art of Audience Engagement: LLM-Based Thin-Slicing of Scientific Talks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10768v1)
- **Authors**: Ralf Schmälzle, Sue Lim, Yuetong Du, Gary Bente
- **Abstract**: This paper examines the thin-slicing approach - the ability to make accurate judgments based on minimal information - in the context of scientific presentations. Drawing on research from nonverbal communication and personality psychology, we show that brief excerpts (thin slices) reliably predict overall presentation quality. Using a novel corpus of over one hundred real-life science talks, we employ Large Language Models (LLMs) to evaluate transcripts of full presentations and their thin slices. By correlating LLM-based evaluations of short excerpts with full-talk assessments, we determine how much information is needed for accurate predictions. Our results demonstrate that LLM-based evaluations align closely with human ratings, proving their validity, reliability, and efficiency. Critically, even very short excerpts (less than 10 percent of a talk) strongly predict overall evaluations. This suggests that the first moments of a presentation convey relevant information that is used in quality evaluations and can shape lasting impressions. The findings are robust across different LLMs and prompting strategies. This work extends thin-slicing research to public speaking and connects theories of impression formation to LLMs and current research on AI communication. We discuss implications for communication and social cognition research on message reception. Lastly, we suggest an LLM-based thin-slicing framework as a scalable feedback tool to enhance human communication.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the concept of "thin-slicing," the ability to make accurate judgments based on minimal information, in the context of science presentations. The authors leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) to evaluate the quality of speech transcripts and their corresponding "thin slices" (short excerpts). They correlate the LLM-based evaluations of these slices with evaluations of the full talks to determine the amount of information needed for accurate prediction of presentation quality. Their results show that even very short excerpts (less than 10% of the talk) can strongly predict overall presentation evaluations. This suggests that the initial moments of a presentation are crucial and convey relevant information that shapes lasting impressions. The findings are validated with human rater comparisons and are shown to be robust across different LLMs and prompting strategies. The paper proposes an LLM-based thin-slicing framework as a scalable feedback tool to improve human communication.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper demonstrates several novel aspects:

1.  **Application of LLMs to Thin-Slicing of Scientific Presentations:** While thin-slicing has been studied in various domains, applying it to the evaluation of scientific presentations is a less explored area.  Using LLMs to automate this process and scale the evaluation of speech quality is also innovative.

2.  **Emphasis on Science Communication:**  The focus on scientific talks is crucial, as effective science communication is essential for public understanding and knowledge dissemination.  Most prior thin-slicing research related to public speaking focused on general presentations.

3.  **Validation with Human Raters:** The paper includes a validation step by comparing LLM evaluations with human ratings. This increases the credibility of the results and confirms that LLMs capture human-perceived aspects of speech quality.

4.  **Framework for Feedback and Augmentation:** The paper proposes a practical LLM-based framework for providing feedback to speakers and augmenting communication training. This makes the research actionable.

However, there are some weaknesses to consider:

*   **Reliance on Transcripts:** The analysis is based on text transcripts, which ignores non-verbal cues (tone of voice, body language, visual aids) that contribute significantly to presentation quality. While they acknowledge this limitation, its impact should not be underestimated.
*   **Generalizability to other Presentation Contexts:** The study primarily examines "science talks" in a conference-like setting. The results might not be generalizable to more persuasive presentations or informal speaking scenarios.
*   **Limited Exploration of "Why":** While the paper demonstrates that thin slices *can* predict overall quality, it delves less into *why* certain aspects of the initial moments are so predictive. Identifying the specific linguistic or rhetorical features within the thin slices that drive the predictions could provide deeper insights.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable and novel approach by utilizing LLMs to assess the quality of scientific presentations through the lens of thin-slicing. The results are compelling, revealing that even short excerpts of a presentation can be indicative of its overall quality. Furthermore, the validation with human raters and demonstration of robust findings across different LLMs enhance the reliability and generalizability of the conclusions. The suggested framework for feedback and training holds practical implications for improving communication skills.

However, the reliance on transcripts limits the scope by neglecting non-verbal cues, and the potential for generalizability is somewhat constrained by the focus on scientific presentations and academics. Additionally, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth exploration of the underlying factors that contribute to the predictive power of the thin slices.

Overall, the paper represents a significant advancement in applying LLMs to public speaking research and offers a promising avenue for enhancing science communication.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Deep Audio Watermarks are Shallow: Limitations of Post-Hoc Watermarking Techniques for Speech
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10782v1)
- **Authors**: Patrick O'Reilly, Zeyu Jin, Jiaqi Su, Bryan Pardo
- **Abstract**: In the audio modality, state-of-the-art watermarking methods leverage deep neural networks to allow the embedding of human-imperceptible signatures in generated audio. The ideal is to embed signatures that can be detected with high accuracy when the watermarked audio is altered via compression, filtering, or other transformations. Existing audio watermarking techniques operate in a post-hoc manner, manipulating "low-level" features of audio recordings after generation (e.g. through the addition of a low-magnitude watermark signal). We show that this post-hoc formulation makes existing audio watermarks vulnerable to transformation-based removal attacks. Focusing on speech audio, we (1) unify and extend existing evaluations of the effect of audio transformations on watermark detectability, and (2) demonstrate that state-of-the-art post-hoc audio watermarks can be removed with no knowledge of the watermarking scheme and minimal degradation in audio quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Deep Audio Watermarks Are Shallow: Limitations of Post-Hoc Watermarking Techniques for Speech" investigates the robustness of existing state-of-the-art (SOTA) audio watermarking methods for speech audio. These methods generally operate in a post-hoc manner, embedding signatures as low-magnitude perturbations after audio generation. The authors demonstrate that these watermarks are vulnerable to transformation-based removal attacks, specifically through the use of neural network-based low-bitrate audio codecs and denoisers.  The study extends previous evaluations by considering a wider range of transformations, particularly those based on neural networks, and highlights that these transformations can effectively remove watermarks with minimal perceived degradation in audio quality, even without knowledge of the watermarking scheme. The authors argue for the development of more robust audio watermarking techniques that are less susceptible to such attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic evaluation of a broad range of neural network-based transformations, including codecs, vocoders, and denoisers, as potential watermark removal techniques. While previous work has explored the robustness of audio watermarks against some transformations, this paper provides a more comprehensive analysis and demonstrates the vulnerability of current SOTA post-hoc watermarking methods to more sophisticated, readily available tools. The emphasis on speech-specific codecs and denoisers further adds to its novelty. The band-splitting technique for working with different sampling rates is a small but useful contribution.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the field of audio watermarking, especially in the context of generative AI. The paper convincingly shows that existing post-hoc techniques are not sufficiently robust to protect synthetic speech from unauthorized use or modification. This underscores the need for more resilient watermarking approaches that are integrated directly into the audio generation process or are less susceptible to signal processing and neural network-based attacks. The identification of low-bitrate neural codecs as a particularly effective removal technique is a crucial observation. The paper clearly establishes the limitations of a class of current methods and provides strong motivation for new research directions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper examines a wide variety of audio transformations, going beyond simple signal processing techniques to include advanced neural network-based methods.

    *   **Clear Demonstration of Vulnerability:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the vulnerability of post-hoc watermarking techniques to removal attacks.

    *   **Relevant Problem:** The problem of protecting synthetic speech is highly relevant, given the increasing capabilities of generative AI models.

    *   **Well-Written and Clear:** The paper is well-structured and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Watermarking Techniques:** While the paper evaluates multiple watermarking techniques, a deeper investigation and detailed comparison into the intricacies of each method’s underlying algorithm would improve the paper.

    *   **Limited Mitigation Strategies:** The paper focuses primarily on identifying vulnerabilities and doesn't offer specific solutions or new designs for more robust watermarking techniques. However, the paper's conclusions do a great job of directing next steps for future works.

    *   **Modest Impact Beyond Identification of a Problem:** While it identifies a serious problem, its impact is primarily diagnostic. It doesn't propose and validate a novel, robust solution.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in audio watermarking by shifting the focus toward more robust techniques that are less susceptible to neural network-based removal attacks. It highlights the need for methods that are integrated into the audio generation process or that leverage different embedding strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7. While the paper lacks a novel solution, its comprehensive evaluation and clear demonstration of the vulnerability of existing SOTA audio watermarking methods are significant contributions. The work is well-motivated, technically sound, and has the potential to influence future research in the field by highlighting a critical gap in current watermarking techniques. The emphasis on neural network-based transformations and their effectiveness in removing watermarks is particularly valuable and addresses a timely and relevant challenge. However, a larger influence might be seen with more variety of watermark methods and perhaps the inclusion of a few mitigating ideas for robust solutions.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Sword of Damocles in ViTs: Computational Redundancy Amplifies Adversarial Transferability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10804v1)
- **Authors**: Jiani Liu, Zhiyuan Wang, Zeliang Zhang, Chao Huang, Susan Liang, Yunlong Tang, Chenliang Xu
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) have demonstrated impressive performance across a range of applications, including many safety-critical tasks. However, their unique architectural properties raise new challenges and opportunities in adversarial robustness. In particular, we observe that adversarial examples crafted on ViTs exhibit higher transferability compared to those crafted on CNNs, suggesting that ViTs contain structural characteristics favorable for transferable attacks. In this work, we investigate the role of computational redundancy in ViTs and its impact on adversarial transferability. Unlike prior studies that aim to reduce computation for efficiency, we propose to exploit this redundancy to improve the quality and transferability of adversarial examples. Through a detailed analysis, we identify two forms of redundancy, including the data-level and model-level, that can be harnessed to amplify attack effectiveness. Building on this insight, we design a suite of techniques, including attention sparsity manipulation, attention head permutation, clean token regularization, ghost MoE diversification, and test-time adversarial training. Extensive experiments on the ImageNet-1k dataset validate the effectiveness of our approach, showing that our methods significantly outperform existing baselines in both transferability and generality across diverse model architectures.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the role of computational redundancy in Vision Transformers (ViTs) and its impact on adversarial transferability. It observes that adversarial examples crafted on ViTs exhibit higher transferability than those crafted on CNNs.  The authors propose exploiting this redundancy (both data-level and model-level) to improve the quality and transferability of adversarial examples. They introduce techniques like attention sparsity manipulation, attention head permutation, clean token regularization, ghost Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) diversification, and test-time adversarial training. Experiments on ImageNet-1k demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach, showing significant outperformance over existing baselines in transferability and generality across diverse model architectures. The paper also experiments with an online learning strategy to diversify the transformer representation at each block.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its *explicit* focus on exploiting computational redundancy in ViTs to *enhance* adversarial transferability.  While previous work has looked at reducing computation in ViTs for efficiency or improving white-box attacks, this paper cleverly turns the redundancy inherent in ViTs into an advantage for black-box attack settings. Several of the individual techniques (e.g., exploiting attention sparsity, using clean tokens) have precedents in related areas, but the specific combination and adaptation for ViTs, targeting *transferability*, is relatively novel. The proposed online learning strategy for transformer blocks diversification at a granular level further augments the novelty. The introduction of "ghost MoE" for diversifying the FFN is an interesting adaptation of MoE concepts.

*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications. The enhanced transferability demonstrated by the proposed methods means that ViTs might be more vulnerable to black-box attacks than previously thought. This underscores the need for developing more robust defenses specifically tailored to ViTs. By providing insights into the link between computational redundancy and transferability, the paper offers a new perspective for future research in adversarial robustness. Demonstrating improved transferability is always significant, as it highlights vulnerabilities in real-world scenarios where the attacker has limited access.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies and frames the issue of exploiting computational redundancy for adversarial transferability in ViTs.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The analysis of data-level and model-level redundancy is well-reasoned.
    *   **Multiple techniques:** The paper presents a suite of techniques, offering a multifaceted approach to enhancing transferability.
    *   **Extensive experiments:**  The experiments are thorough, covering a variety of models and comparing against relevant baselines on a standard dataset (ImageNet-1k). The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    *   **Clear writing:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental advances:** While the overall approach is novel, some of the individual techniques build upon existing ideas. The innovation lies more in their application and combination within the ViT context.
    *   **Computational cost of online strategy:** While test-time adversarial training is explored to reduce cost, the online strategy for diversifiying the transformer blocks incurs additional computation that could be limiting.
    *   **Limited defensive perspective:**  The paper focuses primarily on attacks. While demonstrating the weaknesses is valuable, a deeper discussion of potential defenses informed by these findings would strengthen the work.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Although extensive experiments are performed, more thorough ablation studies to dissect the contribution of each technique component would improve clarity and provide greater insights.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in adversarial robustness for ViTs.  It highlights the importance of considering computational redundancy when designing attacks and defenses. The proposed techniques could serve as building blocks for future attacks and motivate the development of novel defense mechanisms specifically targeting the exploited redundancies.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the relatively incremental nature of some of the individual techniques, but the well-executed and novel combination of ideas to leverage ViT's redundancy for enhanced transferability, the strong experimental validation, and the potential impact on future research in ViT robustness, a score of 7 is justified. The paper presents a useful contribution, successfully demonstrates its effectiveness, and provides valuable insights for further research. However, the reliance on and adaptation of existing concepts, along with the limited discussion of defenses and high computation costs, prevents a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tabular foundation model to detect empathy from visual cues
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10808v1)
- **Authors**: Md Rakibul Hasan, Shafin Rahman, Md Zakir Hossain, Aneesh Krishna, Tom Gedeon
- **Abstract**: Detecting empathy from video interactions is an emerging area of research. Video datasets, however, are often released as extracted features (i.e., tabular data) rather than raw footage due to privacy and ethical concerns. Prior research on such tabular datasets established tree-based classical machine learning approaches as the best-performing models. Motivated by the recent success of textual foundation models (i.e., large language models), we explore the use of tabular foundation models in empathy detection from tabular visual features. We experiment with two recent tabular foundation models $-$ TabPFN v2 and TabICL $-$ through in-context learning and fine-tuning setups. Our experiments on a public human-robot interaction benchmark demonstrate a significant boost in cross-subject empathy detection accuracy over several strong baselines (accuracy: $0.590 \rightarrow 0.730$; AUC: $0.564 \rightarrow 0.669$). In addition to performance improvement, we contribute novel insights and an evaluation setup to ensure generalisation on unseen subjects in this public benchmark. As the practice of releasing video features as tabular datasets is likely to persist due to privacy constraints, our findings will be widely applicable to future empathy detection video datasets as well.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of tabular foundation models (TabPFN v2 and TabICL) for empathy detection from visual cues extracted from videos of human-robot interactions.  The authors address the limitation that most video empathy datasets are released as extracted features (tabular data) due to privacy concerns.  They experiment with in-context learning and fine-tuning approaches on a publicly available human-robot interaction benchmark. The results demonstrate a significant improvement in cross-subject empathy detection accuracy compared to traditional tree-based methods.  The authors also contribute a refined evaluation setup to ensure generalisation on unseen subjects, addressing a potential flaw in previous evaluation protocols.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

1.  **Application of Tabular Foundation Models to Empathy Detection:**  While tabular foundation models have gained traction in other domains, their application to empathy detection using visual cues is relatively novel. It addresses a specific niche where privacy concerns limit the use of raw video data, forcing reliance on tabular features.

2.  **Cross-Subject Generalisation Emphasis:** The explicit focus on improving and evaluating cross-subject generalisation is a significant contribution.  The recognition that prior work suffered from data leakage (same subjects in training and test sets) and the design of a leave-one-subject-out cross-validation protocol strengthens the paper's claims of true generalisation.

3.  **In-Context Learning vs. Fine-Tuning Comparison:** The paper provides a comparative analysis of in-context learning and fine-tuning using tabular foundation models, offering insights into the trade-offs between these two approaches for empathy detection.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from:

1.  **Addressing a Practical Problem:** The approach is relevant because it tackles the common scenario where privacy constraints necessitate working with tabular features rather than raw video.  The findings are immediately applicable to existing and future empathy detection datasets released in this format.

2.  **Performance Improvement:**  The demonstrated improvement in empathy detection accuracy over strong baselines (particularly in the cross-subject generalisation setting) suggests that tabular foundation models can be a valuable tool in this domain.

3.  **Benchmark Refinement:**  The identification and correction of a potential flaw in the existing evaluation protocol for the HRI dataset enhances the reliability and reproducibility of future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   Clearly articulated problem statement and motivation.
*   Rigorous experimental design with well-defined evaluation metrics.
*   Detailed comparison of different approaches (ICL vs. fine-tuning).
*   Careful attention to data leakage and generalisation issues.
*   Reproducible implementation with provided code.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Limitation:** The experiments are conducted on a single HRI dataset. While the results are promising, it is important to validate the findings on other empathy detection datasets with different characteristics and modalities. The generalisability of the findings may not be as wide as the paper suggests.
*   **Limited Hyperparameter Tuning for ICL:** The ICL experiments are performed using default configurations for TabICL and TabPFN, which might not be optimal for the specific task and dataset. Hyperparameter optimisation for ICL could potentially lead to better performance and provide a more complete comparison.
*   **Lack of Deeper Analysis of Learned Representations:** The paper focuses on performance metrics but lacks a deeper analysis of the learned representations by tabular foundation models. Understanding how these models capture relevant information for empathy detection would provide valuable insights.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence future research in empathy detection by:

*   Promoting the use of tabular foundation models for scenarios where raw video data is unavailable.
*   Highlighting the importance of cross-subject generalisation and the need for rigorous evaluation protocols.
*   Providing a benchmark for future studies using tabular foundation models in empathy detection.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates solid novelty in applying tabular foundation models to empathy detection and addresses an important practical constraint regarding data privacy. The emphasis on cross-subject generalisation is a crucial contribution. However, the reliance on a single dataset, the limited hyperparameter tuning for ICL, and the lack of in-depth analysis of learned representations slightly limit the overall impact. The work provides a valuable contribution to the field, but further validation and exploration are needed to fully establish the potential of tabular foundation models for empathy detection in a broader range of scenarios. The score of 7.5 reflects the significant contribution, tempered by the limitations discussed.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### GaSLight: Gaussian Splats for Spatially-Varying Lighting in HDR
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10809v1)
- **Authors**: Christophe Bolduc, Yannick Hold-Geoffroy, Zhixin Shu, Jean-François Lalonde
- **Abstract**: We present GaSLight, a method that generates spatially-varying lighting from regular images. Our method proposes using HDR Gaussian Splats as light source representation, marking the first time regular images can serve as light sources in a 3D renderer. Our two-stage process first enhances the dynamic range of images plausibly and accurately by leveraging the priors embedded in diffusion models. Next, we employ Gaussian Splats to model 3D lighting, achieving spatially variant lighting. Our approach yields state-of-the-art results on HDR estimations and their applications in illuminating virtual objects and scenes.
