The paper investigates the alignment between Large Language Model (LLM) decisions and human moral judgments in the context of the "Moral Machine" experiment.  It focuses on how LLM decisions vary when prompted with different personas representing various sociodemographic factors (age, gender, culture, religion, political orientation, income, and education).  The authors find that LLM moral decisions change substantially based on the assigned persona, sometimes more dramatically than human decisions would.  Notably, they identify a "partisan sorting" phenomenon, where political personas particularly influence LLM decisions. The paper concludes by discussing ethical implications and risks associated with deploying LLMs in moral decision-making applications, emphasizing the potential for bias amplification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of examining LLM alignment in the moral machine experiment isn't entirely new.  Prior work (cited in the paper) has looked at LLM performance on this task.  However, the **focus on persona-dependent behavior** and the granular examination of various sociodemographic factors is a clear step forward. Specifically, the identification of *partisan sorting* is a novel and interesting finding.  Previous studies often considered LLMs as a monolith, whereas this one dissects the influence of the LLM’s assigned identity.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the increasing deployment of LLMs in real-world applications, including those with ethical implications (autonomous vehicles, etc.).  Understanding how these models make decisions and whether they reflect human values is crucial. Demonstrating that LLMs are susceptible to significant shifts in moral judgments based on superficial persona assignments raises serious concerns.  The observed partisan sorting effect is particularly worrying, as it suggests a potential for biased decision-making aligned with political ideologies, potentially reinforcing existing societal biases. This directly addresses a critical question regarding fairness and safety of the models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The paper clearly outlines its methodology, including the persona creation process, the selection of scenarios, and the metrics used for evaluation (AMCE, MDD). This improves replicability.
    *   **Rigorous experimentation:** The authors utilize a reasonable number of scenarios (10,000) and evaluate multiple LLMs (GPT-4, GPT-3.5, Llama2).
    *   **Interesting findings:** The identification of the partisan sorting phenomenon and the demonstration of greater decision variability in LLMs compared to humans are compelling findings.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and uses visualizations effectively (radar plots, bar charts) to present the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplified Personas:** The use of binary personas (e.g., "male" vs. "female," "conservative" vs. "progressive") may oversimplify the complexity of human sociodemographics.  Intersectional identities are not explicitly addressed.  While acknowledged by the authors as a limitation, the impact of this simplification needs to be considered carefully.
    *   **Moral Machine limitations:**  The moral machine experiment is itself a simplification of real-world ethical dilemmas.  It forces binary choices and doesn't account for the nuances of context, intent, and consequence.  Extrapolating these results to complex real-world situations should be done with caution. The choice of scenarios and emphasis on traffic accidents might skew results.
    *   **Model selection:** The justification for selecting three top-performing models could be clearer. While these choices provide a good starting point, it's important to contextualize whether the partisan sorting effects observed are model-dependent.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   Encouraging more research on persona-dependent behavior in LLMs, considering a wider range of sociodemographic factors and intersectional identities.
    *   Motivating the development of techniques to mitigate bias and ensure fairness in LLM decision-making, particularly in ethically sensitive applications.
    *   Raising awareness among policymakers and developers about the potential risks of deploying LLMs without careful consideration of their susceptibility to bias.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field by rigorously examining persona-dependent behavior in LLMs within the moral machine framework, specifically highlighting the *partisan sorting* phenomena. While the choice of the LLMs and reliance on binary, simplified personas do introduce limitations, the paper offers valuable insights regarding vulnerabilities for deploying these models in ethically sensitive applications. Future work building upon this research will need to address these limitations to provide more conclusive evidence regarding the real-world implications of the findings. The observed partisan sorting effect deserves significant consideration, warranting this score.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### ARise: Towards Knowledge-Augmented Reasoning via Risk-Adaptive Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10893v1)
- **Authors**: Yize Zhang, Tianshu Wang, Sirui Chen, Kun Wang, Xingyu Zeng, Hongyu Lin, Xianpei Han, Le Sun, Chaochao Lu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities and are receiving increasing attention to enhance their reasoning through scaling test--time compute. However, their application in open--ended, knowledge--intensive, complex reasoning scenarios is still limited. Reasoning--oriented methods struggle to generalize to open--ended scenarios due to implicit assumptions of complete world knowledge. Meanwhile, knowledge--augmented reasoning (KAR) methods fail to address two core challenges: 1) error propagation, where errors in early steps cascade through the chain, and 2) verification bottleneck, where the explore--exploit tradeoff arises in multi--branch decision processes. To overcome these limitations, we introduce ARise, a novel framework that integrates risk assessment of intermediate reasoning states with dynamic retrieval--augmented generation (RAG) within a Monte Carlo tree search paradigm. This approach enables effective construction and optimization of reasoning plans across multiple maintained hypothesis branches. Experimental results show that ARise significantly outperforms the state--of--the--art KAR methods by up to 23.10%, and the latest RAG-equipped large reasoning models by up to 25.37%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ARISE: Towards Knowledge-Augmented Reasoning via Risk-Adaptive Search":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ARISE, a novel framework designed to enhance knowledge-augmented reasoning (KAR) in large language models (LLMs). ARISE addresses two key challenges that often plague KAR methods: error propagation and verification bottleneck. It achieves this by integrating risk assessment of intermediate reasoning states with dynamic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) within a Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) paradigm. ARISE iteratively refines reasoning steps through decomposition, retrieval-then-reasoning, providing fine-grained knowledge for LLMs.  Risk assessment dynamically balances exploration and exploitation to guide the search toward reliable and novel reasoning directions. The experimental results demonstrate that ARISE outperforms state-of-the-art KAR methods and RAG-equipped large reasoning models across several multi-hop question answering benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several existing techniques (RAG, MCTS, risk assessment) into a cohesive framework specifically tailored for addressing the shortcomings of KAR in LLMs. While each component is not entirely novel, the way they are integrated and the application to the KAR problem presents a unique approach. The idea of using risk assessment to guide the MCTS search is particularly interesting. However, it's not clear how different this is from other reward shaping approaches in tree search.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a crucial limitation of LLMs in complex reasoning tasks. By mitigating error propagation and effectively balancing exploration and exploitation, ARISE demonstrates a tangible improvement in accuracy and efficiency in knowledge-intensive scenarios. This is important for deploying LLMs in real-world applications like science, finance, and healthcare.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the problem and the proposed solution.
    *   The integration of existing techniques is well-motivated.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, comparing ARISE to a wide range of baselines and using multiple datasets and LLMs.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of ARISE.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the concept of risk assessment is compelling, its implementation relies on computing average log-likelihoods from the policy model, which is computationally intensive and adds to the complexity of the framework. The choice of likelihood calculation should be more rigorously justified with respect to other measures of uncertainty.
    *   The limitations section mentions the need for systematic prompt optimization, a key aspect for LLMs. The choice of prompts can significantly impact performance and generalization ability. This could limit the reproducibility.
    *   The paper notes limitations in Llama models, this raises concerns about the models with respect to the proposed method.
    *   It is unclear how the MCTS is limited to a tree structure, when knowledge graphs may prove better for maintaining multiple possible hypotheses.

*   **Potential Influence:** ARISE has the potential to influence future research in knowledge-augmented reasoning by providing a new framework that effectively tackles error propagation and verification bottlenecks. The results could inspire further exploration of risk-adaptive search strategies and the integration of different reasoning techniques. The framework is flexible and could be adapted for other types of reasoning tasks such as planning, or mathematical solving.

*   **Justification:** ARISE presents a solid engineering contribution by synergistically combining existing methods to enhance knowledge-augmented reasoning in LLMs. The novelty stems from its holistic approach.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** ARISE offers a novel integration of existing techniques to effectively improve knowledge-augmented reasoning, showcasing significant practical improvements. Although it does not introduce groundbreaking individual innovations, it represents a substantial step forward for reasoning in LLMs. The comprehensive experiments provide evidence of ARISE's performance compared to existing approaches. The score reflects this strong contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Efficient Reasoning Models: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10903v1)
- **Authors**: Sicheng Feng, Gongfan Fang, Xinyin Ma, Xinchao Wang
- **Abstract**: Reasoning models have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex and logic-intensive tasks by generating extended Chain-of-Thoughts (CoTs) prior to arriving at a final answer. Yet, the emergence of this "slow-thinking" paradigm, with numerous tokens generated in sequence, inevitably introduces substantial computational overhead. To this end, it highlights an urgent need for effective acceleration. This survey aims to provide a comprehensive overview of recent advances in efficient reasoning. It categorizes existing works into three key directions: (1) shorter - compressing lengthy CoTs into concise yet effective reasoning chains; (2) smaller - developing compact language models with strong reasoning capabilities through techniques such as knowledge distillation, other model compression techniques, and reinforcement learning; and (3) faster - designing efficient decoding strategies to accelerate inference. A curated collection of papers discussed in this survey is available in our GitHub repository.
- **Summary**: The paper provides a survey of efficient reasoning models, categorizing them into three main directions: shortening long Chain-of-Thoughts (CoTs), building small language models (SLMs) with strong reasoning capabilities, and designing faster decoding strategies. It systematically reviews recent advances in each category, offering a taxonomy of existing methods and highlighting their relationships. The survey also discusses evaluation metrics, datasets, and benchmarks, and concludes by outlining key challenges and future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper synthesizes and organizes existing work in efficient reasoning, which is useful given the rapid advancements in this field. The categorization of methods into "shorter," "smaller," and "faster" provides a helpful framework for understanding the various approaches.  However, the survey doesn't introduce completely new perspectives or revolutionary ideas within each category, but rather provides a comprehensive overview. The novelty lies primarily in the synthesis and organization of information rather than in proposing new methodologies.

**Significance:** The survey addresses a very important and timely problem: the computational cost of reasoning with large language models. By summarizing the state of efficient reasoning, the paper serves as a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners seeking to develop and deploy more practical reasoning models. It highlights key areas for future research, such as balancing efficiency and safety and adapting techniques to multimodal settings. However, the survey primarily focuses on *what* has been done, rather than deeply analyzing the *why* behind the successes and failures of different approaches, or offering strong, insightful predictions about which directions are most promising. For example, while the paper touches on safety concerns, a more in-depth analysis of the trade-offs between efficiency and robustness would increase its significance. Similarly, a discussion on limitations of the reviewed approaches and suggestions on how these could be overcome would strengthen the survey's impact.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey covers a wide range of techniques for efficient reasoning.
*   **Clear Organization:** The categorization of methods simplifies understanding the landscape of research.
*   **Timeliness:** Addresses a current and pressing issue in the field of LLMs.
*   **Useful Resources:** Provides links to a curated GitHub repository and discusses evaluation metrics and datasets.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Analytical Depth:** The survey primarily describes existing methods without deeply analyzing their underlying principles, limitations, and trade-offs.
*   **Lack of Strong Predictive Insights:** While it identifies future directions, it does not offer strong, well-justified predictions on which approaches are most likely to succeed.
*   **Relatively Descriptive, Not Prescriptive:** It does not offer concrete guidelines for researchers to develop novel and more efficient reasoning methods. It presents what's been done, but doesn't push the field forward with specific recommendations for future directions.
*   **Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper is a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners interested in efficient reasoning models. Its comprehensive coverage and clear organization make it a helpful starting point for understanding the field. However, its limited analytical depth and lack of predictive insights prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. While useful, its novelty lies primarily in consolidating information rather than presenting fundamentally new ideas. Thus, a score of 7 reflects its utility as a comprehensive overview while acknowledging its limitations in deeper analysis and visionary guidance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### InterAnimate: Taming Region-aware Diffusion Model for Realistic Human Interaction Animation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10905v1)
- **Authors**: Yukang Lin, Yan Hong, Zunnan Xu, Xindi Li, Chao Xu, Chuanbiao Song, Ronghui Li, Haoxing Chen, Jun Lan, Huijia Zhu, Weiqiang Wang, Jianfu Zhang, Xiu Li
- **Abstract**: Recent video generation research has focused heavily on isolated actions, leaving interactive motions-such as hand-face interactions-largely unexamined. These interactions are essential for emerging biometric authentication systems, which rely on interactive motion-based anti-spoofing approaches. From a security perspective, there is a growing need for large-scale, high-quality interactive videos to train and strengthen authentication models. In this work, we introduce a novel paradigm for animating realistic hand-face interactions. Our approach simultaneously learns spatio-temporal contact dynamics and biomechanically plausible deformation effects, enabling natural interactions where hand movements induce anatomically accurate facial deformations while maintaining collision-free contact. To facilitate this research, we present InterHF, a large-scale hand-face interaction dataset featuring 18 interaction patterns and 90,000 annotated videos. Additionally, we propose InterAnimate, a region-aware diffusion model designed specifically for interaction animation. InterAnimate leverages learnable spatial and temporal latents to effectively capture dynamic interaction priors and integrates a region-aware interaction mechanism that injects these priors into the denoising process. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents the first large-scale effort to systematically study human hand-face interactions. Qualitative and quantitative results show InterAnimate produces highly realistic animations, setting a new benchmark. Code and data will be made public to advance research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the InterAnimate paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework, InterAnimate, for generating realistic human interaction animations, specifically focusing on hand-face interactions.  It addresses the lack of attention to interactive motions in existing video generation research. The core components include a region-aware diffusion model and an identity preserver. The approach leverages learnable spatial and temporal latents to capture dynamic interaction priors and employs a region attention mechanism.  The paper also contributes a new large-scale hand-face interaction dataset, InterHF, containing 18 interaction patterns and 90,000 annotated videos, which is crucial for training and evaluating the proposed model. Experiments demonstrate that InterAnimate outperforms existing methods in generating realistic and coherent hand-face animations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in several aspects: (1) Addressing the under-explored area of *interactive* human motion, specifically hand-face interactions, in video generation. Most previous work focuses on isolated actions or human-object interactions.  (2) The region-aware diffusion model, which focuses attention on the interacting regions (hand and face) for better contact dynamics and facial deformation. (3) The InterHF dataset itself is a significant contribution. A large-scale, high-quality dataset dedicated to hand-face interactions didn't exist before.

* **Significance:**  The significance is multifaceted: (1) **Addressing a critical gap:** Existing video generation research largely ignored complex interactions like hand-face. This work directly tackles this, enabling more realistic and natural human animation. (2) **Security application:** The paper highlights the relevance to biometric authentication systems relying on interactive motion-based anti-spoofing. Improved generation of such interactions can enhance the training and robustness of these systems. (3) **Advancement of video generation:** The proposed region-aware diffusion model is a potentially generalizable technique that could be adapted to other types of interactive motion or even human-object interaction scenarios. (4) **Benchmark:**  InterHF dataset sets a new benchmark for evaluating hand-face interaction animation models. The strong quantitative and qualitative results establish a new state-of-the-art for the community.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Problem:**  Clearly identifies and addresses a gap in the existing literature.
    * **Technical Soundness:** The proposed InterAnimate architecture appears technically solid, with well-explained components (region attention, ID Preserver).
    * **Comprehensive Dataset:** InterHF is a substantial contribution, providing a much-needed resource for the field.
    * **Strong Results:**  The quantitative and qualitative results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of InterAnimate over existing methods.  The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of individual components.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generalization Discussion:** While the region-aware approach is valuable, the paper could benefit from a deeper discussion of its potential limitations and how it might be adapted to more general interaction scenarios beyond hand-face.
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't provide specific details on the computational cost of training and inference, which is an important factor for practical applicability.  The use of 8 A100 GPUs suggests a significant computational overhead.
    * **Dataset Bias:**  Even with a large and diverse dataset, there are likely to be inherent biases in InterHF in terms of demographics, lighting conditions, and interaction styles. This should be discussed.
    * **Qualitative Artifacts:** While the qualitative results are impressive, subtle artifacts or inconsistencies are likely present in the generated videos, which should be acknowledged.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of video generation, stimulating further research on interactive human motion. The InterHF dataset is poised to become a standard benchmark.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution to an area that has been largely neglected by the research community. InterAnimate's technical design and the InterHF dataset are significant advancements. The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. While there are some limitations (computational cost, potential biases), the overall impact and potential for future research justify a high score. A score of 8 reflects that this is a highly influential and well-executed piece of work that opens up new avenues for research, although it is not without room for improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Understanding LLMs' Cross-Lingual Context Retrieval: How Good It Is And Where It Comes From
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10906v1)
- **Authors**: Changjiang Gao, Hankun Lin, Shujian Huang, Xin Huang, Xue Han, Junlan Feng, Chao Deng, Jiajun Chen
- **Abstract**: The ability of cross-lingual context retrieval is a fundamental aspect of cross-lingual alignment of large language models (LLMs), where the model extracts context information in one language based on requests in another language. Despite its importance in real-life applications, this ability has not been adequately investigated for state-of-the-art models. In this paper, we evaluate the cross-lingual context retrieval ability of over 40 LLMs across 12 languages to understand the source of this ability, using cross-lingual machine reading comprehension (xMRC) as a representative scenario. Our results show that several small, post-trained open LLMs show strong cross-lingual context retrieval ability, comparable to closed-source LLMs such as GPT-4o, and their estimated oracle performances greatly improve after post-training. Our interpretability analysis shows that the cross-lingual context retrieval process can be divided into two main phases: question encoding and answer retrieval, which are formed in pre-training and post-training, respectively. The phasing stability correlates with xMRC performance, and the xMRC bottleneck lies at the last model layers in the second phase, where the effect of post-training can be evidently observed. Our results also indicate that larger-scale pretraining cannot improve the xMRC performance. Instead, larger LLMs need further multilingual post-training to fully unlock their cross-lingual context retrieval potential. Our code and is available at https://github.com/NJUNLP/Cross-Lingual-Context-Retrieval
- **Summary**: This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of the cross-lingual context retrieval abilities of large language models (LLMs). The authors investigate over 40 LLMs across 12 languages, focusing on cross-lingual machine reading comprehension (xMRC) as a representative scenario. Their findings highlight that several small, post-trained open LLMs demonstrate comparable performance to closed-source LLMs like GPT-4 in cross-lingual context retrieval. Interpretability analysis suggests a two-phased process of question encoding (pre-training) and answer retrieval (post-training), with the latter serving as a bottleneck. Furthermore, they argue that simply scaling up pretraining is insufficient; multilingual post-training is crucial to unlock the full cross-lingual potential of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's strength lies in its *breadth of empirical evaluation*. Systematically benchmarking a large number of LLMs, both open and closed-source, on a standardized xMRC task provides valuable insights into their cross-lingual abilities. The analysis goes beyond mere performance comparison, delving into *interpretability* through layer-wise attribution and hidden state similarity analysis. This is a welcome addition to the field, which often prioritizes performance over understanding model behaviors.

However, the paper's *methodological novelty is relatively limited*. While the use of xMRC is practical, it relies on a somewhat simplistic copy-and-paste approach to answering questions, sidestepping the complexities of abstractive summarization or knowledge integration. The interpretability techniques, while well-chosen (AttentionLRP), are not novel in themselves. Similarly, the core finding - that post-training improves cross-lingual abilities - is not entirely surprising. Many recent works emphasize the importance of domain adaptation through tuning.

That being said, the work *provides substantial empirical evidence* to support these claims. The analysis of performance bottlenecks, highlighting the importance of the answer retrieval phase, offers valuable directions for future research on improving multilingual alignment.  The observation that smaller post-trained models catch up to larger ones specifically underscores the importance of efficient cross-lingual training. Moreover, the paper's discussion of insufficient or language-biased post-training in larger models raises important considerations about effectively scaling multilingual LLMs.

The paper's weakness rests on a few factors. The analysis heavily features LLaMA variants. While a common family of models to study, it is not clear how much of the insights transfer to other architectures. Further, while a broad landscape of LLMs is tested, the analysis tends to gravitate towards LLaMA and focus there. While the observations on larger models needing better fine-tuning and a possible bias are reasonable, the empirical section on those is not as fleshed out.

Despite the limitations, the study is useful as a comprehensive benchmark of cross-lingual capabilities for different LLMs. The paper presents a useful baseline with carefully selected models and languages that can easily be expanded upon in future work.

Score: 7

**Justification:**

The paper earns a score of 7 due to its:

*   **Strengths:** Extensive empirical evaluation across numerous models and languages; thoughtful interpretability analysis revealing a two-phased xMRC process and bottlenecks; identification of potentially language-biased post-training in larger models; provides a strong benchmark and good starting point.

*   **Weaknesses:** Limited methodological novelty; reliance on a simplified xMRC task; Analysis is dominated by results on the LLaMA model family; The insights on post-training needing to be improved for larger models are more theoretical than empirically backed.

*   **Significance:** The paper provides valuable insights into the current state of cross-lingual context retrieval in LLMs, highlighting the importance of post-training and identifying key areas for improvement. It serves as a useful benchmark for future research in multilingual LLMs and contribute to building better alignment. However, the lack of a specific novel methodology or result limits its potential for transformative influence.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Embedding Radiomics into Vision Transformers for Multimodal Medical Image Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10916v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yang, Haiming Zhu, Rihui Zhang, Haipeng Zhang, Jianliang Wang, Chunhao Wang, Minbin Chen, Fang-Fang Yin
- **Abstract**: Background: Deep learning has significantly advanced medical image analysis, with Vision Transformers (ViTs) offering a powerful alternative to convolutional models by modeling long-range dependencies through self-attention. However, ViTs are inherently data-intensive and lack domain-specific inductive biases, limiting their applicability in medical imaging. In contrast, radiomics provides interpretable, handcrafted descriptors of tissue heterogeneity but suffers from limited scalability and integration into end-to-end learning frameworks. In this work, we propose the Radiomics-Embedded Vision Transformer (RE-ViT) that combines radiomic features with data-driven visual embeddings within a ViT backbone. Purpose: To develop a hybrid RE-ViT framework that integrates radiomics and patch-wise ViT embeddings through early fusion, enhancing robustness and performance in medical image classification. Methods: Following the standard ViT pipeline, images were divided into patches. For each patch, handcrafted radiomic features were extracted and fused with linearly projected pixel embeddings. The fused representations were normalized, positionally encoded, and passed to the ViT encoder. A learnable [CLS] token aggregated patch-level information for classification. We evaluated RE-ViT on three public datasets (including BUSI, ChestXray2017, and Retinal OCT) using accuracy, macro AUC, sensitivity, and specificity. RE-ViT was benchmarked against CNN-based (VGG-16, ResNet) and hybrid (TransMed) models. Results: RE-ViT achieved state-of-the-art results: on BUSI, AUC=0.950+/-0.011; on ChestXray2017, AUC=0.989+/-0.004; on Retinal OCT, AUC=0.986+/-0.001, which outperforms other comparison models. Conclusions: The RE-ViT framework effectively integrates radiomics with ViT architectures, demonstrating improved performance and generalizability across multimodal medical image classification tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel "Radiomics-Embedded Vision Transformer" (RE-ViT) framework for multimodal medical image classification. This framework combines handcrafted radiomic features with data-driven visual embeddings within a unified Vision Transformer architecture. The key innovation lies in early fusion of radiomic features and patch-wise visual embeddings, enhancing the model's robustness and performance. The RE-ViT model processes the input image by dividing it into patches.  For each patch, it extracts radiomic features (intensity, texture, heterogeneity) and creates patch embeddings via linear projection.  These two embedding types are averaged, normalized, combined with positional encodings, tokenized, and then fed to a Transformer encoder. The model is evaluated across three datasets: breast ultrasound (BUSI), chest X-ray (ChestXray2017), and retinal OCT. Results demonstrate superior performance compared to CNNs (VGG-16, ResNet) and a hybrid (TransMed) model, with ablation studies highlighting the contributions of each component. The study also visualizes attention maps to demonstrate the model's selective leverage of radiomic or visual embeddings based on the image modality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty stems from the early fusion of handcrafted radiomic features with learned visual embeddings within a Transformer architecture. While the individual components (radiomics, ViTs) are not new, the *specific way* they are integrated and the demonstrated benefits are novel. The design of the RE-ViT allows for capturing interactions between the handcrafted and learned features from the very beginning of training. This differs from late-fusion or post-hoc integration approaches common in the literature, where radiomics are treated as external descriptors or used to guide feature extraction. The visualization of attention maps to analyze the model’s behaviour is another contribution.

*   **Significance:** The study addresses a key challenge in medical image analysis: the data-intensive nature of ViTs and their lack of inherent inductive biases, which can limit their utility when datasets are small. Radiomics offers domain-specific knowledge but often lacks scalability. By combining these, RE-ViT aims to improve performance and interpretability. The demonstrated results, achieving state-of-the-art results on three diverse medical imaging tasks, support the significance of this approach. This is not just an incremental improvement; it is a principled approach to leveraging the strengths of both handcrafted features and data-driven learning in a way that is potentially generalizable to other medical imaging applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Performance:**  Consistent state-of-the-art results across diverse medical imaging datasets.
    *   **Principled Approach:**  The early fusion design offers a more integrated approach to combining radiomics and ViTs.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Extensive ablation studies and comparisons to other models.
    *   **Interpretability Insights:** The attention map visualizations offer insights into how the model leverages different modalities.
    *   **Compatibility:** Inherent compatibility with existing ViT frameworks.
    *   **Clarity and Structure:** The paper is well-written and structured, making it relatively easy to follow the methodology and understand the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Fixed Radiomic Features:** The study acknowledges that the radiomic features are fixed and not task-specific. This might limit efficiency. More adaptive feature selection strategies should be considered.
    *   **Limited Datasets:** While the datasets are diverse, they are all publicly available and might not fully represent the complexity of real-world clinical environments. Multi-institutional data would be beneficial.
    *   **Explainability Limitations:** The explainability is limited to attention map visualization. More rigorous, quantitative interpretability frameworks (SHAP, etc.) would strengthen the clinical validation.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper lacks information on the computational cost and resources required for training and deploying RE-ViT. This may be a limiting factor in certain practical scenarios.
    *   **Generalizability is unclear**. It is necessary to check whether RE-ViT is applicable to other modalities or tasks in medical imaging.

*   **Potential Influence:** The RE-ViT framework has the potential to influence the field of medical image analysis by offering a more effective and interpretable approach to combining handcrafted features and data-driven learning. The integration of radiomics with ViTs addresses a core weakness of ViTs in the medical domain, where data is often limited. It can also pave the way for similar hybrid architectures in other areas where domain knowledge and data-driven approaches can be effectively combined.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper is a solid contribution that offers a novel and principled solution to a key problem in medical image analysis. It is well-evaluated and demonstrates significant performance improvements. The early fusion of radiomics and ViTs, combined with the interpretability insights, is a significant advancement. However, there are limitations related to fixed radiomic features, the scope of the datasets used, limited quantitive explaintability, and computational costs. The score reflects the significant positive contributions offset by these limitations. The paper's potential influence on the field is promising, but further validation and refinement are needed.

- **Classification**: physics.med-ph
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards A Universal Graph Structural Encoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10917v1)
- **Authors**: Jialin Chen, Haolan Zuo, Haoyu Peter Wang, Siqi Miao, Pan Li, Rex Ying
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large-scale pre-training have shown the potential to learn generalizable representations for downstream tasks. In the graph domain, however, capturing and transferring structural information across different graph domains remains challenging, primarily due to the inherent differences in topological patterns across various contexts. Additionally, most existing models struggle to capture the complexity of rich graph structures, leading to inadequate exploration of the embedding space. To address these challenges, we propose GFSE, a universal graph structural encoder designed to capture transferable structural patterns across diverse domains such as molecular graphs, social networks, and citation networks. GFSE is the first cross-domain graph structural encoder pre-trained with multiple self-supervised learning objectives. Built on a Graph Transformer, GFSE incorporates attention mechanisms informed by graph inductive bias, enabling it to encode intricate multi-level and fine-grained topological features. The pre-trained GFSE produces generic and theoretically expressive positional and structural encoding for graphs, which can be seamlessly integrated with various downstream graph feature encoders, including graph neural networks for vectorized features and Large Language Models for text-attributed graphs. Comprehensive experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate GFSE's capability to significantly enhance the model's performance while requiring substantially less task-specific fine-tuning. Notably, GFSE achieves state-of-the-art performance in 81.6% evaluated cases, spanning diverse graph models and datasets, highlighting its potential as a powerful and versatile encoder for graph-structured data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GFSE (Graph Foundational Structural Encoder), a novel approach for pre-training graph neural networks to learn universal structural representations across different graph domains (e.g., molecular graphs, social networks). GFSE leverages a Graph Transformer architecture enhanced with biased attention mechanisms informed by graph inductive bias. It is pre-trained using multiple self-supervised learning objectives: shortest path distance regression, motif counting, community detection, and graph contrastive learning. The pre-trained GFSE generates positional and structural encodings (PSEs) that can be integrated into downstream graph feature encoders (including graph neural networks and Large Language Models (LLMs) for text-attributed graphs) to enhance performance.  Experiments across synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that GFSE can significantly improve model performance with limited task-specific fine-tuning, achieving state-of-the-art results in many cases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to introduce the *first* cross-domain graph structural encoder pre-trained with multiple self-supervised learning objectives. While previous graph pre-training methods exist (e.g., GraphMAE, GPSE), GFSE's combination of objectives *across domains* and its specific architectural choices might represent a degree of novelty. The integration with LLMs and vectorized features for other types of graph data (feature-attributed and text-attributed graphs), is a valuable architectural innovation. The explicit incorporation of relative positional encoding into the attention bias of the transformer is a notable design choice that addresses limitations in standard graph transformers.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in graph representation learning: the difficulty of transferring knowledge across different graph domains. By focusing on universal structural attributes rather than domain-specific features, GFSE aims to improve the generalizability of graph models. The improved expressiveness of the positional encoding and integration with LLMs is a promising direction. The reported performance gains on various datasets suggest that GFSE has the potential to be a useful tool for graph-based tasks, especially in scenarios with limited labeled data or when transferring knowledge across domains is crucial. The theoretical justification concerning Weisfeiler-Lehman expressiveness provides further support for the model's design.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:**  The paper presents a well-designed framework that considers multiple aspects of graph structure (connectivity, motifs, communities).
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The extensive experimental evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of GFSE across a variety of datasets and tasks.  Achieving state-of-the-art results in a significant percentage of evaluated cases is impressive.
    *   **Integration with LLMs:** The seamless integration with LLMs for text-attributed graphs is a valuable feature that expands the applicability of GFSE.
    *   **Theoretical Support:** The expressiveness analysis using Structural Encoding enhanced Global Weisfeiler-Lehman test (SEG-WL) lends theoretical backing to the model's architecture.
    *   **Ablation studies** that evaluate different aspects of the model, tasks, and uncertainty.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While the authors argue that the chosen pre-training tasks are "balanced and effective", the overall framework involves a complex combination of architectural choices and objectives. It would be good to see a more simplified ablation study that validates specific inductive bias, or the type of edge level or node level information being captured for each task.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Pre-computing certain values, like shortest path distances, can become computationally expensive for large graphs. The paper only mentions that this can be solved with METIS. It would be useful to evaluate the performance with METIS partitioning in larger graphs.
    *   **Clarity:** Some sections could benefit from more detailed explanations. For instance, the precise details of how GFSE is integrated with the LLM during the generation of graph tokens, is not fully transparent.

*   **Potential Influence:** GFSE has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Providing a strong baseline for cross-domain graph representation learning.
    *   Inspiring further research on learning universal structural attributes for graphs.
    *   Facilitating the application of graph models to new domains with limited labeled data.
    *   Encouraging the development of more expressive and generalizable graph encoders.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** GFSE makes a significant contribution towards addressing the challenge of cross-domain transfer in graph representation learning. While some graph pre-training techniques already exist, GFSE's multi-objective approach, informed by graph inductive bias, combined with solid empirical support, warrants a high score. The weaknesses related to complexity, computational scaling and clarity hold it back from a higher rating. The expressiveness analysis provides theoretical justification, and the gains on real-world datasets shows promise as a universal graph structural encoder. The consistent improvements over multiple baselines and datasets are also valuable.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Human-Agent Teaming: A Review of Empirical Studies from the Process Dynamics Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10918v1)
- **Authors**: Mengyao Wang, Jiayun Wu, Shuai Ma, Nuo Li, Peng Zhang, Ning Gu, Tun Lu
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of AI, including Large Language Models, has propelled autonomous agents forward, accelerating the human-agent teaming (HAT) paradigm to leverage complementary strengths. However, HAT research remains fragmented, often focusing on isolated team development phases or specific challenges like trust calibration while overlooking the real-world need for adaptability. Addressing these gaps, a process dynamics perspective is adopted to systematically review HAT using the T$^4$ framework: Team Formation, Task and Role Development, Team Development, and Team Improvement. Each phase is examined in terms of its goals, actions, and evaluation metrics, emphasizing the co-evolution of task and team dynamics. Special focus is given to the second and third phases, highlighting key factors such as team roles, shared mental model, and backup behaviors. This holistic perspective identifies future research directions for advancing long-term adaptive HAT.
- **Summary**: **Summary**

This paper presents a systematic review of Human-Agent Teaming (HAT) research within the Human-Computer Interaction (HCI) community. It addresses the fragmented nature of existing HAT research and the lack of explicit focus on adaptability. The authors propose a HAT Process Dynamics Framework (T4) based on Kozlowski's human team development theory, comprising Team Formation, Task and Role Development, Team Development, and Team Improvement phases. The review analyzes empirical studies using a hybrid coding approach, focusing on task and team dynamics within each phase. It highlights the importance of understanding the evolving nature of HAT to foster long-term adaptive team development and identifies future research directions, particularly in team formation, team improvement and agent delegation. It focuses on SMM and the challenges thereof.

**Critical Evaluation**

The paper's strength lies in its attempt to provide a holistic and structured perspective on HAT, something that's often missing in current research. The adoption of a process dynamics perspective, drawing inspiration from human team development theories, is a useful lens for understanding HAT's complexities. The T4 framework offers a systematic way to categorize and analyze existing HAT research, providing a potentially useful organizing principle for the field.

However, the novelty of the paper is somewhat limited by its reliance on established theories from human team dynamics. While adapting these theories to the HAT context is valuable, the paper doesn't fundamentally introduce novel concepts specific to the unique challenges and opportunities presented by human-agent collaboration.  The framework is heavily adapted from earlier models rather than offering any new critical insights into these models. Also, the review focuses primarily on the HCI perspective, while other fields like Human-Robot Interaction (HRI) have conducted similar work.

The paper's significance lies in its potential to synthesize fragmented research and provide actionable insights for building adaptive HAT systems. The identified research gaps, particularly in team formation and improvement, can guide future research efforts.  The section on constructing SMM's, including challenges such as addressing mismatched commitments and reasoning gaps, is a valuable contribution.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited novelty:** The framework relies heavily on adapting existing theories from human team development, limiting its groundbreaking potential. It largely repurposes older models rather than creating new insights.
*   **HCI Focus:** The narrow focus on HCI research omits relevant empirical studies and theoretical frameworks in HRI and other fields exploring human-AI collaboration.
*   **Uneven depth:** While the review covers a wide range of topics, some areas (like agent delegation in the Team Improvement phase) receive less detailed analysis.
*   **T4 Framework's Practicality:** The practical application and validation of the T4 framework are not fully demonstrated, leaving questions about its utility for guiding the design and implementation of HAT systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a comprehensive review and a helpful organizing framework but lacks groundbreaking theoretical contributions or a strong emphasis on the latest trends. It repurposes earlier concepts rather than innovating or suggesting any new perspectives. The inclusion of material from human teams is potentially beneficial. While the potential impact of the review is moderate, its contribution is worthwhile and beneficial, but it could be more comprehensive by including more materials. 

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### MSCRS: Multi-modal Semantic Graph Prompt Learning Framework for Conversational Recommender Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10921v1)
- **Authors**: Yibiao Wei, Jie Zou, Weikang Guo, Guoqing Wang, Xing Xu, Yang Yang
- **Abstract**: Conversational Recommender Systems (CRSs) aim to provide personalized recommendations by interacting with users through conversations. Most existing studies of CRS focus on extracting user preferences from conversational contexts. However, due to the short and sparse nature of conversational contexts, it is difficult to fully capture user preferences by conversational contexts only. We argue that multi-modal semantic information can enrich user preference expressions from diverse dimensions (e.g., a user preference for a certain movie may stem from its magnificent visual effects and compelling storyline). In this paper, we propose a multi-modal semantic graph prompt learning framework for CRS, named MSCRS. First, we extract textual and image features of items mentioned in the conversational contexts. Second, we capture higher-order semantic associations within different semantic modalities (collaborative, textual, and image) by constructing modality-specific graph structures. Finally, we propose an innovative integration of multi-modal semantic graphs with prompt learning, harnessing the power of large language models to comprehensively explore high-dimensional semantic relationships. Experimental results demonstrate that our proposed method significantly improves accuracy in item recommendation, as well as generates more natural and contextually relevant content in response generation. We have released the code and the expanded multi-modal CRS datasets to facilitate further exploration in related research\footnote{https://github.com/BIAOBIAO12138/MSCRS-main}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MSCRS, a multi-modal semantic graph prompt learning framework for conversational recommender systems (CRSs). It addresses the limitation of solely relying on conversational contexts for extracting user preferences by incorporating multi-modal semantic information (textual and image features of items mentioned in conversations). The framework involves three main steps: 1) Extracting textual and image features of items; 2) Constructing modality-specific graph structures (collaborative, textual, and image) to capture higher-order semantic associations; and 3) Integrating these graphs with prompt learning using large language models (LLMs). The authors demonstrate through experiments on two CRS datasets that MSCRS improves item recommendation accuracy and generates more natural and contextually relevant responses compared to existing methods. They also release the code and expanded datasets to encourage further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has a reasonable degree of novelty.
    *   **Strengths:** The idea of integrating multi-modal semantic information (particularly image features) along with collaborative information into a prompt-learning framework for CRSs is a valuable contribution. Most existing works focus primarily on textual information or knowledge graphs. The modular structure, with separate graph construction for each modality (text, image, and collaborative), before fusion, is sensible for preserving intra-modality relationships.
    *   **Weaknesses:**  While the combination is novel, the individual components are not entirely new. The use of graph neural networks (GNNs) for encoding semantic relationships and prompt learning with LLMs is relatively well-established. The novelty lies in the specific combination and application to the CRS domain. Additionally, the method of extracting visual features using a pre-trained ViT model is somewhat standard.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Strengths:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation in current CRSs – the inability to fully capture user preferences due to the sparseness of conversational contexts. By leveraging multi-modal information, the framework allows for a more comprehensive understanding of user needs. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate improvements in both recommendation accuracy and response quality. The release of code and expanded datasets can facilitate future research in this area.
    *   **Weaknesses:**  The experimental evaluation, while demonstrating improvements, could benefit from more in-depth analysis. It would be more compelling to have ablation studies to show the relative contributions of each of the multi-modal graph components (Collaborative semantic graph, Textual semantic graph, and Image semantic graph) against the overall performance. While statistical significance is indicated, a larger variety of baseline approaches for prompt tuning would be of additional benefit. Additionally, there is little discussion on the computational overhead and scalability of the proposed approach. The approach relies on LLMs and constructing/fusing various graph structures, which raises questions about its feasibility for real-time large-scale CRS applications.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized. The framework is clearly explained, and the experimental setup is well-described. However, Figure 1 is less helpful than it could be as it is challenging to read, and the details of the prompt templates as provided in Figure 3 are not very detailed.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**. Here's the rationale:

The paper introduces a novel combination of techniques to address a relevant problem in conversational recommendation. It clearly demonstrates how multi-modal information enhances user preference modeling and leads to improved performance. The release of the code and datasets is a positive step.

However, the individual components aren't groundbreaking, and the experiments lack an in-depth analysis of the impact of each component. There is also little discussion on computational complexity and scalability of the approach, which has been a limitation in GNN models in the past, limiting the impact on large-scale CRSs. Thus, although a beneficial and valuable contribution, the novelty and comprehensiveness leave some room for improvement.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can LLMs Leverage Observational Data? Towards Data-Driven Causal Discovery with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10936v1)
- **Authors**: Yuni Susanti, Michael Färber
- **Abstract**: Causal discovery traditionally relies on statistical methods applied to observational data, often requiring large datasets and assumptions about underlying causal structures. Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have introduced new possibilities for causal discovery by providing domain expert knowledge. However, it remains unclear whether LLMs can effectively process observational data for causal discovery. In this work, we explore the potential of LLMs for data-driven causal discovery by integrating observational data for LLM-based reasoning. Specifically, we examine whether LLMs can effectively utilize observational data through two prompting strategies: pairwise prompting and breadth first search (BFS)-based prompting. In both approaches, we incorporate the observational data directly into the prompt to assess LLMs' ability to infer causal relationships from such data. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that incorporating observational data enhances causal discovery, boosting F1 scores by up to 0.11 point using both pairwise and BFS LLM-based prompting, while outperforming traditional statistical causal discovery baseline by up to 0.52 points. Our findings highlight the potential and limitations of LLMs for data-driven causal discovery, demonstrating their ability to move beyond textual metadata and effectively interpret and utilize observational data for more informed causal reasoning. Our studies lays the groundwork for future advancements toward fully LLM-driven causal discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) for data-driven causal discovery. It investigates whether LLMs can effectively use observational data to infer causal relationships, going beyond relying solely on metadata or pre-existing knowledge. The authors propose prompting strategies to incorporate observational data into LLM-based reasoning, comparing pairwise and breadth-first search (BFS) prompting methods. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that integrating observational data enhances causal discovery, outperforming traditional statistical causal discovery baselines. The work highlights the potential of LLMs to utilize observational data for more informed causal reasoning, moving toward LLM-driven causal discovery.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a gap:**  The paper tackles a critical gap in the use of LLMs for causal discovery. Existing LLM-based methods often rely on metadata and textual descriptions, neglecting the valuable information present in observational data. This work aims to bridge this gap, exploring how LLMs can directly reason from data.
    *   **Novel approach:** The integration of observational data into LLM prompts for causal inference is a significant step. The paper introduces and compares two distinct prompting strategies: pairwise and BFS.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The study is supported by experiments on benchmark datasets, providing quantitative evidence for the effectiveness of incorporating observational data. The results demonstrate that LLMs can outperform traditional statistical methods and improve their performance by leveraging data.
    *   **Clear problem definition and presentation:** The paper clearly defines the problem, presents the proposed methods, and discusses the results in a well-organized manner.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scalability Challenges:** The paper mentions the quadratic query requirement for pairwise prompting and the linear requirement for BFS. While BFS addresses the pair-wise problem, The scalability of the BFS approach is potentially limited by the LLM's token limitations because the query history for the context window needs to be in the prompt.
    *   **Limited data size:**  LLMs are kept at a fixed number of 100 observational data due to token limitations.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The effectiveness of LLMs can vary across datasets. The paper suggests exploring performance across a broader range of datasets.
    *   **Sampling Strategy Limitations:** The paper doesn't demonstrate that different sampling techniques (random, cluster, systematic, etc) offer significant advantages.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper's novelty lies in systematically investigating the direct use of observational data within LLM prompts for causal discovery. This extends the current application of LLMs in the domain, which is primarily focused on leveraging expert knowledge from textual data.
    *   The significance is moderate to high. By showcasing that LLMs can reason from observational data, the paper opens up new avenues for causal discovery that combines the power of statistical methods with the reasoning abilities of LLMs.
    *   The results could inspire further research on hybrid causal discovery methods and the development of more efficient prompting techniques for LLMs.
    *   The work contributes to the broader understanding of LLMs' capabilities and limitations in complex reasoning tasks.

*   **Overall Impression:**

    The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of causal discovery and LLMs. By demonstrating the potential of LLMs to leverage observational data, the paper opens new avenues for research and application. While some limitations exist, the work provides a solid foundation for future advancements in LLM-driven causal discovery.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper earns a 7 because it addresses a relevant problem with a novel approach and provides empirical evidence to support its claims. It's a valuable addition to the literature and paves the way for future research in this area.  The limitations related to scalability and dataset dependency, however, prevent it from achieving a higher score. A score above 7 would be justified with more work to address these limitations, perhaps with innovative token reduction techniques or alternative prompting strategies that allow the LLMs to reason across larger observational data spaces.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unveiling Challenges for LLMs in Enterprise Data Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10950v1)
- **Authors**: Jan-Micha Bodensohn, Ulf Brackmann, Liane Vogel, Anupam Sanghi, Carsten Binnig
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated significant potential for automating data engineering tasks on tabular data, giving enterprises a valuable opportunity to reduce the high costs associated with manual data handling. However, the enterprise domain introduces unique challenges that existing LLM-based approaches for data engineering often overlook, such as large table sizes, more complex tasks, and the need for internal knowledge. To bridge these gaps, we identify key enterprise-specific challenges related to data, tasks, and background knowledge and conduct a comprehensive study of their impact on recent LLMs for data engineering. Our analysis reveals that LLMs face substantial limitations in real-world enterprise scenarios, resulting in significant accuracy drops. Our findings contribute to a systematic understanding of LLMs for enterprise data engineering to support their adoption in industry.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Unveiling Challenges for LLMs in Enterprise Data Engineering" investigates the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on data engineering tasks in real-world enterprise settings, contrasting it with their performance on public benchmarks. It identifies key enterprise-specific challenges related to data characteristics, task complexity, and the need for internal knowledge. The study conducts experiments using several state-of-the-art LLMs from OpenAI, Anthropic, and Meta on representative SAP customer data and a variety of data engineering tasks. The results demonstrate that LLMs face significant limitations in enterprise scenarios, leading to accuracy drops compared to their performance on web-sourced data. The paper emphasizes the necessity for tailored approaches and further research to make LLMs viable for enterprise data engineering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper provides a valuable and systematic analysis of the challenges encountered when applying LLMs to data engineering tasks within enterprise environments. While some initial work has pointed out discrepancies between public benchmarks and enterprise data, this study goes further by categorizing the challenges into data, task, and knowledge aspects and evaluating their isolated and combined impact on several LLMs. The focus on a specific and widespread enterprise system (SAP) is also novel. The identified challenges aren't entirely new, but framing them in a structured way and conducting empirical studies to quantify their impact is a significant contribution.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work lies in its ability to provide a grounded understanding of the limitations of current LLMs for enterprise data engineering. It highlights crucial gaps that existing LLM-based approaches often overlook. By revealing the reasons behind performance declines in real-world scenarios, this research can help guide future development efforts towards more effective LLM solutions for enterprises. The insights can inform the design of specialized models, prompting strategies, and system architectures tailored for enterprise data. Furthermore, it points to the need to combine LLMs with more traditional data engineering methods.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper provides a structured framework for analyzing the unique challenges in enterprise data engineering, categorizing them into data, task, and knowledge challenges.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The study includes well-designed experiments with real-world enterprise data, allowing for a thorough evaluation of LLM performance under different conditions.
*   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers practical insights for researchers and practitioners, outlining the factors that contribute to LLM performance declines and suggesting directions for improvement.
*   **Real-world Application Focus:** The use of SAP data and case studies ensures the relevance of the findings to real-world enterprise scenarios.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the paper considers a range of tasks (column type annotation, entity matching, data integration), it might not cover all the potential applications of LLMs in enterprise data engineering. Further expansion of the tasks would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Specific Data Source:** The focus on SAP data may limit the generalizability of the findings to other types of enterprise data. Although SAP is a major enterprise system, future work could benefit from incorporating data from other systems.
*   **LLM Versions:** The rapid development in LLMs means that some of the specific models tested may already be outdated. While the core challenges identified likely remain relevant, updates with newer LLMs would be useful.
*   **Cost Analysis:** The cost analysis focuses on token costs. More detailed cost modeling, considering API request limits, infrastructure costs, and the potential need for fine-tuning would offer a more holistic view.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and systematic analysis of the challenges associated with using LLMs in enterprise data engineering. The experiments are well-designed, and the results are insightful, providing a grounded understanding of the limitations of current LLMs in real-world scenarios. The identified data, task, and knowledge categories offer a good framework for understanding these limitations, and the paper explores multiple dimensions within each category. While some limitations remain regarding the scope of tasks and the data source, the paper offers a meaningful contribution with good potential for future research.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### When is Task Vector Provably Effective for Model Editing? A Generalization Analysis of Nonlinear Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10957v1)
- **Authors**: Hongkang Li, Yihua Zhang, Shuai Zhang, Meng Wang, Sijia Liu, Pin-Yu Chen
- **Abstract**: Task arithmetic refers to editing the pre-trained model by adding a weighted sum of task vectors, each of which is the weight update from the pre-trained model to fine-tuned models for certain tasks. This approach recently gained attention as a computationally efficient inference method for model editing, e.g., multi-task learning, forgetting, and out-of-domain generalization capabilities. However, the theoretical understanding of why task vectors can execute various conceptual operations remains limited, due to the highly non-convexity of training Transformer-based models. To the best of our knowledge, this paper provides the first theoretical characterization of the generalization guarantees of task vector methods on nonlinear Transformers. We consider a conceptual learning setting, where each task is a binary classification problem based on a discriminative pattern. We theoretically prove the effectiveness of task addition in simultaneously learning a set of irrelevant or aligned tasks, as well as the success of task negation in unlearning one task from irrelevant or contradictory tasks. Moreover, we prove the proper selection of linear coefficients for task arithmetic to achieve guaranteed generalization to out-of-domain tasks. All of our theoretical results hold for both dense-weight parameters and their low-rank approximations. Although established in a conceptual setting, our theoretical findings were validated on a practical machine unlearning task using the large language model Phi-1.5 (1.3B).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a theoretical analysis of task vector methods for model editing in nonlinear Transformers. It focuses on understanding when and why task vector approaches perform well in multi-task learning, unlearning, and out-of-domain generalization. The authors provide generalization guarantees for task vector methods, focusing on a conceptual learning setting involving binary classification tasks based on discriminative patterns. They theoretically prove the effectiveness of task addition for learning irrelevant or aligned tasks, the success of task negation for unlearning tasks, and the proper selection of linear coefficients for out-of-domain generalization. The analysis considers both dense-weight parameters and low-rank approximations. The theoretical results are validated on a practical machine unlearning task using the Phi-1.5 large language model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is in providing the *first* theoretical characterization, to the best of the authors' knowledge, of task vector methods' generalization performance in *nonlinear* Transformers.  Previous work has leaned on empirical observations or made simplifying assumptions like linearizing models under the Neural Tangent Kernel regime. This paper attempts to move beyond that and analyze the non-convex setting. The feature learning analysis approach to justify the use of linear combination of task vectors is also of value. This is a step forward, though the conceptual setting is still somewhat limiting.

*   **Significance:** Task vector methods are gaining popularity due to their computational efficiency in model editing.  A theoretical understanding of their capabilities and limitations is important for guiding the practical application of these methods. Providing insights into the effect of task correlations on the arithmetic operations of task vectors and offering conditions for successful out-of-domain generalization can guide the choice of task arithmetic parameters. The paper also provides theoretical support for the use of low-rank and sparse approximations, which is crucial for efficient deployment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **First Theoretical Treatment:** Providing a theoretical framework for a relatively new and empirically successful technique.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** Covers multi-task learning, unlearning, and out-of-domain generalization.
    *   **Practical Validation:**  Validating theoretical findings with experiments on a large language model (Phi-1.5).
    *   **Considers practical constraints**: Analyzes low rank approximations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Conceptual Setting:** The analysis is performed on a simplified data model and a one-layer Transformer, which raises questions about the generalizability of the results to more complex, real-world scenarios. While validated empirically, the gap between theory and practice remains.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While Phi-1.5 experiments are used for validation, the focus of the theoretical analysis on single-head one-layer transformers doesn't fully address the complexities inherent in current large language models.
    *   **Simplifying Assumptions:** As in most theoretical works, making assumptions, while necessary for tractability, inherently limit the scope of the derived results.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development and application of task vector methods by providing a better understanding of their theoretical properties. It could stimulate further research on generalizing the theoretical results to more complex architectures and tasks, and on developing more robust task vector selection strategies.

**Justification:**

The paper is an important contribution to the theoretical understanding of model editing using task vectors. The authors address a gap in the literature by providing generalization guarantees for task vector methods in nonlinear Transformers. The theoretical analysis is rigorous and the results are validated with experiments. However, the simplified setting limits the generalizability of the results and requires future research to expand the theoretical framework to more complex architectures and tasks.

**Score: 6.5**

The paper makes a solid contribution by providing the *first* theoretical understanding in a *nonlinear* setting. However, the simplifying assumptions and the conceptual setting means that the generalizability to more complex models isn't guaranteed and the theoretical analysis has room for improvement. Hence it scores just above the middle, but with significant potential for higher impact through future work.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### An Efficient and Mixed Heterogeneous Model for Image Restoration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10967v1)
- **Authors**: Yubin Gu, Yuan Meng, Kaihang Zheng, Xiaoshuai Sun, Jiayi Ji, Weijian Ruan, Liujuan Cao, Rongrong Ji
- **Abstract**: Image restoration~(IR), as a fundamental multimedia data processing task, has a significant impact on downstream visual applications. In recent years, researchers have focused on developing general-purpose IR models capable of handling diverse degradation types, thereby reducing the cost and complexity of model development. Current mainstream approaches are based on three architectural paradigms: CNNs, Transformers, and Mambas. CNNs excel in efficient inference, whereas Transformers and Mamba excel at capturing long-range dependencies and modeling global contexts. While each architecture has demonstrated success in specialized, single-task settings, limited efforts have been made to effectively integrate heterogeneous architectures to jointly address diverse IR challenges. To bridge this gap, we propose RestorMixer, an efficient and general-purpose IR model based on mixed-architecture fusion. RestorMixer adopts a three-stage encoder-decoder structure, where each stage is tailored to the resolution and feature characteristics of the input. In the initial high-resolution stage, CNN-based blocks are employed to rapidly extract shallow local features. In the subsequent stages, we integrate a refined multi-directional scanning Mamba module with a multi-scale window-based self-attention mechanism. This hierarchical and adaptive design enables the model to leverage the strengths of CNNs in local feature extraction, Mamba in global context modeling, and attention mechanisms in dynamic feature refinement. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that RestorMixer achieves leading performance across multiple IR tasks while maintaining high inference efficiency. The official code can be accessed at https://github.com/ClimBin/RestorMixer.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the information provided.

**Summary**

The paper introduces RestorMixer, a new image restoration (IR) model designed to handle diverse degradation types by integrating heterogeneous architectures: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), Transformers, and Mamba. It employs a three-stage encoder-decoder structure. The initial stage uses CNN-based blocks for efficient local feature extraction. Subsequent stages combine a refined multi-directional scanning Mamba module with a multi-scale window-based self-attention mechanism.  The model aims to leverage the strengths of each architecture, CNNs for local features, Mamba for global context, and attention for dynamic feature refinement. Experiments on various IR tasks demonstrate that RestorMixer achieves leading performance while maintaining high inference efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integration* of three distinct architectural paradigms (CNN, Transformer, and Mamba) within a single IR model. While each architecture has been explored individually for IR tasks, RestorMixer's approach to combining them hierarchically and adaptively based on the resolution and feature characteristics represents a relatively novel attempt. It doesn't necessarily introduce fundamentally *new* building blocks within each paradigm but rather a new way of *orchestrating* existing ones.

**Significance:** The potential significance stems from addressing the limitations of single-architecture approaches to general-purpose IR. If RestorMixer demonstrably achieves superior performance *and* efficiency compared to existing methods, it could influence the design of future IR models, encouraging the exploration of heterogeneous architectures. The claim of maintaining high inference efficiency alongside leading performance is particularly important for practical applications. General-purpose IR models save time and costs associated with architecture engineering per individual task (deblurring, de-snowing, etc).

**Strengths:**
