The paper presents a novel and effective approach to video summarization that leverages the power of LLMs. The state-of-the-art results demonstrate the significance of this approach. However, the computational cost, reliance on pre-trained models (LLM hallucination concerns, domain applicability), and limited validation datasets slightly limit the overall impact. Given the growing interest in LLMs, this paper has the potential to inspire future research and development in multimedia content analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### VEXP: A Low-Cost RISC-V ISA Extension for Accelerated Softmax Computation in Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11227v1)
- **Authors**: Run Wang, Gamze Islamoglu, Andrea Belano, Viviane Potocnik, Francesco Conti, Angelo Garofalo, Luca Benini
- **Abstract**: While Transformers are dominated by Floating-Point (FP) Matrix-Multiplications, their aggressive acceleration through dedicated hardware or many-core programmable systems has shifted the performance bottleneck to non-linear functions like Softmax. Accelerating Softmax is challenging due to its non-pointwise, non-linear nature, with exponentiation as the most demanding step. To address this, we design a custom arithmetic block for Bfloat16 exponentiation leveraging a novel approximation algorithm based on Schraudolph's method, and we integrate it into the Floating-Point Unit (FPU) of the RISC-V cores of a compute cluster, through custom Instruction Set Architecture (ISA) extensions, with a negligible area overhead of 1\%. By optimizing the software kernels to leverage the extension, we execute Softmax with 162.7$\times$ less latency and 74.3$\times$ less energy compared to the baseline cluster, achieving an 8.2$\times$ performance improvement and 4.1$\times$ higher energy efficiency for the FlashAttention-2 kernel in GPT-2 configuration. Moreover, the proposed approach enables a multi-cluster system to efficiently execute end-to-end inference of pre-trained Transformer models, such as GPT-2, GPT-3 and ViT, achieving up to 5.8$\times$ and 3.6$\times$ reduction in latency and energy consumption, respectively, without requiring re-training and with negligible accuracy loss.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "VEXP: A Low-Cost RISC-V ISA Extension for Accelerated Softmax Computation in Transformers" addresses the performance bottleneck caused by Softmax computations in Transformer models, which becomes prominent when GEMM operations are highly accelerated. It proposes a custom arithmetic block for Bfloat16 exponentiation, integrated into the FPU of RISC-V cores via custom ISA extensions. The solution leverages an enhanced Schraudolph's approximation algorithm.  Experiments show significant reductions in latency and energy consumption for Softmax and FlashAttention-2 kernels, leading to improved performance and energy efficiency on Transformer models like GPT-2, GPT-3, and ViT, all while incurring a minimal area overhead. The paper emphasizes maintaining accuracy and avoiding retraining of models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:
    *   **Specific Focus on Softmax Bottleneck:** While many papers address Transformer acceleration, this one specifically targets the emerging Softmax bottleneck in highly optimized systems.
    *   **Low-Cost ISA Extension Approach:** Using a RISC-V ISA extension for exponentiation is a relatively low-cost, flexible approach compared to dedicated hardwired accelerators for the entire Softmax function.
    *   **Enhanced Schraudolph's Method for Bfloat16:** Applying Schraudolph's method and its refinement to Bfloat16 data is a novel adaptation for Transformer workloads. The enhancements to the algorithm itself, as outlined in prior work by Belano et al. [25], contribute to improved accuracy.
    *   **End-to-End Demonstration:** The paper provides a comprehensive end-to-end evaluation, showcasing the impact on actual Transformer models and highlighting the benefits of minimal accuracy loss and no retraining requirements.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Impact:** The demonstrated improvements in latency and energy efficiency for Transformer inference have practical implications for deploying these models on resource-constrained devices.
    *   **RISC-V Ecosystem:** The paper reinforces the potential of RISC-V as a platform for domain-specific acceleration, showcasing the ease of adding custom instructions for specific workloads.
    *   **Balanced Approach:**  By focusing on a targeted ISA extension, the design strikes a balance between performance, flexibility, and cost, making it a potentially attractive solution for a wider range of applications compared to more aggressive, fully customized accelerators.
    *   **Scalability:** The scalability experiments are crucial, demonstrating that the improvements persist across multi-cluster systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents detailed performance, energy, and accuracy results for various configurations and models.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology, including the hardware implementation details, software optimizations, and benchmarking setup, is clearly explained.
    *   **Contextualization:** The paper clearly positions its contribution within the existing literature, highlighting the advantages over existing Softmax acceleration approaches.
    *   **Silicon Implementation:** Providing an implementation for a real technology node (GF 12nm) adds significant weight to the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Comparison:** While the comparison to prior work is useful, a direct comparison to a closely related Softmax accelerator on the *same* hardware platform would be highly valuable. This could strengthen the case for the proposed approach's superiority more convincingly.  Table IV's comparison relies on comparing different architectures implemented on different platforms and technologies.
    *   **Power Consumption:** The power consumption of the cluster increased by 1.8%. Although the energy per operation is drastically reduced for Softmax, the overall effect on system-level power could limit the benefits in power-constrained environments. This aspect could be discussed in more detail.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach has the potential to influence future designs of domain-specific accelerators for AI inference, particularly in the context of RISC-V architectures. The focus on low-cost ISA extensions and the balancing act between performance, flexibility, and cost is a valuable lesson for the field. The ability to avoid model retraining further enhances the practical value of this work.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, the paper merits a score of 7.

*   The focus on the Softmax bottleneck, the low-cost ISA extension approach, the adaptation of Schraudolph's method, and the end-to-end evaluation contribute significantly to the paper's novelty.
*   The paper's impact on practical Transformer deployments and the RISC-V ecosystem further underscores its significance.
*   The detailed evaluations, clear methodology, and solid implementation strengthen the paper's credibility.
*   The main weakness is the difficulty of direct comparison due to the reliance on literature data and increased cluster power, which may slightly temper the overall enthusiasm for this work, despite its overall merits.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Nondeterministic Polynomial-time Problem Challenge: An Ever-Scaling Reasoning Benchmark for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11239v1)
- **Authors**: Chang Yang, Ruiyu Wang, Junzhe Jiang, Qi Jiang, Qinggang Zhang, Yanchen Deng, Shuxin Li, Shuyue Hu, Bo Li, Florian T. Pokorny, Xiao Huang, Xinrun Wang
- **Abstract**: Reasoning is the fundamental capability of large language models (LLMs). Due to the rapid progress of LLMs, there are two main issues of current benchmarks: i) these benchmarks can be crushed in a short time (less than 1 year), and ii) these benchmarks may be easily hacked. To handle these issues, we propose the ever-scalingness for building the benchmarks which are uncrushable, unhackable, auto-verifiable and general. This paper presents Nondeterministic Polynomial-time Problem Challenge (NPPC), an ever-scaling reasoning benchmark for LLMs. Specifically, the NPPC has three main modules: i) npgym, which provides a unified interface of 25 well-known NP-complete problems and can generate any number of instances with any levels of complexities, ii) npsolver: which provides a unified interface to evaluate the problem instances with both online and offline models via APIs and local deployments, respectively, and iii) npeval: which provides the comprehensive and ready-to-use tools to analyze the performances of LLMs over different problems, the number of tokens, the aha moments, the reasoning errors and the solution errors. Extensive experiments over widely-used LLMs demonstrate: i) NPPC can successfully decrease the performances of advanced LLMs' performances to below 10%, demonstrating that NPPC is uncrushable, ii) DeepSeek-R1, Claude-3.7-Sonnet, and o1/o3-mini are the most powerful LLMs, where DeepSeek-R1 outperforms Claude-3.7-Sonnet and o1/o3-mini in most NP-complete problems considered, and iii) the numbers of tokens, aha moments in the advanced LLMs, e.g., Claude-3.7-Sonnet and DeepSeek-R1, are observed first to increase and then decrease when the problem instances become more and more difficult. We believe that NPPC is the first ever-scaling reasoning benchmark, serving as the uncrushable and unhackable testbed for LLMs toward artificial general intelligence (AGI).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NPPC (Nondeterministic Polynomial-time Problem Challenge), a novel ever-scaling reasoning benchmark for Large Language Models (LLMs). NPPC addresses the issues of existing benchmarks being quickly "crushed" and easily "hacked." It proposes "ever-scalingness" as a principle for building benchmarks that are uncrushable, unhackable, auto-verifiable, and general. NPPC consists of three modules: `npgym` (a unified interface to 25 NP-complete problems with instance generation), `npsolver` (evaluation interface for online and offline models), and `npeval` (comprehensive performance analysis tools). The paper presents extensive experiments demonstrating that NPPC can reduce advanced LLMs' performance to below 10%, making it uncrushable. They also identify DeepSeek-R1, Claude-3.7-Sonnet, and 03-mini as top-performing LLMs and analyze token usage and "aha moments".

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of an ever-scaling, auto-verifiable benchmark is a significant contribution. The focus on NP-complete problems, leveraging their computational properties ("difficult to solve, easy to verify"), is clever and provides a strong foundation for generating challenging and verifiable instances. The integration of three modules (`npgym`, `npsolver`, `npeval`) into a single framework is also a positive aspect, offering a comprehensive toolset. However, the *idea* of using NP-complete problems isn't entirely new (as the authors acknowledge NPHardEval). The key novelty lies in the full system design and execution of the "ever-scalingness" principle.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: the rapid obsolescence of LLM benchmarks. NPPC's ability to effectively challenge state-of-the-art LLMs and expose their limitations is valuable. The detailed performance analysis, including token usage, "aha moments," and error types, provides insights for improving LLM reasoning capabilities. The open-source release is a positive step towards fostering further research and development. The claim of being the "first ever-scaling reasoning benchmark" is a strong statement and *requires* rigorous support and contrast with existing benchmarks, even those that partially address scaling.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined principles for benchmark construction.
    *   Sound rationale for using NP-complete problems.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse LLMs.
    *   Detailed analysis of performance, token usage, and reasoning failures.
    *   Open-source availability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Generality of NP-Complete Problems:** While many real-world problems *can* be reduced to NP-complete problems, the relevance and impact of NPPC on solving *practical* problems are not thoroughly addressed. Just because a problem *can* be expressed as an NPC problem doesn't automatically mean solving it within NPPC translates directly to better performance on the original real-world task. Further discussion and demonstration of such relationships would be beneficial.
    *   **Prompting methodology**: The reliance on json outputs may limit the LLM from leveraging their full power of language generation and therefore reasoning capabilites. It is a choice made to automate solution extraction and evaluation, which may come at a cost of measuring LLMs in their more natural language solving approach.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Tool Usage**: A brief discussion that NPC can lead to LLMs using tools for complex problems can be further explained and integrated as part of the framework. A clear direction of measuring LLM's performance with and without tools usage in the framework will highlight LLMs performance across different settings.
    *   **Scalability is not proven**: The claim of the framework being "ever-scaling" must be proven with a well-thought out scaling strategy. A static set of benchmarks will be easily memorized by LLMs over time.
    *    **Limited insight into solution quality**: NP-complete problems can have multiple valid solutions. It is unclear whether the framework measures optimal solutions or just simply correct ones.

*   **Potential Influence:** NPPC has the potential to become a valuable tool for evaluating and guiding the development of LLM reasoning capabilities. By consistently challenging LLMs and providing detailed performance analysis, it can accelerate progress towards more robust and reliable AI systems.

**Justification of Score:**

NPPC presents a significant contribution, particularly concerning the "ever-scalingness" principle and its comprehensive framework. However, the limited discussion on the real-world relevance of NP-complete problem-solving, prompts methodologies and tool usage, and proving scalability prevent a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Distillation-Supervised Convolutional Low-Rank Adaptation for Efficient Image Super-Resolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11271v1)
- **Authors**: Xinning Chai, Yao Zhang, Yuxuan Zhang, Zhengxue Cheng, Yingsheng Qin, Yucai Yang, Li Song
- **Abstract**: Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) have been widely used in efficient image super-resolution. However, for CNN-based methods, performance gains often require deeper networks and larger feature maps, which increase complexity and inference costs. Inspired by LoRA's success in fine-tuning large language models, we explore its application to lightweight models and propose Distillation-Supervised Convolutional Low-Rank Adaptation (DSCLoRA), which improves model performance without increasing architectural complexity or inference costs. Specifically, we integrate ConvLoRA into the efficient SR network SPAN by replacing the SPAB module with the proposed SConvLB module and incorporating ConvLoRA layers into both the pixel shuffle block and its preceding convolutional layer. DSCLoRA leverages low-rank decomposition for parameter updates and employs a spatial feature affinity-based knowledge distillation strategy to transfer second-order statistical information from teacher models (pre-trained SPAN) to student models (ours). This method preserves the core knowledge of lightweight models and facilitates optimal solution discovery under certain conditions. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that DSCLoRA improves PSNR and SSIM over SPAN while maintaining its efficiency and competitive image quality. Notably, DSCLoRA ranked first in the Overall Performance Track of the NTIRE 2025 Efficient Super-Resolution Challenge. Our code and models are made publicly available at https://github.com/Yaozzz666/DSCF-SR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DSCLORA (Distillation-Supervised Convolutional Low-Rank Adaptation), a method for improving the performance of efficient image super-resolution (SR) CNNs without increasing architectural complexity or inference costs. The approach integrates ConvLoRA into the SPAN network by replacing SPAB modules with SConvLB modules and incorporating ConvLoRA layers into the pixel shuffle block.  DSCLORA leverages low-rank decomposition for parameter updates and employs spatial feature affinity-based knowledge distillation to transfer knowledge from a pre-trained SPAN teacher model. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate improved PSNR and SSIM over SPAN while maintaining efficiency. The method ranked first in the NTIRE 2025 Efficient Super-Resolution Challenge's Overall Performance Track.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Efficiency:** The primary strength lies in improving performance (PSNR, SSIM) without increasing the number of parameters or computational complexity (FLOPs). This is a crucial factor in practical SR applications, particularly on resource-constrained devices.
    * **Knowledge Distillation:** The use of spatial affinity-based knowledge distillation seems effective in transferring valuable information from teacher models to the student model, leading to better performance.  The choice of spatial affinity over simpler pixel-level losses is well-justified by related works they cite.
    * **Low-Rank Adaptation:** Adapting LoRA for CNN-based SR models provides a mechanism to fine-tune existing architectures efficiently, addressing the increasing model complexity usually associated with higher performance.
    * **Strong Results:** The first-place ranking in the NTIRE 2025 Efficient Super-Resolution Challenge is a solid indicator of the method's effectiveness and competitiveness.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are relatively thorough, examining the impact of different teacher models, distillation loss functions, and LoRA configurations. This helps validate the contributions of individual components.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:**  While the combination of techniques is effective, the individual components (ConvLoRA, Knowledge Distillation, SPAN architecture) are not entirely novel. The novelty lies primarily in the specific integration and optimization for the efficient SR domain.
    * **Limited Architectural Exploration:** The paper focuses on applying DSCLORA to the SPAN architecture. It doesn't extensively explore its applicability to other efficient SR architectures. Although citing several other architectures, it mainly compares performance instead of exploring transferability.
    * **Dependence on Teacher Model:**  The performance is tied to the teacher model's quality. While the ablation studies explore different teacher models, a stronger analysis of the relationship between teacher model complexity and student model performance would be beneficial.
    * **Lack of Deeper Justification for Parameters:**  The choice of LoRA ranks (r and alpha) and distillation weights (lambda values) is largely empirical. A more theoretical justification or sensitivity analysis could strengthen the work.
    * **Limited Qualitative Examples:** While the provided qualitative results look promising, including more visual comparisons, especially on images with diverse characteristics (e.g., different textures, lighting conditions), would further support the claims.

* **Significance:**
    * The work contributes to the growing field of efficient image super-resolution.
    * The application of LoRA to lightweight CNNs for SR is a valuable exploration, offering a practical solution for resource-constrained environments.
    *  The knowledge distillation strategy, particularly the use of spatial affinity, is a beneficial addition to the SR literature.
    *  The NTIRE challenge victory adds credibility and demonstrates practical value.

**Justification for Score:**

While the individual components are not entirely new, the paper's success lies in the effective combination and optimization of these techniques specifically for the efficient image super-resolution task.  The performance gains, coupled with maintained efficiency (verified through experiments and the NTIRE challenge victory), are significant. The thorough ablation studies help validate the design choices. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the limited exploration of architectural transferability, and the empirical parameter tuning prevent a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Misleading Queries to Accurate Answers: A Three-Stage Fine-Tuning Method for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11277v1)
- **Authors**: Guocong Li, Weize Liu, Yihang Wu, Ping Wang, Shuaihan Huang, Hongxia Xu, Jian Wu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit excellent performance in natural language processing (NLP), but remain highly sensitive to the quality of input queries, especially when these queries contain misleading or inaccurate information. Existing methods focus on correcting the output, but they often overlook the potential of improving the ability of LLMs to detect and correct misleading content in the input itself. In this paper, we propose a novel three-stage fine-tuning method that enhances the ability of LLMs to detect and correct misleading information in the input, further improving response accuracy and reducing hallucinations. Specifically, the three stages include (1) training LLMs to identify misleading information, (2) training LLMs to correct the misleading information using built-in or external knowledge, and (3) training LLMs to generate accurate answers based on the corrected queries. To evaluate our method, we conducted experiments on three datasets for the hallucination detection task and the question answering (QA) task, as well as two datasets containing misleading information that we constructed. The experimental results demonstrate that our method significantly improves the accuracy and factuality of LLM responses, while also enhancing the ability to detect hallucinations and reducing the generation of hallucinations in the output, particularly when the query contains misleading information. We will publicly release our code upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**
The paper addresses the challenge of large language models (LLMs) being sensitive to misleading or inaccurate information in input queries. It proposes a novel three-stage fine-tuning method to improve LLMs' ability to detect and correct such information before generating responses. The three stages involve: (1) training LLMs to identify misleading information, (2) training them to correct the identified information using built-in or external knowledge, and (3) training them to generate accurate answers based on the corrected queries. The authors conduct experiments on hallucination detection and question answering datasets, as well as two datasets they construct containing misleading information. The experimental results suggest that their method significantly improves the accuracy and factuality of LLM responses, reduces hallucinations, and enhances the ability to detect misleading information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a critical and practical problem: the vulnerability of LLMs to misleading input.
    *   The proposed three-stage fine-tuning method is well-structured and logical. It offers a clear and potentially effective approach to mitigating the problem.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, utilizing both existing datasets and newly constructed datasets with misleading information. This allows for a robust evaluation of the proposed method.
    *   The results demonstrate significant improvements in LLM performance, particularly when dealing with misleading queries. This is a valuable contribution.
    *   The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each stage of the fine-tuning process.
    *   The case study illustrates how the method corrects misleading information and generates accurate responses.
    *   The paper includes a thorough discussion of related work and clearly positions its contribution within the field.
    *   The scalability analysis indicates the method's potential for use with various model sizes.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Knowledge:** The method heavily relies on either the LLM's internal knowledge or access to a reliable external knowledge base for the correction stage. The paper acknowledges this limitation but does not offer substantial solutions for scenarios where knowledge is incomplete or inaccurate. This is a significant constraint in real-world applications.
    *   **Dataset Construction:** While the construction of misleading datasets is a valuable effort, the method of generating them relies on the model itself (Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct). This could introduce bias or limit the diversity of misleading information. More details on how this process was carefully managed to avoid circular dependencies would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Generalizability:** Although the paper claims strong cross-task generalization capabilities, the training data is limited to QA and HaluEval-QAmis datasets. While performance improvements on other datasets are promising, more diverse training data could further enhance generalizability.
    *   **Complexity:** The method involves a complex three-stage fine-tuning process. This may be computationally expensive and require significant expertise to implement. A discussion of the practical considerations for implementing this method in resource-constrained environments would be useful.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis:** While the paper presents overall performance improvements, a more detailed error analysis could provide deeper insights into the types of misleading information the method is most effective at handling and where it still struggles. What common failure modes are observed?
    *   **Comparison to Simpler Baselines**:  The paper compares itself against a standard SFT baseline. Stronger baselines such as iterative self-correction methods or RAG approaches tuned to mitigate misleading inputs, would further highlight the advantage of the proposed approach.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the explicit focus on addressing misleading information *within the input query* itself, rather than solely focusing on output correction. While other works have explored output correction or utilized external knowledge sources, the proposed three-stage fine-tuning approach offers a structured method for enhancing LLMs' inherent ability to identify and correct flawed queries. This has the potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs, particularly in knowledge-intensive domains.

*   **Score Justification:**
The paper presents a novel approach with strong empirical results, demonstrating a valuable contribution to the field. However, the reliance on high-quality knowledge, the potential bias in dataset construction, and the complexity of the fine-tuning process limit its overall impact. Furthermore, more in-depth analysis of the method, the types of errors it avoids, and comparisons to robust baseline methods are lacking. Taking these factors into consideration, a score of **7** is assigned. This reflects the paper's significant contribution, the potential for future development, and the identified limitations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automated Python Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11290v1)
- **Authors**: Joshua Otten, Antonios Anastasopoulos, Kevin Moran
- **Abstract**: Python is one of the most commonly used programming languages in industry and education. Its English keywords and built-in functions/modules allow it to come close to pseudo-code in terms of its readability and ease of writing. However, those who do not speak English may not experience these advantages. In fact, they may even be hindered in their ability to understand Python code, as the English nature of its terms creates an additional layer of overhead. To that end, we introduce the task of automatically translating Python's natural modality (keywords, error types, identifiers, etc.) into other human languages. This presents a unique challenge, considering the abbreviated nature of these forms, as well as potential untranslatability of advanced mathematical/programming concepts across languages. We therefore create an automated pipeline to translate Python into other human languages, comparing strategies using machine translation and large language models. We then use this pipeline to acquire translations from five common Python libraries (pytorch, pandas, tensorflow, numpy, and random) in seven languages, and do a quality test on a subset of these terms in French, Greek, and Bengali. We hope this will provide a clearer path forward towards creating a universal Python, accessible to anyone regardless of nationality or language background.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper on Automated Python Translation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the task of automatically translating Python's keywords, error types, identifiers, and other natural language aspects into other human languages to make Python more accessible to non-English speakers.  It addresses the challenge of abbreviated and potentially ambiguous terms in Python and presents a pipeline involving term expansion, translation, and optional abbreviation. The authors compare various strategies using machine translation (Google Translate) and large language models (GPT-4, Llama2), testing different prompting methods. They evaluate the pipeline on five common Python libraries (pytorch, pandas, tensorflow, numpy, and random) in seven languages, including a manual quality test of a subset of terms in French, Greek, and Bengali. The paper also explores fine-tuning Llama2 to translate full code blocks.  The goal is to provide a clearer path towards a universal Python, accessible to anyone regardless of language background.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of automatically translating the natural language aspects of Python to make it more accessible is itself a solid contribution, and indeed represents a significant step toward breaking down the linguistic barriers surrounding programming languages.
*   **Significance:** Making Python more accessible could have a broader impact on computer science education and software development by allowing people from diverse linguistic backgrounds to learn and contribute more easily.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of the English-centric nature of programming languages like Python and its potential impact on accessibility.
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The authors explore a range of methods, from machine translation to large language models, and different prompting strategies to address the task.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes experimental results, evaluating different translation models and providing a basic quality test via human annotators. The detailed tables of results allow the reader to analyze the performance of various components of the pipeline.
    *   **Pipeline Development:** The development of a pipeline architecture including term expansion, translation and abbreviation scheme, which can be used to translate python terms is itself significant.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Depth in Quality Assessment:** The manual quality test, while helpful, is limited to a subset of terms and languages. A more rigorous evaluation with a larger sample size and a broader range of annotators would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Issues with LLM outputs:** The output generated by large language models did not reliably follow instructions or the required formats which is a significant issue.
    *   **Generality:** The focus is heavily on Python. While Python is a popular language, the applicability of this approach to other programming languages with different structures and conventions could be discussed further.
    *   **Dependency on Model Quality:** The accuracy of the translation relies heavily on the quality and capabilities of the underlying translation models (Google Translate, LLMs). Improvements in these models would likely directly improve the results, potentially overshadowing the contributions of this specific work.
    *   **Limited evaluation of Code Block Translation:** The analysis of code block translation is superficial, merely providing general scores without any specifics on the shortcomings of this approach.

*   **Impact and Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could stimulate further research in multilingual programming and accessibility.
    *   The pipeline and the created dataset of translations for various Python libraries may serve as a valuable resource for other researchers.
    *   The insights on the effectiveness of different translation models and prompting strategies could inform future development in this area.
    *   The method may also be used for localized code documentation.

**Rationale for the Score:**

The paper presents a well-defined problem, explores a reasonable approach, and provides empirical results. The novelty is good, and the potential impact on accessibility is significant. However, the limitations in the quality assessment, reliance on existing models, and issues with formatting and structure of generated outputs detract from the overall contribution. This leads to an overestimation of the true impact of the pipeline. Also, the finetuning of llama model, suffers from serious structural issues and is not fully evaluated.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Autoregressive Distillation of Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11295v1)
- **Authors**: Yeongmin Kim, Sotiris Anagnostidis, Yuming Du, Edgar Schönfeld, Jonas Kohler, Markos Georgopoulos, Albert Pumarola, Ali Thabet, Artsiom Sanakoyeu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models with transformer architectures have demonstrated promising capabilities in generating high-fidelity images and scalability for high resolution. However, iterative sampling process required for synthesis is very resource-intensive. A line of work has focused on distilling solutions to probability flow ODEs into few-step student models. Nevertheless, existing methods have been limited by their reliance on the most recent denoised samples as input, rendering them susceptible to exposure bias. To address this limitation, we propose AutoRegressive Distillation (ARD), a novel approach that leverages the historical trajectory of the ODE to predict future steps. ARD offers two key benefits: 1) it mitigates exposure bias by utilizing a predicted historical trajectory that is less susceptible to accumulated errors, and 2) it leverages the previous history of the ODE trajectory as a more effective source of coarse-grained information. ARD modifies the teacher transformer architecture by adding token-wise time embedding to mark each input from the trajectory history and employs a block-wise causal attention mask for training. Furthermore, incorporating historical inputs only in lower transformer layers enhances performance and efficiency. We validate the effectiveness of ARD in a class-conditioned generation on ImageNet and T2I synthesis. Our model achieves a $5\times$ reduction in FID degradation compared to the baseline methods while requiring only 1.1\% extra FLOPs on ImageNet-256. Moreover, ARD reaches FID of 1.84 on ImageNet-256 in merely 4 steps and outperforms the publicly available 1024p text-to-image distilled models in prompt adherence score with a minimal drop in FID compared to the teacher. Project page: https://github.com/alsdudrla10/ARD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Autoregressive Distillation of Diffusion Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoRegressive Distillation (ARD), a novel distillation method for diffusion transformer models. ARD aims to improve few-step distillation, addressing the exposure bias issue in existing techniques. It leverages the historical trajectory of the ODE used in diffusion models to predict future steps, instead of relying solely on the most recently denoised sample. This approach mitigates error accumulation and uses the historical information as a source of coarse-grained information. ARD modifies the teacher transformer architecture by adding token-wise time embeddings and employing a block-wise causal attention mask. The method is validated on ImageNet class-conditional generation and text-to-image synthesis, demonstrating a reduction in FID degradation compared to baselines and improved prompt adherence in text-to-image tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of using the historical trajectory in the distillation process is a significant and novel contribution. Existing methods often rely on only the most recent estimate, making them susceptible to error propagation. The specific architectural modifications (token-wise time embedding, causal attention mask, and selective application to lower transformer layers) are also well-motivated and add to the novelty.  The connection to autoregressive modeling is clear and provides a strong theoretical foundation.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work lies in its potential to make diffusion models more efficient. Reducing the number of steps required for image synthesis is crucial for practical applications. The reported performance improvements (reduced FID degradation, improved prompt adherence, efficiency) are substantial and practically relevant. The ability to distill to few steps *without* significant performance loss is a major advantage. Scaling to high-resolution text-to-image models is also a major strength.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem statement and well-motivated approach:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing few-step distillation methods (exposure bias) and presents a solid rationale for ARD.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Extensive experiments on ImageNet and text-to-image synthesis demonstrate the effectiveness of ARD. The comparisons with baselines and public models are comprehensive. The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of various design choices. The provided qualitative results align with the quantitative analysis.
    *   **Careful Architectural Design:** The architectural modifications are well-explained and justified. The analysis of attention scores provides evidence for the effectiveness of the design.
    *   **Open-source code release:** The authors released the project page with code, which supports the reproducibility of the experiments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Discussion of Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** While the paper provides some experimental details, a more detailed discussion of the sensitivity of ARD to hyperparameters would be beneficial. It is known that diffusion models are often sensitive to such choices.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alternative Architectures:** While the work focuses on transformer architectures, it would be interesting to explore the applicability of ARD to other types of diffusion models (e.g., CNN-based).
    *   **Runtime Comparison:** Comparing training time can be beneficial.
    *   **Broader Impact:** Although this paper has positive impact by increasing efficiency, negative societal impacts and potential biases of this kind of technology should be discussed.

*   **Potential Influence:** ARD has the potential to significantly impact the field of diffusion models. It provides a practical approach to accelerate inference without sacrificing quality. The autoregressive perspective may also inspire other researchers to explore new ways of improving diffusion models. Further, ARD can inspire researchers to study the use of historical data in the distillation process, particularly for time-series data.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective distillation technique that addresses a critical bottleneck in diffusion models (slow inference). The improvements are significant and the experimental results are compelling. The potential impact on the field is substantial. The weaknesses are minor and do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. Therefore, I assign a score of 8. The ARD method is innovative and it is not an incremental improvement over existing distillation methods.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Big Brother is Watching: Proactive Deepfake Detection via Learnable Hidden Face
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11309v1)
- **Authors**: Hongbo Li, Shangchao Yang, Ruiyang Xia, Lin Yuan, Xinbo Gao
- **Abstract**: As deepfake technologies continue to advance, passive detection methods struggle to generalize with various forgery manipulations and datasets. Proactive defense techniques have been actively studied with the primary aim of preventing deepfake operation effectively working. In this paper, we aim to bridge the gap between passive detection and proactive defense, and seek to solve the detection problem utilizing a proactive methodology. Inspired by several watermarking-based forensic methods, we explore a novel detection framework based on the concept of ``hiding a learnable face within a face''. Specifically, relying on a semi-fragile invertible steganography network, a secret template image is embedded into a host image imperceptibly, acting as an indicator monitoring for any malicious image forgery when being restored by the inverse steganography process. Instead of being manually specified, the secret template is optimized during training to resemble a neutral facial appearance, just like a ``big brother'' hidden in the image to be protected. By incorporating a self-blending mechanism and robustness learning strategy with a simulative transmission channel, a robust detector is built to accurately distinguish if the steganographic image is maliciously tampered or benignly processed. Finally, extensive experiments conducted on multiple datasets demonstrate the superiority of the proposed approach over competing passive and proactive detection methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Big Brother is Watching: Proactive Deepfake Detection via Learnable Hidden Face."

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel proactive deepfake detection method that bridges the gap between passive detection and proactive defense. The core idea is to embed a "learnable face" into a host face image using an invertible steganography network. This hidden face acts as an "inspector," monitoring for any tampering or forgery. The steganography network is designed to be semi-fragile; it should be resistant to benign image processing operations (like JPEG compression) but sensitive to malicious manipulations (deepfakes). The embedded face template is not manually specified but is learned during training to resemble a generic human face, maximizing imperceptibility. The detector determines authenticity by analyzing the difference between the original and restored hidden face. The paper includes a training strategy involving a simulated transmission channel with both benign and malicious manipulations, and multi-task loss functions for optimizing both detection accuracy and face template renewal. Extensive experiments are conducted to demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a fresh perspective on proactive deepfake detection. The concept of "hiding a learnable face within a face" is interesting and reasonably novel. It departs from traditional watermarking approaches by learning the template instead of using a predefined one. This adaptive nature of the hidden template allows the method to be tuned towards specific face image distributions and manipulations which is a useful advancement. The approach further integrates ideas from semi-fragile watermarking to offer robustness against benign manipulations, increasing the practical applicability of the method.

*   **Significance:** Deepfake detection is a very significant and active area of research. Proactive methods are crucial to address the shortcomings of passive techniques, and this paper addresses a key limitation of those proactive detection methods which struggle to create the balance between robustness and imperceptibility. Also, passive methods are more accurate in general because they don't distort the images to begin with. That makes the novelty even more useful in terms of potential benefit in the near future. The performance demonstrated on diverse datasets, coupled with a design aimed at addressing robustness and imperceptibility could be impactful to proactive strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Innovative concept:** Hiding a learnable face is an inspired approach to monitoring image integrity.
    *   **Adaptive template learning:** The learned template approach is more robust and adaptive than using a fixed watermark.
    *   **Semi-fragile steganography:** The steganography network is designed to be robust to common image processing but sensitive to malicious manipulations.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper includes extensive experiments, showing strong performance against diverse datasets and forgery methods.
    *   **Ablation studies:** The ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of each component in the proposed framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The system has several complex components. More explanation could be given to explain the specific effects of each component.
    *   **Scalability concerns:** The current architecture relies on a relatively complex architecture. While the authors mention reducing size of the steganographic model, it's still a concern. The practical applicability and suitability of this method for real-time applications needs to be considered more thoroughly.
    *   **Reliance on specific forgery methods in training**: While the self-blending mechanism is meant to generalize, the training process heavily involves that technique. A broader set of forgery simulation techniques or some theoretical justification for this choice would have strengthened the generality argument.
    *   **Vulnerability to adaptive attacks:** As with any proactive defense mechanism, this method might be vulnerable to adaptive attacks where an attacker learns the characteristics of the hidden face and crafts deepfakes that specifically avoid triggering the detector. The authors should acknowledge this limitation and discuss potential mitigation strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:** This research could influence the development of more adaptive and robust proactive deepfake detection methods. The learnable hidden face concept and semi-fragile steganography can inspire new approaches to image integrity verification.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates clear novelty in concept and implementation, and is an important contribution for proactive defence strategies against deepfakes. The concept of creating a semi-fragile signal that blends and adapts to source images is particularly useful, and demonstrates well a combination of machine learning techniques. However, the relative complexity of the design, reliance on a particular forgery method, and lack of a scalability plan holds it back. It could definitely be useful in the near future, so a mid-high score is warranted.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Optimizing LLM Inference: Fluid-Guided Online Scheduling with Memory Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11320v1)
- **Authors**: Ruicheng Ao, Gan Luo, David Simchi-Levi, Xinshang Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are indispensable in today's applications, but their inference procedure -- generating responses by processing text in segments and using a memory-heavy Key-Value (KV) cache -- demands significant computational resources, particularly under memory constraints. This paper formulates LLM inference optimization as a multi-stage online scheduling problem where sequential prompt arrivals and KV cache growth render conventional scheduling ineffective. We develop a fluid dynamics approximation to provide a tractable benchmark that guides algorithm design. Building on this, we propose the Waiting for Accumulated Inference Threshold (WAIT) algorithm, which uses multiple thresholds to schedule incoming prompts optimally when output lengths are known, and extend it to Nested WAIT for cases with unknown output lengths. Theoretical analysis shows that both algorithms achieve near-optimal performance against the fluid benchmark in heavy traffic conditions, balancing throughput, latency, and Time to First Token (TTFT). Experiments with the Llama-7B model on an A100 GPU using both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate improved throughput and reduced latency relative to established baselines like vLLM and Sarathi. This work bridges operations research and machine learning, offering a rigorous framework for the efficient deployment of LLMs under memory constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of optimizing inference scheduling for Large Language Models (LLMs) under memory constraints. It frames LLM inference as a multi-stage online scheduling problem with dynamically increasing memory demands due to the Key-Value (KV) cache. The authors introduce a fluid dynamics approximation to establish a tractable benchmark for algorithm design. They then propose two algorithms: WAIT (Waiting for Accumulated Inference Threshold) for scenarios with known output lengths, and Nested WAIT for scenarios with unknown output lengths. Both algorithms aim for near-optimal performance compared to the fluid benchmark under heavy traffic, balancing throughput, latency, and Time to First Token (TTFT). The paper provides theoretical analysis and numerical experiments to validate the algorithms' effectiveness, comparing them to existing approaches like vLLM and Sarathi. Finally the work discuss about extensions for time-varying arrival rates and different segment designs, showing that the developed solutions maintain comparably theoretical guarantees.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel elements:

    *   **Fluid Dynamics Model:**  The use of fluid dynamics to model LLM inference scheduling is a significant contribution. This provides a tractable analytical framework for a complex problem. This approximation could, however, be more rigorous when the LLMs become larger.
    *   **WAIT and Nested WAIT Algorithms:**  These algorithms represent a practical approach to LLM inference scheduling, particularly the Nested WAIT algorithm's handling of unknown output lengths. However, the algorithms hinge on pre-defined thresholds, and the derivation and selection of optimal thresholds are not completely clear.
    *   **Heavy Traffic Analysis:**  The analysis of performance under heavy traffic offers useful theoretical guarantees about the algorithm's scalability.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of efficient LLM inference is highly significant due to the computational demands and increasing deployment of LLMs. This paper contributes by:

    *   **Providing a Theoretical Foundation:** Unlike many system-level optimization efforts, this paper offers a theoretically grounded approach to LLM scheduling.
    *   **Addressing Memory Constraints:** Explicitly considering memory limitations is crucial for practical deployment on hardware with finite resources.
    *   **Demonstrating Practical Improvements:** The numerical experiments show that the proposed algorithms outperform existing inference engines (vLLM, Sarathi) in terms of throughput and latency.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of LLM inference scheduling, highlighting the challenges of dynamic memory constraints and prompt heterogeneity.
    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides formal definitions, propositions, and theorems to support its claims. The heavy traffic analysis offers guarantees about asymptotic optimality.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes both synthetic and real-world experiments to evaluate the algorithms' performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Threshold Derivation and Tuning:** The paper explains how thresholds are derived but is not clear about the threshold's computation that gives best theoretical results and the effect of the thresholds on the LLM behaviour under real settings.
    *   **Limitations of Heavy Traffic Analysis:** While heavy traffic analysis provides useful insights, its relevance to scenarios with low or moderate traffic may be limited.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The experiments are conducted on a single GPU (A100) and with the Llama-7B model. Further validation on other models, hardware configurations, and datasets would strengthen the results.
    *   **Model Simplifications:** The batch processing cost model (Equation 1) is a linear approximation.  While justified by experiments, it could be refined for increased accuracy.
    *   **Limited baselines:** In the experiment section, it could be better to include additional baseline results such as FCFS policy.

*   **Impact:**

    *   The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM inference scheduling, providing a theoretical benchmark and practical algorithms for optimization.
    *   The fluid dynamics modeling approach could be adopted in other areas of LLM optimization.
    *   The algorithms could be incorporated into existing LLM inference engines to improve performance.

*   **Concerns:**

    *   The paper focuses primarily on maximizing throughput and reducing latency. It does not address fairness or quality of generation in any detail. Addressing these aspects is important for real-world applications.
    *   The results demonstrate an improvement in throughput, but a detailed analysis of the overheads due to the algorithm is missing. How much time does the algorithm spend choosing the right batch?

**Justification:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the field, especially by providing a more theoretically grounded approach than many system-level works. While the algorithms may require further refinement and the heavy traffic analysis has limitations, the work presents a clear framework for LLM inference scheduling.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Minimalist Approach to LLM Reasoning: from Rejection Sampling to Reinforce
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11343v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Xiong, Jiarui Yao, Yuhui Xu, Bo Pang, Lei Wang, Doyen Sahoo, Junnan Li, Nan Jiang, Tong Zhang, Caiming Xiong, Hanze Dong
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a prevailing approach for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks. Among recent methods, GRPO stands out for its empirical success in training models such as DeepSeek-R1, yet the sources of its effectiveness remain poorly understood. In this work, we revisit GRPO from a reinforce-like algorithm perspective and analyze its core components. Surprisingly, we find that a simple rejection sampling baseline, RAFT, which trains only on positively rewarded samples, yields competitive performance than GRPO and PPO. Our ablation studies reveal that GRPO's main advantage arises from discarding prompts with entirely incorrect responses, rather than from its reward normalization. Motivated by this insight, we propose Reinforce-Rej, a minimal extension of policy gradient that filters both entirely incorrect and entirely correct samples. Reinforce-Rej improves KL efficiency and stability, serving as a lightweight yet effective alternative to more complex RL algorithms. We advocate RAFT as a robust and interpretable baseline, and suggest that future advances should focus on more principled designs for incorporating negative samples, rather than relying on them indiscriminately. Our findings provide guidance for future work in reward-based LLM post-training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "A Minimalist Approach to LLM Reasoning: from Rejection Sampling to Reinforce":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates reinforcement learning (RL) methods for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on mathematical reasoning tasks. It revisits and analyzes the GRPO algorithm, known for its success in training models like DeepSeek-R1, and compares it with simpler approaches like RAFT (rejection sampling).  The authors surprisingly find that RAFT achieves competitive or even better early-stage performance compared to GRPO and PPO.  They demonstrate that GRPO's advantage primarily stems from filtering out prompts with entirely incorrect responses, rather than complex reward normalization techniques. Based on this, the paper proposes Reinforce-Rej, a minimal extension of policy gradient that filters both entirely incorrect and entirely correct samples, improving KL efficiency and stability.  The authors advocate for RAFT as a robust baseline and suggest future research focus on principled negative sample incorporation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Clarity and Focus:** The paper is well-written and clearly articulates its research question, methodology, and findings. It effectively breaks down complex algorithms and simplifies them for analysis.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough empirical evaluation with controlled ablation studies, isolating the effects of different components within RL algorithms. This allows for a nuanced understanding of their contribution to performance. The use of multiple models (Qwen, LLaMA) and benchmarks strengthens the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Counter-Intuitive Findings:** The paper challenges the prevailing belief that complex RL algorithms with sophisticated components are always superior to simpler methods like rejection sampling. The finding that RAFT achieves competitive performance is surprising and valuable.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper's findings have practical implications for LLM fine-tuning. The identification of RAFT as a strong baseline and the effectiveness of Reinforce-Rej provide simpler and more efficient alternatives to complex RL algorithms.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The paper offers valuable insights into the role of negative samples in RL for LLMs, highlighting the potential harm of indiscriminate inclusion of low-quality or misleading data. The analysis of policy entropy and KL divergence provides a deeper understanding of the learning dynamics of different algorithms.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Core Algorithms:** The core algorithms (RAFT, Reinforce, GRPO, DPO) are not novel in themselves. The paper's novelty lies in the comparative analysis and ablation studies in the *context* of LLM fine-tuning for reasoning.
*   **Reasoning for Filtering Correct Samples in Reinforce-Rej:** The rationale for removing *correct* samples in Reinforce-Rej, while supported by the experimental results, could be explained more theoretically. What is the reason that removing *correct* samples help? This is not explored enough in the paper.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper could have discussed the sensitivity of RAFT and Reinforce-Rej to hyperparameters, such as the number of responses generated per prompt and the temperature parameter. A better analysis of sensitivity and robustness could be helpful.
*   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses exclusively on mathematical reasoning tasks. It would be beneficial to investigate whether the findings generalize to other complex reasoning tasks or different types of LLM applications.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by:

*   **Demystifying GRPO:** It provides a more interpretable understanding of GRPO's effectiveness, highlighting the importance of data filtering over complex algorithmic components.
*   **Promoting Simplicity:** It advocates for simpler and more efficient RL algorithms for LLM fine-tuning, which can lower the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners.
*   **Guiding Future Research:** It suggests a new direction for research, focusing on principled methods for incorporating sample quality, rather than indiscriminately using negative feedback.
*   **Providing Practical Baselines:** RAFT and Reinforce-Rej offer strong and easily implementable baselines for reward-based LLM fine-tuning.

**Overall:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of LLM fine-tuning. It presents compelling empirical evidence and insightful analysis that challenges conventional wisdom and offers practical guidance for future research. While the core algorithms are not novel, the paper's comparative analysis, ablation studies, and counter-intuitive findings make it a significant contribution.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper is well-executed, provides valuable insights, and has practical implications. While it lacks groundbreaking algorithmic novelty and could explore some aspects further, it successfully challenges the state-of-the-art on LLM fine-tuning. The finding that a simple RAFT model is on par with complex algorithms is crucial and will have widespread impact on future works in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Seedream 3.0 Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11346v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Gao, Lixue Gong, Qiushan Guo, Xiaoxia Hou, Zhichao Lai, Fanshi Li, Liang Li, Xiaochen Lian, Chao Liao, Liyang Liu, Wei Liu, Yichun Shi, Shiqi Sun, Yu Tian, Zhi Tian, Peng Wang, Rui Wang, Xuanda Wang, Xun Wang, Ye Wang, Guofeng Wu, Jie Wu, Xin Xia, Xuefeng Xiao, Zhonghua Zhai, Xinyu Zhang, Qi Zhang, Yuwei Zhang, Shijia Zhao, Jianchao Yang, Weilin Huang
- **Abstract**: We present Seedream 3.0, a high-performance Chinese-English bilingual image generation foundation model. We develop several technical improvements to address existing challenges in Seedream 2.0, including alignment with complicated prompts, fine-grained typography generation, suboptimal visual aesthetics and fidelity, and limited image resolutions. Specifically, the advancements of Seedream 3.0 stem from improvements across the entire pipeline, from data construction to model deployment. At the data stratum, we double the dataset using a defect-aware training paradigm and a dual-axis collaborative data-sampling framework. Furthermore, we adopt several effective techniques such as mixed-resolution training, cross-modality RoPE, representation alignment loss, and resolution-aware timestep sampling in the pre-training phase. During the post-training stage, we utilize diversified aesthetic captions in SFT, and a VLM-based reward model with scaling, thereby achieving outputs that well align with human preferences. Furthermore, Seedream 3.0 pioneers a novel acceleration paradigm. By employing consistent noise expectation and importance-aware timestep sampling, we achieve a 4 to 8 times speedup while maintaining image quality. Seedream 3.0 demonstrates significant improvements over Seedream 2.0: it enhances overall capabilities, in particular for text-rendering in complicated Chinese characters which is important to professional typography generation. In addition, it provides native high-resolution output (up to 2K), allowing it to generate images with high visual quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Seedream 3.0 technical report:

**Summary:**

The Seedream 3.0 technical report introduces a high-performance Chinese-English bilingual text-to-image foundation model. It addresses limitations of Seedream 2.0 related to prompt alignment, typography, visual aesthetics, and resolution. The core improvements involve enhancing the training dataset using a defect-aware paradigm and a dual-axis data sampling framework, incorporating mixed-resolution training, cross-modality RoPE, representation alignment loss, and resolution-aware timestep sampling. Post-training improvements include diverse aesthetic captions in SFT and a VLM-based reward model.  A novel acceleration paradigm reduces inference time significantly.  The report claims substantial improvements in text rendering (especially for complex Chinese characters), high-resolution output, and overall image quality.  It also mentions integration into Doubao and Jimeng platforms and superior performance in benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Improvement:** The paper details improvements across the entire pipeline, from data construction to model acceleration. This is a significant undertaking and suggests a holistic approach to tackling the challenges of text-to-image generation.
    *   **Focus on Practical Issues:** Addressing specific issues like fine-grained typography, especially in Chinese, and visual aesthetics demonstrates a practical focus beyond just achieving high scores on general benchmarks.  The focus on higher resolutions is also a practical benefit.
    *   **Detailed Technical Explanations:** The paper provides reasonable detail on the technical improvements (mixed-resolution training, cross-modality RoPE, data sampling). This allows for reproducibility and future research based on these techniques.
    *   **Speedup:** The reported speedup through consistent noise expectation and timestep sampling is a significant practical advantage, making the model more usable.
    *   **Benchmark Success:**  Achieving first place on the Artificial Analysis Text to Image Model Leaderboard is a strong indicator of overall performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Core Architecture:** The paper inherits the core architecture from Seedream 2.0.  While it claims increases in parameters and several improvements, the fundamental model structure remains the same. The novelty primarily lies in the training techniques and data handling.
    *   **Vague Descriptions:** Some descriptions are vague. For example, while "defect-aware training" and "dual-axis collaborative data sampling" are mentioned, more specifics on their implementation and impact are needed for a fully rigorous assessment.
    *   **Benchmarking Concerns:** While ranking highly on the Artificial Analysis Arena is good, these leaderboards are often subject to gaming and may not fully reflect real-world performance in all scenarios.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** The paper mentions several techniques, but there's a lack of detailed ablation studies demonstrating the individual contribution of each improvement.  Without these, it's difficult to assess which techniques are truly critical and which provide marginal gains.
    *   **Subjective Evaluation:** The reliance on human evaluations, while necessary, introduces subjectivity.  More detail on the evaluation protocol and inter-rater agreement would strengthen these claims.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** The comparison against GPT-4o only addresses a small subset of capabilities, mainly editing and text rendering. The broad generation quality comparison is subjective without concrete metrics.
    *   **Overselling:** The conclusion tends to oversell, characterizing Seedream 3.0's effect on the field of image generation. The innovations are significant, but not groundbreaking enough for "revolutionizing" or "reshaping" the field.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    *   The work improves the state-of-the-art in text-to-image generation, particularly for Chinese and potentially for applications requiring high-resolution output.
    *   The novel training techniques (data sampling, cross-modality RoPE, etc.) could be adopted by other researchers.
    *   The acceleration paradigm is a valuable contribution to making diffusion models more practical.

**Justification for Score:**

While Seedream 3.0 shows significant improvements over its predecessor and performs well on benchmarks, the core architecture isn't fundamentally novel. The primary contributions lie in the training techniques, data handling, and model acceleration. While these are valuable and practical, the lack of ablation studies to isolate the impact of individual techniques, the potential for bias in subjective evaluations, and the overly enthusiastic claims temper the overall assessment. Also, while focusing on Chinese text is commendable, the impact on a general audience is relatively small. Therefore, a score of 7 seems appropriate.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Teaching Large Language Models to Reason through Learning and Forgetting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11364v1)
- **Authors**: Tianwei Ni, Allen Nie, Sapana Chaudhary, Yao Liu, Huzefa Rangwala, Rasool Fakoor
- **Abstract**: Leveraging inference-time search in large language models has proven effective in further enhancing a trained model's capability to solve complex mathematical and reasoning problems. However, this approach significantly increases computational costs and inference time, as the model must generate and evaluate multiple candidate solutions to identify a viable reasoning path. To address this, we propose an effective approach that integrates search capabilities directly into the model by fine-tuning it using both successful (learning) and failed reasoning paths (forgetting) derived from diverse search methods. While fine-tuning the model with these data might seem straightforward, we identify a critical issue: the model's search capability tends to degrade rapidly if fine-tuning is performed naively. We show that this degradation can be substantially mitigated by employing a smaller learning rate. Extensive experiments on the challenging Game-of-24 and Countdown mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that our approach not only outperforms both standard fine-tuning and inference-time search baselines but also significantly reduces inference time by 180$\times$.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces a method to improve the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) for mathematical problems by fine-tuning them on both successful and failed reasoning paths generated by diverse search algorithms. The approach, called unlikelihood fine-tuning (UFT), aims to directly integrate search capabilities into the model by leveraging supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on correct paths and unlikelihood loss on incorrect ones. The paper identifies that naive fine-tuning degrades search capabilities and proposes using a smaller learning rate to mitigate this issue. The method is evaluated on the Game-of-24 and Countdown mathematical reasoning benchmarks, showing improved performance over both standard fine-tuning and inference-time search baselines, along with a reduction in inference time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** While the idea of using both positive and negative examples for fine-tuning LLMs is not entirely new, the specific application to integrate diverse search strategies and the explicit focus on mitigating the degradation of search capability during fine-tuning are novel aspects. The method of using unlikelihood loss to "forget" incorrect reasoning paths is a reasonable approach. However, the novelty is incremental rather than a significant paradigm shift. Prior work already explores distillation and reinforcement learning techniques for improving LLM reasoning, this work builds upon that existing body of knowledge.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem: the high computational cost of inference-time search in LLMs. Reducing this cost while maintaining or improving performance is a valuable contribution. The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements on established benchmarks, and the analysis provides insights into the importance of data quality, negative examples, and controlled learning rates. The identified trade-off between CoT reasoning performance and search capability raises important questions for further research. The 180x reduction in inference time is a significant achievement and could potentially enable broader adoption of LLMs for complex reasoning tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of high inference costs associated with search-based reasoning in LLMs.
*   **Simple and Effective Approach:** UFT is a relatively simple method to implement, making it potentially attractive to practitioners.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements on two challenging benchmarks. The gains in success rate and inference time reduction are significant.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The paper provides insights into the factors that affect the performance of UFT, such as data quality and learning rate.
*   **Open Source:** The authors build upon existing open source LLM libraries, which strengthens adoption and collaboration potential.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The approach combines existing techniques (SFT, unlikelihood loss) in a novel way, but the individual components are not groundbreaking.
*   **Limited Generalizability Evidence:** The experiments are focused on mathematical reasoning tasks. It is unclear how well UFT would generalize to other types of reasoning problems or tasks.
*   **Dependence on Ground-Truth Verifiers:** The method relies on the availability of accurate rule-based verifiers for labeling successful and failed reasoning paths. This may limit its applicability to domains where such verifiers are not readily available. The use of verifiers limits the ability to perform zero shot adaptation to unseen tasks.
*   **Limited exploration of hyperparameter optimisation: ** The effect of a wide range of learning rates is explored. This indicates the sensitivity of the method to carefully selecting the learning rate for the problem at hand. The selection of learning rate based only average success rate is not robust. Validation based on inference time would further strengthen the claims of the paper.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore the use of negative examples for fine-tuning LLMs and by highlighting the importance of carefully controlling the learning rate to avoid degrading existing capabilities. The observed reduction in inference time could have practical implications for the deployment of LLMs in resource-constrained environments.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM reasoning. While the novelty of the individual components is limited, the way they are combined and the insights derived from the experiments are valuable. The clear problem definition, empirical results, and practical implications justify a score in the upper-mid range. However, the limited generalizability evidence, reliance on ground-truth verifiers, and dependence on parameter tuning prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### OpenTuringBench: An Open-Model-based Benchmark and Framework for Machine-Generated Text Detection and Attribution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11369v1)
- **Authors**: Lucio La Cava, Andrea Tagarelli
- **Abstract**: Open Large Language Models (OLLMs) are increasingly leveraged in generative AI applications, posing new challenges for detecting their outputs. We propose OpenTuringBench, a new benchmark based on OLLMs, designed to train and evaluate machine-generated text detectors on the Turing Test and Authorship Attribution problems. OpenTuringBench focuses on a representative set of OLLMs, and features a number of challenging evaluation tasks, including human/machine-manipulated texts, out-of-domain texts, and texts from previously unseen models. We also provide OTBDetector, a contrastive learning framework to detect and attribute OLLM-based machine-generated texts. Results highlight the relevance and varying degrees of difficulty of the OpenTuringBench tasks, with our detector achieving remarkable capabilities across the various tasks and outperforming most existing detectors. Resources are available on the OpenTuringBench Hugging Face repository at https://huggingface.co/datasets/MLNTeam-Unical/OpenTuringBench
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OpenTuringBench, a new benchmark and framework for machine-generated text (MGT) detection and authorship attribution, specifically designed for open large language models (OLLMs). The benchmark features a large dataset (>500K texts) built from news articles generated by a diverse set of recent OLLMs. It includes challenging evaluation tasks such as detecting human/machine-manipulated text, out-of-domain texts, and outputs from previously unseen models. The authors also propose OTBDetector, a contrastive learning framework for detecting and attributing OLLM-generated text, and demonstrate its effectiveness in the conducted experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by addressing the growing need for benchmarks tailored to the rapidly evolving landscape of open large language models. The focus on OLLMs is timely and relevant, considering their increasing accessibility and potential for misuse. While some existing benchmarks exist for MGT detection, OpenTuringBench differentiates itself through its broader coverage of recent OLLMs, challenging tasks like authorship attribution under various conditions, and its larger size. The introduction of OTBDetector, while building upon existing contrastive learning approaches, is tailored to the specific challenges of the benchmark.  However, the novelty could be strengthened by exploring different domains beyond news articles.

**Significance:** The work is significant because it provides a much-needed resource for developing and evaluating MGT detection tools in the context of OLLMs. The inclusion of challenging tasks like detecting manipulated text and out-of-domain samples is particularly important for addressing real-world scenarios where attackers may try to evade detection.
