The release of both the benchmark and the OTBDetector framework facilitates further research and development in this area.
The study's detailed analysis of different MGT detection methods provides valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses, guiding future research directions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant Focus:** Addressing the challenges of MGT detection in the context of OLLMs is a timely and important contribution.
*   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** OpenTuringBench offers a large-scale and diverse dataset with challenging evaluation tasks.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a thorough analysis of different MGT detection methods, revealing their strengths and weaknesses.
*   **Framework Availability:** Releasing both the benchmark and the OTBDetector framework promotes further research and development.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** Acknowledgement of ethical impacts relating to MGT.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Domain Limitation:** The benchmark primarily focuses on news articles. Expanding to other domains (e.g., creative writing, social media) would increase its generalizability.
*   **OLLM Family Bias:** Even though the number of different models is large, they can be biased to one popular architecture, so future work needs to consider all architectures to avoid the bias.
*   **Continual Learning Concerns:** The detector must be fully retrained when new models are to be detected.

**Potential Influence:**

OpenTuringBench is likely to become a widely used resource in the MGT detection community, facilitating the development of more robust and effective detection tools. The paper's findings will guide future research directions and inform the development of policies and guidelines for responsible AI usage. However, because OpenTuringBench uses a limited set of OLLM families, the results on these models cannot necessarily be translated to all models, limiting the actual significance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to a relevant and rapidly evolving field. The strengths of the benchmark (size, diversity, challenging tasks) and the accompanying framework outweigh the weaknesses (domain limitation, the focus on a specific set of LLMs).  The significance stems from addressing a very important problem.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cancer-Myth: Evaluating AI Chatbot on Patient Questions with False Presuppositions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11373v1)
- **Authors**: Wang Bill Zhu, Tianqi Chen, Ching Ying Lin, Jade Law, Mazen Jizzini, Jorge J. Nieva, Ruishan Liu, Robin Jia
- **Abstract**: Cancer patients are increasingly turning to large language models (LLMs) as a new form of internet search for medical information, making it critical to assess how well these models handle complex, personalized questions. However, current medical benchmarks focus on medical exams or consumer-searched questions and do not evaluate LLMs on real patient questions with detailed clinical contexts. In this paper, we first evaluate LLMs on cancer-related questions drawn from real patients, reviewed by three hematology oncology physicians. While responses are generally accurate, with GPT-4-Turbo scoring 4.13 out of 5, the models frequently fail to recognize or address false presuppositions in the questions-posing risks to safe medical decision-making. To study this limitation systematically, we introduce Cancer-Myth, an expert-verified adversarial dataset of 585 cancer-related questions with false presuppositions. On this benchmark, no frontier LLM -- including GPT-4o, Gemini-1.Pro, and Claude-3.5-Sonnet -- corrects these false presuppositions more than 30% of the time. Even advanced medical agentic methods do not prevent LLMs from ignoring false presuppositions. These findings expose a critical gap in the clinical reliability of LLMs and underscore the need for more robust safeguards in medical AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Cancer-Myth: Evaluating Large Language Models on Patient Questions with False Presuppositions" addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLMs for real-world medical applications. It highlights that while current medical benchmarks focus on exams or general consumer queries, they fail to assess LLMs' ability to handle complex, personalized patient questions that often contain *false presuppositions* – incorrect beliefs that patients assume are shared knowledge.  The authors found that LLMs, despite generally providing accurate information, frequently fail to recognize and address these misconceptions, potentially leading to unsafe medical decisions. They introduce "Cancer-Myth," an expert-verified adversarial dataset of cancer-related questions with embedded false presuppositions, and demonstrate that even the most advanced LLMs struggle to correct these misconceptions. They also test medical agentic methods, finding them ineffective in addressing false presuppositions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in identifying a specific and highly relevant weakness in LLMs' performance in a critical domain (medical advice). Previous research has focused on accuracy in answering medical questions, but this work shifts the focus to a higher-level cognitive task: identifying and correcting *misinformation presented within a question*. The construction of the Cancer-Myth dataset is also a novel contribution, providing a valuable resource for future research. The identification and definition of categories of false assumptions in the patient inquiries is another point of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential impact on the safe and reliable deployment of LLMs in healthcare.  The finding that LLMs routinely fail to address false presuppositions raises serious concerns about their use as medical advisors, potentially leading to patients delaying or forgoing appropriate treatment. The work is particularly relevant in the context of increasing reliance on LLMs for medical information and the limited medical resources available for severe diseases. This is a significant safety concern that must be addressed before LLMs can be considered safe in healthcare.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of false presuppositions in patient questions and its relevance to LLM safety.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors use a combination of qualitative analysis (physician reviews of LLM responses) and quantitative evaluation (using the Cancer-Myth dataset).
    *   **Expert Validation:**  The involvement of hematology oncology physicians in reviewing questions and evaluating responses adds credibility to the findings.
    *   **Dataset Creation:** The Cancer-Myth dataset represents a valuable resource for future research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The dataset focuses specifically on cancer-related questions. While this allows for in-depth analysis, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other medical domains.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation relies on LLM-based verification, which, while validated against human annotators, is still susceptible to biases and errors. While this does not present a fundamental flaw, it could cause small inaccuracies in the assessment.
    *   **Limited Solutions:** The paper effectively highlights the problem but doesn't offer concrete solutions for mitigating it. This naturally sets the stage for future work but leaves the reader without immediate remedies.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in several ways:

    *   **Spurring further research to improve the LLMs safety in medical advice**.
    *   **Encouraging the development of new benchmarks:**  It will motivate the creation of datasets and evaluation metrics that specifically target the ability of LLMs to handle misinformation and subtle errors in user input.
    *   **Guiding the design of more robust LLMs:**  It will prompt researchers to develop techniques for improving LLMs' ability to recognize and correct false presuppositions, potentially incorporating mechanisms for knowledge verification and clarification of user intent.
    *   **Shaping guidelines for LLM deployment in healthcare:**  The findings could inform the development of guidelines and best practices for using LLMs in medical settings, emphasizing the need for human oversight and caution when dealing with complex patient questions.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying a critical safety gap in LLMs' performance in healthcare and providing a valuable resource (Cancer-Myth dataset) for future research. While the study is somewhat limited in scope (cancer-specific) and doesn't offer immediate solutions, the clearly defined problem and rigorous methodology makes it a valuable contribution. The paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in medical AI, leading to more robust and reliable LLMs for healthcare applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Omni$^2$: Unifying Omnidirectional Image Generation and Editing in an Omni Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11379v1)
- **Authors**: Liu Yang, Huiyu Duan, Yucheng Zhu, Xiaohong Liu, Lu Liu, Zitong Xu, Guangji Ma, Xiongkuo Min, Guangtao Zhai, Patrick Le Callet
- **Abstract**: $360^{\circ}$ omnidirectional images (ODIs) have gained considerable attention recently, and are widely used in various virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR) applications. However, capturing such images is expensive and requires specialized equipment, making ODI synthesis increasingly important. While common 2D image generation and editing methods are rapidly advancing, these models struggle to deliver satisfactory results when generating or editing ODIs due to the unique format and broad 360$^{\circ}$ Field-of-View (FoV) of ODIs. To bridge this gap, we construct \textbf{\textit{Any2Omni}}, the first comprehensive ODI generation-editing dataset comprises 60,000+ training data covering diverse input conditions and up to 9 ODI generation and editing tasks. Built upon Any2Omni, we propose an \textbf{\underline{Omni}} model for \textbf{\underline{Omni}}-directional image generation and editing (\textbf{\textit{Omni$^2$}}), with the capability of handling various ODI generation and editing tasks under diverse input conditions using one model. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority and effectiveness of the proposed Omni$^2$ model for both the ODI generation and editing tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Omni², a novel omnidirectional image (ODI) generation and editing model. It also presents Any2Omni, a large-scale dataset comprising over 60,000 training samples, covering various ODI generation and editing tasks with multiple input modalities. Omni² leverages a transformer-based architecture with a viewport-based bidirectional attention mechanism to maintain consistency across different views. The model aims to unify ODI generation and editing tasks within a single framework, handling various input conditions. The authors conduct extensive experiments that demonstrate Omni²'s superior performance in generating and editing ODIs compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Contribution (Any2Omni):** Creating a large, comprehensive dataset like Any2Omni is a significant contribution. The scarcity of such datasets in the ODI space limits research progress. Having a dataset covering multiple tasks (text-to-image, inpainting, outpainting, editing) and input modalities is a considerable asset to the community. The authors' detailed description of the data generation pipeline enhances the value.
    *   **Unified Approach (Omni²):** The idea of unifying ODI generation and editing within a single model is compelling. It simplifies the process and allows for potential knowledge transfer across tasks. Demonstrating the ability to handle diverse input conditions (text, images, depth maps) is a significant advantage.
    *   **Viewport-Based Bidirectional Attention:** The proposed attention mechanism is a novel way to achieve view consistency in ODIs. Existing methods often rely on additional modules or external attention, while Omni² modifies the Transformer architecture itself. The described benefits in terms of simplicity and holistic control are worth noting.
    *   **Performance:** The results demonstrate improvements over existing methods in various ODI generation tasks, indicated by both quantitative metrics (FID, IS, CS) and qualitative evaluations. The editing results seem plausible, given that there is not existing method for comparing. The runtime performance is also good.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty (Model):** While the viewport-based bidirectional attention is a contribution, the core architecture appears to be built on existing components (VAE from SDXL, Transformer from Phi-3). The incremental nature of the architecture could be seen as a limitation.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Editing Tasks:** While the paper introduces ODI editing tasks, the evaluation is limited. Comparing against the application of existing 2D methods on ODIs is a reasonable baseline, but it doesn't strongly highlight the benefits of a true ODI-aware editing approach. A more comprehensive comparison with existing 2D methods is necessary.
    *   **Over claim in the title:** Claiming to *unify* omnidirectional image generation and editing tasks might be overly broad. Given the specific focus on a defined set of generation and editing tasks, a more precise and scoped claim would improve the paper's credibility.
    *   **Limited novelty beyond the dataset:** Many the improvements in the results come from training with a custom dataset that has much better captions. It will be interesting if training the baseline model on the dataset will lead to a similar performance.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The dataset has high potential to drive innovation in the ODI generation and editing domains.
    *   The approach of viewport-based attention can inspire similar approaches in other 360 domains such as video.
    *   The experimental results provide some interesting comparisons that help better guide future research directions.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The Any2Omni dataset is likely to become a standard resource for researchers working on ODI generation and editing.
    *   The Omni² model could serve as a baseline for future research in unified ODI generation and editing.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a good contribution, primarily driven by the Any2Omni dataset. The Omni² model demonstrates promising results, but the architectural novelty is incremental, and the evaluation of the editing tasks could be stronger. The claim that the work *unifies* the task might be an overstatement, given that it only incorporates a limited amount of tasks. The novelty is not as profound as one would expect from top tier publication. Therefore, I assign a score that reflects the dataset's significance, the model's potential, and some limitations in the architectural novelty and thoroughness of the editing task evaluation.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### RankAlign: A Ranking View of the Generator-Validator Gap in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11381v1)
- **Authors**: Juan Diego Rodriguez, Wenxuan Ding, Katrin Erk, Greg Durrett
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) have become generally more capable and accurate across many tasks, some fundamental sources of unreliability remain in their behavior. One key limitation is their inconsistency at reporting the the same information when prompts are changed. In this paper, we consider the discrepancy between a model's generated answer and their own verification of that answer, the generator-validator gap. We define this gap in a more stringent way than prior work: we expect correlation of scores from a generator and a validator over the entire set of candidate answers. We show that according to this measure, a large gap exists in various settings, including question answering, lexical semantics tasks, and next-word prediction. We then propose RankAlign, a ranking-based training method, and show that it significantly closes the gap by 31.8% on average, surpassing all baseline methods. Moreover, this approach generalizes well to out-of-domain tasks and lexical items.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "RankAlign: A Ranking View of the Generator-Validator Gap in Large Language Models" investigates the generator-validator (G-V) gap in LLMs, which refers to the inconsistency between a model's generated answer and its verification of that answer.  Instead of treating this as a binary classification problem (correct/incorrect), the authors propose a more stringent measure: correlation between the generator's confidence (log probability) and the validator's confidence *across the entire set* of candidate answers.  They demonstrate that a significant G-V gap exists under this new measure in various settings.  The paper then introduces RankAlign, a ranking-based fine-tuning approach, which aligns the validator's rankings with those derived from the generator's log probabilities.  Experiments show that RankAlign significantly reduces the G-V gap, generalizing well to out-of-domain tasks and novel lexical items.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper introduces a novel formulation of the G-V gap.  Previous works typically focused on the binary accuracy of validation decisions given generated answers.  Defining the gap in terms of correlation of log-odds across *all* candidate answers is a meaningful advancement. The RankAlign training objective is also new and addresses the proposed metric effectively.
    *   **Weaknesses:** While the specific RankAlign loss function is novel, the general idea of using ranking losses to align different model outputs is not entirely new. The connection to DPO, while discussed, also highlights the similarities to prior work in preference optimization.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The significance lies in addressing a fundamental issue in LLM reliability: inconsistency. Closing the G-V gap has important implications for using LLMs in evaluation settings (LLMs-as-judges) and for improving model trust and calibration. The fact that RankAlign generalizes to unseen tasks and lexical items adds to its practical value.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The paper is primarily empirical. While the method demonstrably improves the correlation between generator and validator, the theoretical underpinnings of *why* RankAlign works are not deeply explored. It is unclear that maximizing correlation is the *best* objective function or if better performance can be obtained by directly maximizing the probabilities of correct answers. Further, the degradation in accuracy on some tasks warrants a deeper investigation into the trade-offs involved.

*   **Impact and Clarity:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly presents the problem, proposed solution, and experimental results. The figures are helpful in illustrating the concepts. However, a more in-depth discussion of the limitations and potential future research directions would strengthen the paper.

* **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The paper scores highly due to its insightful reframing of the G-V gap and the effective RankAlign method. The experiments are comprehensive and demonstrate the method's practical benefits. However, there are weaknesses that prevent it from scoring higher. There is limited theoretical analysis, questions surrounding accuracy, and limited scope of the problem. As such, the contributions are incremental and the proposed method is a single step forward on what is an important problem.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### VideoPanda: Video Panoramic Diffusion with Multi-view Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11389v1)
- **Authors**: Kevin Xie, Amirmojtaba Sabour, Jiahui Huang, Despoina Paschalidou, Greg Klar, Umar Iqbal, Sanja Fidler, Xiaohui Zeng
- **Abstract**: High resolution panoramic video content is paramount for immersive experiences in Virtual Reality, but is non-trivial to collect as it requires specialized equipment and intricate camera setups. In this work, we introduce VideoPanda, a novel approach for synthesizing 360$^\circ$ videos conditioned on text or single-view video data. VideoPanda leverages multi-view attention layers to augment a video diffusion model, enabling it to generate consistent multi-view videos that can be combined into immersive panoramic content. VideoPanda is trained jointly using two conditions: text-only and single-view video, and supports autoregressive generation of long-videos. To overcome the computational burden of multi-view video generation, we randomly subsample the duration and camera views used during training and show that the model is able to gracefully generalize to generating more frames during inference. Extensive evaluations on both real-world and synthetic video datasets demonstrate that VideoPanda generates more realistic and coherent 360$^\circ$ panoramas across all input conditions compared to existing methods. Visit the project website at https://research-staging.nvidia.com/labs/toronto-ai/VideoPanda/ for results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the VideoPanda paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VideoPanda, a novel approach for generating 360° panoramic videos.  It builds upon existing video diffusion models, enhancing them with multi-view attention layers to ensure consistency across different views, which are then stitched together to create the panoramic video. VideoPanda can generate these videos either from text prompts or by extending single-view videos. The model is trained jointly using text-only and single-view video conditions, and supports autoregressive generation for longer videos. To mitigate the computational challenges of multi-view video generation, a random subsampling strategy during training is employed, allowing the model to generalize to longer videos and more views during inference. The paper presents quantitative and qualitative evaluations demonstrating that VideoPanda produces more realistic and coherent 360° panoramas compared to existing methods, along with a user study that shows a preference for its outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adapting video diffusion models with multi-view attention to generate 360° panoramic videos is reasonably novel. While previous works have explored multi-view image generation and 360° video generation separately, VideoPanda specifically tackles the challenge of generating *dynamic* 360° videos conditioned on both text and single-view videos, which is a valuable extension. The random subsampling strategy to manage computational cost is also a practical contribution, although not fundamentally groundbreaking.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to make immersive content creation more accessible.  High-quality panoramic videos are crucial for VR experiences, but current acquisition methods are expensive. VideoPanda offers a generative alternative, potentially reducing the barrier to entry for VR content creation. The ability to extend single-view videos into panoramic formats is particularly useful, given the abundance of existing single-view content. The comparative results demonstrate clear improvements over a strong baseline (360DVD), especially in image quality and consistency, which is important for visually immersive experiences.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper demonstrates a clear improvement in quality and coherence compared to existing methods, backed by quantitative metrics and qualitative examples.
    *   The conditioning flexibility (text and single-view video) makes the model versatile.
    *   The autoregressive generation capability allows for creating longer panoramic videos.
    *   The random subsampling training strategy is a clever approach to handling computational constraints.
    *   The method addresses a practical problem in the VR/AR domain.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dependence on a pre-trained video diffusion model means that VideoPanda's performance is limited by the capabilities of that underlying model. This isn't necessarily a flaw, but it's a dependence.
    *   The autoregressive generation, while useful, still suffers from error accumulation, leading to blurriness over long sequences. While they acknowledge the issue and employ noise augmentation, a more robust solution would be beneficial.
    *   The evaluation could be strengthened by including comparisons to more diverse baselines (e.g., if PanoDiffusion wasn't prone to overfitting to indoor scenes, including comparisons with this).
    *   The prompts in the paper are also simple and the multi-view attention is not explicitly used for control purposes. Therefore, the advantages of multi-view representations for generation are not completely leveraged.
    *   The significance is mainly due to the *potential* accessibility that the method could lead to, but the actual benefit for VR/AR content creators are unquantifiable.

*   **Potential Influence:** VideoPanda is likely to influence future research in panoramic video generation, especially in exploring improved architectures for maintaining consistency across views and developing more robust autoregressive techniques. The approach of using multi-view attention in video diffusion models could also be applied to other tasks, such as generating multi-view consistent standard videos.

**Justification for the Score:**

VideoPanda presents a valuable and reasonably novel approach to panoramic video generation with demonstrable improvements over a strong baseline. The ability to condition on text and single-view videos and the strategy of mitigating computational burden are beneficial. However, the limitations in autoregressive generation and the dependence on a pre-trained model slightly temper its overall impact. While significant in the sense of opening doors, the *actual* impact for VR/AR content creators is not yet quantifiable.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### DataDecide: How to Predict Best Pretraining Data with Small Experiments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11393v1)
- **Authors**: Ian Magnusson, Nguyen Tai, Ben Bogin, David Heineman, Jena D. Hwang, Luca Soldaini, Akshita Bhagia, Jiacheng Liu, Dirk Groeneveld, Oyvind Tafjord, Noah A. Smith, Pang Wei Koh, Jesse Dodge
- **Abstract**: Because large language models are expensive to pretrain on different datasets, using smaller-scale experiments to decide on data is crucial for reducing costs. Which benchmarks and methods of making decisions from observed performance at small scale most accurately predict the datasets that yield the best large models? To empower open exploration of this question, we release models, data, and evaluations in DataDecide -- the most extensive open suite of models over differences in data and scale. We conduct controlled pretraining experiments across 25 corpora with differing sources, deduplication, and filtering up to 100B tokens, model sizes up to 1B parameters, and 3 random seeds. We find that the ranking of models at a single, small size (e.g., 150M parameters) is a strong baseline for predicting best models at our larger target scale (1B) (~80% of com parisons correct). No scaling law methods among 8 baselines exceed the compute-decision frontier of single-scale predictions, but DataDecide can measure improvement in future scaling laws. We also identify that using continuous likelihood metrics as proxies in small experiments makes benchmarks including MMLU, ARC, HellaSwag, MBPP, and HumanEval >80% predictable at the target 1B scale with just 0.01% of the compute.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "How to Predict Best Pretraining Data with Small Experiments":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DATADECIDE, a large-scale suite of pre-trained language models and datasets designed to empirically evaluate methods for predicting the best pretraining data given small-scale experiments. The authors train models on 25 different data recipes (corpora with varying sources, deduplication, and filtering), across 14 model sizes (4M to 1B parameters), and with 3 random seeds, resulting in over 30,000 model checkpoints. They then compare various methods for extrapolating performance from small models to a target large model (1B parameters), including single-scale ranking and scaling law fitting. The key findings are that: a) Single-scale ranking (choosing the best data recipe at a small model size) is a surprisingly strong baseline. b) Established scaling law methods fail to outperform this single-scale baseline in terms of compute efficiency for decision accuracy. c) Continuous likelihood metrics (like correct probability), computed on smaller models, are better predictors than discrete accuracy metrics for certain tasks.  The paper concludes with practical recommendations for efficient data selection and emphasizes that DATADECIDE can serve as a benchmark for future scaling law innovations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in the scale and control of its experimental setup. DATADECIDE is significantly larger than previous efforts in this area, with a more diverse set of data recipes and model sizes. The rigorous experimentation with random seeds, especially at the 1B parameter scale, is also a strength. However, the methods compared (single-scale ranking and scaling laws) are not novel in themselves; the novelty is in the controlled comparison across many datasets and scales. The exploration of continuous likelihood metrics as proxies for accuracy is also a good addition.

*   **Significance:** Data selection is a critical problem in language model pretraining, and the high cost of training large models makes it essential to develop efficient data selection techniques. DATADECIDE provides a valuable resource for researchers working on this problem. The finding that simple single-scale ranking works surprisingly well challenges the assumption that complex scaling law fitting is always necessary.  The identification of task dependencies (some tasks are easier to predict than others) is also important. The release of the dataset allows for future research into this topic, and could drive improvements in how data selection is handled in LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-Scale, Controlled Experiments:** DATADECIDE's comprehensive nature is a major strength, enabling robust conclusions.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:** The paper provides concrete advice for practitioners on how to make data selection decisions.
    *   **Open Resource:** Releasing the models, data, and evaluations will foster further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Methods:** While the paper compares several scaling law methods, it might benefit from exploring more recent and sophisticated approaches that incorporate different types of data metrics and training dynamics. There's also no work with advanced approaches such as active data selection or curriculum learning.
    *   **Task Dependency:** The performance of prediction methods varies substantially across tasks. The paper acknowledges this, but further analysis to understand *why* certain tasks are easier to predict could strengthen the work.
    *   **One Token-Parameter Ratio:** The focus on a single token-parameter ratio limits generalizability of findings.

*   **Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several ways:

    *   Serving as a benchmark for new data selection techniques.
    *   Highlighting the effectiveness of simple baselines (single-scale ranking).
    *   Encouraging the use of continuous likelihood metrics as proxies for accuracy.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field. The large, controlled experiments and the release of the DATADECIDE resource are valuable assets to the research community. While the methods compared are not groundbreaking, the systematic evaluation and the resulting practical recommendations make this a worthwhile contribution. The limitations around method scope and the reasons for task dependency do knock the score down a bit, but ultimately this is a solid contribution.

### Leveraging Point Transformers for Detecting Anatomical Landmarks in Digital Dentistry
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11418v1)
- **Authors**: Tibor Kubík, Oldřich Kodym, Petr Šilling, Kateřina Trávníčková, Tomáš Mojžiš, Jan Matula
- **Abstract**: The increasing availability of intraoral scanning devices has heightened their importance in modern clinical orthodontics. Clinicians utilize advanced Computer-Aided Design techniques to create patient-specific treatment plans that include laboriously identifying crucial landmarks such as cusps, mesial-distal locations, facial axis points, and tooth-gingiva boundaries. Detecting such landmarks automatically presents challenges, including limited dataset sizes, significant anatomical variability among subjects, and the geometric nature of the data. We present our experiments from the 3DTeethLand Grand Challenge at MICCAI 2024. Our method leverages recent advancements in point cloud learning through transformer architectures. We designed a Point Transformer v3 inspired module to capture meaningful geometric and anatomical features, which are processed by a lightweight decoder to predict per-point distances, further processed by graph-based non-minima suppression. We report promising results and discuss insights on learned feature interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Leveraging Point Transformers for Detecting Anatomical Landmarks in Digital Dentistry":

**Summary:**

The paper presents a method for automatically detecting anatomical landmarks in 3D digital dental scans using a Point Transformer v3 (PTv3) architecture.  The method focuses on overcoming challenges such as limited dataset sizes, anatomical variability, and the geometric nature of the data. The approach uses a PTv3-inspired module to extract geometric and anatomical features, a lightweight decoder to predict per-point distances (forming a distance map), and a graph-based non-minima suppression algorithm for accurate landmark localization. The authors participated in the 3DTeethLand Grand Challenge at MICCAI 2024 and present promising results regarding precision and recall. They also discuss the interpretability of learned features and the effect of the distance map representation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Use of a State-of-the-Art Architecture:**  Leveraging PTv3, a recent advancement in point cloud learning, is a significant strength. The architecture is known for its speed and simplicity, making it appealing for clinical applications where real-time performance is desirable.
    *   **Addressing Key Challenges:**  The paper directly tackles the core challenges of landmark detection in dental scans, including data scarcity and anatomical variation. The use of geometric morphing during training is a good attempt to improve robustness to anatomical variations.
    *   **Topology-Driven Non-Minima Suppression:** The proposed non-minima suppression technique, integrated with the mesh topology, is a novel approach that aims to reduce false positives and improve localization accuracy. This is particularly relevant because existing methods often struggle with high false positive rates.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes evaluation metrics such as precision and recall at different error thresholds, which offers a clear understanding of the performance.
    *   **Emphasis on Clinical Applicability:** The emphasis on real-time performance and compact model size highlights the potential for clinical application.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Architecture:** While the *application* of PTv3 to dental landmarking is novel, the *architectural modifications* seem limited to adapting PTv3 and using a lightweight decoder. It isn't clear how much architectural innovation has been incorporated besides utilizing PTv3 as the base.
    *   **Dataset Size:** Although the authors mention the limited dataset size as a challenge, only 200 cases are used to train and validate model which is small by today standards. The performance of deep learning models is highly dependent on size of dataset.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper could benefit from more detailed ablation studies, especially concerning the impact of the topology-driven non-minima suppression. It's hard to quantify how much this module improves performance in isolation.
    *   **Interpretability Claims:** While the paper mentions the interpretability of learned features and demonstrates PCA projections, the depth of analysis is superficial.  A more rigorous analysis of what specific geometric features are being captured would significantly strengthen this claim.
    *   **Baseline comparison**: The paper would benefit greatly from comparison of the proposed methodology with other landmark detection techniques in order to show the true impact of the architecture.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The study extends the application of point cloud learning to a specific medical domain, digital dentistry, and shows the potential of transformer-based architectures.
    *   The topology-driven non-minima suppression technique offers a promising solution to reduce false positives in landmark detection.
    *   The research contributes to the development of automated tools that can improve clinical orthodontics workflows.

**Justification for the Score:**

Despite its strengths, the paper has some limitations regarding novelty and thoroughness. While the use of PTv3 and the topology-driven non-minima suppression show promise, the limited dataset size and lack of extensive ablation studies restrict the ability to definitively assess the method's capabilities and impact. A comparison with other dental landmarking techniques would further elevate this paper.

### Reinforcing Compositional Retrieval: Retrieving Step-by-Step for Composing Informative Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11420v1)
- **Authors**: Quanyu Long, Jianda Chen, Zhengyuan Liu, Nancy F. Chen, Wenya Wang, Sinno Jialin Pan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across numerous tasks, yet they often rely on external context to handle complex tasks. While retrieval-augmented frameworks traditionally focus on selecting top-ranked documents in a single pass, many real-world scenarios demand compositional retrieval, where multiple sources must be combined in a coordinated manner. In this work, we propose a tri-encoder sequential retriever that models this process as a Markov Decision Process (MDP), decomposing the probability of retrieving a set of elements into a sequence of conditional probabilities and allowing each retrieval step to be conditioned on previously selected examples. We train the retriever in two stages: first, we efficiently construct supervised sequential data for initial policy training; we then refine the policy to align with the LLM's preferences using a reward grounded in the structural correspondence of generated programs. Experimental results show that our method consistently and significantly outperforms baselines, underscoring the importance of explicitly modeling inter-example dependencies. These findings highlight the potential of compositional retrieval for tasks requiring multiple pieces of evidence or examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new approach called Reinforcing Compositional Retrieval (RCR) for improving the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on complex tasks that require external context.  Instead of the traditional single-pass retrieval of documents, RCR models context retrieval as a Markov Decision Process (MDP). This allows for sequential selection of context examples, conditioning each retrieval step on the previously selected examples. A tri-encoder architecture is used as the policy model, mapping the query and partial retrieval sequence to a probability distribution over candidate examples. The policy is trained in two stages: first, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to maximize sub-structure coverage in the initial selection, and then reinforcement learning (RL) to align with the LLM's preferences based on the structural similarity of generated programs. The method is evaluated on compositional generalization semantic parsing benchmarks, showing improved performance over baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its formulation of context retrieval as an MDP for compositional tasks. This is a departure from traditional top-k retrieval methods that treat each candidate independently. The tri-encoder architecture is a solid choice to facilitate sequential modelling. Furthermore, the combination of supervised fine-tuning for efficient data creation followed by reinforcement learning is also new and effective. However, there have been other sequential retrieval approaches as well; the novelty is primarily in the combination of techniques and the MDP formulation.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses an important limitation of current LLMs – their reliance on high-quality external context for complex tasks.  By explicitly modeling inter-example dependencies, RCR demonstrates potential for improving context composition, leading to better program generation quality. The experimental results on compositional generalization tasks provide empirical evidence to support these claims. The performance gains are significant, indicating a tangible improvement over existing methods.  The task itself -- compositional generalization in semantic parsing -- is a fairly well-trodden domain, reducing the significance somewhat. The contribution is more methodological rather than creating entirely new task formulations.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the RCR approach.
    * The MDP formulation of context retrieval is a novel and promising idea.
    * The tri-encoder architecture provides a practical way to implement sequential retrieval.
    * The two-stage training approach (SFT and RL) is efficient and effective.
    * The experimental results show consistent improvements over strong baselines.
    * The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of each component of RCR.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * While the paper demonstrates strong results on semantic parsing tasks, it's unclear how well RCR would generalize to other complex tasks, such as multi-hop question answering or summarization. The dependence on structural similarity for reward might be limiting.
    * The experiments are conducted using a relatively small set of datasets, limiting the generalizability of the findings. COVR-10, while useful, is synthetic. GeoQuery, while a classical semantic parsing dataset, has been around for quite some time. A greater diversity of datasets, particularly those with more real-world complexity, would strengthen the paper.
    * The paper could benefit from a more thorough discussion of the computational costs associated with RCR, particularly the overhead of sequential retrieval compared to traditional methods.
    * The approach relies on a reward function based on structural similarity of programs.  Defining robust rewards for other complex tasks might be challenging.

* **Impact:**  If the approach can be adapted to other complex tasks and scaled to larger datasets, it could significantly impact the development of more robust and reliable LLMs. The focus on compositional retrieval is particularly important as LLMs are increasingly used for tasks that require combining information from multiple sources.

* **Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by introducing a novel approach for context retrieval in LLMs. The experimental results are promising, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. However, the limited dataset diversity and reliance on structural similarity as the core element of the reward function somewhat limit the scope and generalizability of the findings. A stronger evaluation on more varied and realistic tasks would have substantially increased its impact.

### ADT: Tuning Diffusion Models with Adversarial Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11423v1)
- **Authors**: Dazhong Shen, Guanglu Song, Yi Zhang, Bingqi Ma, Lujundong Li, Dongzhi Jiang, Zhuofan Zong, Yu Liu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved outstanding image generation by reversing a forward noising process to approximate true data distributions. During training, these models predict diffusion scores from noised versions of true samples in a single forward pass, while inference requires iterative denoising starting from white noise. This training-inference divergences hinder the alignment between inference and training data distributions, due to potential prediction biases and cumulative error accumulation. To address this problem, we propose an intuitive but effective fine-tuning framework, called Adversarial Diffusion Tuning (ADT), by stimulating the inference process during optimization and aligning the final outputs with training data by adversarial supervision. Specifically, to achieve robust adversarial training, ADT features a siamese-network discriminator with a fixed pre-trained backbone and lightweight trainable parameters, incorporates an image-to-image sampling strategy to smooth discriminative difficulties, and preserves the original diffusion loss to prevent discriminator hacking. In addition, we carefully constrain the backward-flowing path for back-propagating gradients along the inference path without incurring memory overload or gradient explosion. Finally, extensive experiments on Stable Diffusion models (v1.5, XL, and v3), demonstrate that ADT significantly improves both distribution alignment and image quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adversarial Diffusion Tuning (ADT), a fine-tuning framework designed to improve the alignment between training and inference distributions in diffusion models. ADT addresses the discrepancies arising from the iterative denoising process during inference versus the single-pass score prediction during training. It incorporates the iterative inference process into the fine-tuning optimization, leveraging adversarial supervision to align the final outputs with the training data.  ADT employs a siamese-network discriminator for robust adversarial training, utilizes an image-to-image sampling strategy to smooth discriminative difficulties, and preserves the original diffusion loss to prevent discriminator hacking. The authors also carefully constrain the backward-flowing path for stable gradient propagation during the iterative inference process.  Experiments on Stable Diffusion models (v1.5, XL, and v3) demonstrate that ADT improves both distribution alignment and image quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its holistic approach to addressing the training-inference divergence problem in diffusion models. While individual components like adversarial training and careful gradient management have been explored in related contexts, the integration of these elements *specifically* to stimulate and then supervise the inference process within a diffusion model training loop is a valuable contribution.  The siamese-network-based discriminator is a reasonable choice for stable adversarial training, as are image-to-image sampling and gradient clipping/stopping techniques. The method extends prior efforts like DRTune but offers a more comprehensive solution. The specific combination and adaptation of these techniques for diffusion models, as well as the implementation details for stability and gradient control, constitutes a novel contribution.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential of improving the sample quality and training efficiency of diffusion models. The explicit alignment of training and inference distributions can lead to better generalization, reduce biases, and ultimately generate more realistic and higher-quality images. This is important, since improving sample quality in these models improves downstream tasks.  The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of ADT across various Stable Diffusion models, which solidifies its practical relevance. The ablation studies give insight into how important each component is. If the results hold at scale, it could become a very popular fine-tuning approach.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Approach:** ADT tackles a fundamental problem in diffusion models by addressing the core divergence between training and inference.
    * **Technical Soundness:** The method employs well-established techniques (adversarial training, siamese networks, gradient control) in a principled way, adapted for the specifics of diffusion models.
    * **Experimental Validation:**  The authors demonstrate the efficacy of ADT on diverse Stable Diffusion models, using standard metrics, and qualitative examples.  The thoroughness of testing adds to the strength of the submission.
    * **Ablation Studies:** Thorough ablation studies help explain what's necessary for the method to succeed.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement?:** While ADT shows improvement, the gains, especially on larger models like SDXL, might be considered incremental.  The jump in performance isn't necessarily earth-shattering, more of a sensible and welcome improvement.
    * **Computational Cost:** Adding adversarial training, which can be computationally demanding, and iterating over the inference process adds cost compared to standard training of a diffusion model. While the paper mentions efficiency aspects, more detailed profiling and comparisons would strengthen the analysis. The increased complexity also means it may be challenging for many to implement from scratch.
    * **Generalizability:** While the paper shows promising results, the experiments are limited to a few specific Stable Diffusion variants and a Midjourney dataset.  Testing across more diverse architectures and datasets would demonstrate broader applicability.
    * **Discriminator Reliance:** The approach heavily relies on the effectiveness of the discriminator. While using a siamese architecture and pre-trained backbone helps, it's still susceptible to limitations of the discriminator's representation.
    * **Metric Discussion:** The authors could better discuss why the CLIP score may not work for all samples.

**Justification for the score:**

Overall, ADT presents a valuable contribution to the field. While the individual techniques aren't entirely novel, the integration of these techniques to specifically address the training-inference divergence in diffusion models, along with the careful design choices for stability and gradient control, justifies a positive assessment.  The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the method. The improvements over other methods are measurable but perhaps incremental, and the added computational costs should be kept in mind. For these reasons, I assign it a score that reflects the balance of these factors.

### A Dual-Space Framework for General Knowledge Distillation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11426v1)
- **Authors**: Xue Zhang, Songming Zhang, Yunlong Liang, Fandong Meng, Yufeng Chen, Jinan Xu, Jie Zhou
- **Abstract**: Knowledge distillation (KD) is a promising solution to compress large language models (LLMs) by transferring their knowledge to smaller models. During this process, white-box KD methods usually minimize the distance between the output distributions of the teacher model and the student model to transfer more information. However, we reveal that the current white-box KD framework exhibits two limitations: a) bridging probability distributions from different output spaces will limit the similarity between the teacher model and the student model; b) this framework cannot be applied to LLMs with different vocabularies. One of the root causes for these limitations is that the distributions from the teacher and the student for KD are output by different prediction heads, which yield distributions in different output spaces and dimensions. Therefore, in this paper, we propose a dual-space knowledge distillation (DSKD) framework that unifies the prediction heads of the teacher and the student models for KD. Specifically, we first introduce two projectors with ideal initialization to project the teacher/student hidden states into the student/teacher representation spaces. After this, the hidden states from different models can share the same head and unify the output spaces of the distributions. Furthermore, we develop an exact token alignment (ETA) algorithm to align the same tokens in two differently-tokenized sequences. Based on the above, our DSKD framework is a general KD framework that supports both off-policy and on-policy KD, and KD between any two LLMs regardless of their vocabularies. Extensive experiments on instruction-following, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks show that DSKD significantly outperforms existing methods based on the current white-box KD framework and surpasses other cross-tokenizer KD methods for LLMs with different vocabularies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new knowledge distillation (KD) framework called Dual-Space Knowledge Distillation (DSKD) for large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to address limitations of existing white-box KD methods, namely, the limited similarity between the teacher and student models and the constraint of requiring the same vocabulary. DSKD addresses these limitations by unifying the output spaces of teacher and student models through projection using ideally initialized projectors. The framework also proposes an exact token alignment (ETA) algorithm to enable KD between LLMs with different vocabularies. The authors demonstrate that DSKD outperforms existing KD methods, including those designed for cross-tokenizer KD, across various tasks like instruction-following, mathematical reasoning, and code generation. The framework is evaluated under both off-policy and on-policy KD settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a genuinely innovative approach to knowledge distillation. The key novelty lies in the dual-space projection concept. This tackles a fundamental problem within the traditional white-box KD setup, where differing prediction heads lead to dissimilar representations. The ETA algorithm is also a useful addition, enabling KD between models with disparate vocabularies. While cross-tokenizer KD exists, the DSKD framework provides a more holistic and potentially more effective solution by directly unifying the output spaces *after* alignment.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is high.  It directly addresses practical hurdles in LLM compression. Being able to transfer knowledge effectively between models with diverse architectures and tokenizers expands the practical applicability of KD. The observed performance gains across several benchmarks support the assertion that DSKD better facilitates knowledge transfer. Also, the rigorous on-policy experiments show the promise of mitigating training-inference mismatch when compressing LLMs, a practical concern often disregarded.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Innovative dual-space projection concept and the ETA algorithm.
    *   Comprehensive experiments across diverse tasks and settings (off-policy/on-policy KD, same/different vocabularies).
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating consistent performance improvements.
    *   The addition of on-policy KD extends the practical applicability of the DSKD.
    *   Ablation studies pinpoint the contribution of different components in the DSKD framework.
    *   GPT-4's preference analysis demonstrates the utility of DSKD.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the optimal initialization of the projectors enhances the method, the success hinges on the quality of both teachers and students, thus needing more scrutiny.
    *   As shown in table IV, there is still a significant performance gap for the KD with different vocabularies compared to the setting with the same vocabulary, so there are still places for improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a strong potential to influence the field. It presents a general and effective framework for KD in LLMs, which could become a standard approach, particularly for scenarios involving models with varying architectures or vocabularies. The concepts of unifying output spaces and token alignment are likely to inspire further research in KD and related areas like model transfer and domain adaptation.

*   **Justification for the Score:** The paper makes a substantial contribution by addressing important limitations of existing KD techniques, offering a general solution that expands its applicability to a more diverse set of LLMs. The comprehensive experiments and clear improvements over baselines justify a high score. While there's scope for further investigation, DSKD is a significant step forward.
