### NormalCrafter: Learning Temporally Consistent Normals from Video Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11427v1)
- **Authors**: Yanrui Bin, Wenbo Hu, Haoyuan Wang, Xinya Chen, Bing Wang
- **Abstract**: Surface normal estimation serves as a cornerstone for a spectrum of computer vision applications. While numerous efforts have been devoted to static image scenarios, ensuring temporal coherence in video-based normal estimation remains a formidable challenge. Instead of merely augmenting existing methods with temporal components, we present NormalCrafter to leverage the inherent temporal priors of video diffusion models. To secure high-fidelity normal estimation across sequences, we propose Semantic Feature Regularization (SFR), which aligns diffusion features with semantic cues, encouraging the model to concentrate on the intrinsic semantics of the scene. Moreover, we introduce a two-stage training protocol that leverages both latent and pixel space learning to preserve spatial accuracy while maintaining long temporal context. Extensive evaluations demonstrate the efficacy of our method, showcasing a superior performance in generating temporally consistent normal sequences with intricate details from diverse videos.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "NormalCrafter: Learning Temporally Consistent Normals from Video Diffusion Priors" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NormalCrafter, a novel video normal estimation model that leverages the temporal priors inherent in video diffusion models to generate temporally consistent normal sequences from open-world videos.  It tackles the challenge of maintaining both spatial fidelity and temporal coherence in video normal estimation. The key contributions are: 1) Semantic Feature Regularization (SFR), which aligns diffusion features with semantic cues to encourage the model to focus on the scene's intrinsic semantics. 2) A two-stage training protocol involving both latent and pixel space learning to preserve spatial accuracy while maintaining long temporal context.  Experimental results demonstrate superior performance compared to existing methods in generating temporally consistent normal sequences with detailed structures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates reasonable novelty by combining video diffusion models with a semantic regularization technique for video normal estimation. The two-stage training protocol also contributes to novelty. While video diffusion models have been used for depth estimation, adapting them for normal estimation with an explicit emphasis on temporal consistency and fine-grained details via SFR is a valuable contribution. The idea of decoupling the training into a latent-space phase for temporal consistency and a pixel-space fine-tuning phase for spatial accuracy is also well-executed and novel.

*   **Significance:** Surface normal estimation is a fundamental task in computer vision, impacting a wide range of applications. Addressing the temporal inconsistency issues present in existing image-based normal estimation methods applied to videos has significant practical value. NormalCrafter's ability to generate temporally stable and detailed normal maps could improve the performance of video editing, 3D reconstruction, and other related tasks. The comprehensive evaluations on diverse datasets, including both single-image and video benchmarks, further strengthen the paper's significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proposed SFR effectively guides the diffusion model towards intrinsic semantics, leading to more accurate and detailed normal estimations.
    *   The two-stage training protocol successfully balances the trade-off between temporal consistency and spatial accuracy.
    *   Comprehensive experimental results show state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks.
    *   The ablation studies thoroughly validate the effectiveness of the proposed components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper highlights the limitations of optical flow-based methods, a more direct comparison (even if implementation details are not publicly available for direct comparison as they are not) and analysis of the runtime performance would add more value.
    *   The paper mentions a large parameter size as a limitation, which creates challenges with deployment in edge devices. How this may be mitigated remains speculative.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in video normal estimation and related areas. The proposed techniques could be extended to other dense prediction tasks. It can also encourage future research into improving diffusion models for video-based tasks.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of video normal estimation. NormalCrafter effectively leverages video diffusion models and semantic regularization to address the challenging problems of temporal inconsistency and spatial detail. The two-stage training strategy further enhances the model's performance. The comprehensive evaluations and ablation studies support the claims made in the paper. While the limitation of the large parameter size is noted, it opens for future research towards model efficiency with pruning, quantization, etc. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, indicating its important contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Masculine Defaults via Gendered Discourse in Podcasts and Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11431v1)
- **Authors**: Maria Teleki, Xiangjue Dong, Haoran Liu, James Caverlee
- **Abstract**: Masculine defaults are widely recognized as a significant type of gender bias, but they are often unseen as they are under-researched. Masculine defaults involve three key parts: (i) the cultural context, (ii) the masculine characteristics or behaviors, and (iii) the reward for, or simply acceptance of, those masculine characteristics or behaviors. In this work, we study discourse-based masculine defaults, and propose a twofold framework for (i) the large-scale discovery and analysis of gendered discourse words in spoken content via our Gendered Discourse Correlation Framework (GDCF); and (ii) the measurement of the gender bias associated with these gendered discourse words in LLMs via our Discourse Word-Embedding Association Test (D-WEAT). We focus our study on podcasts, a popular and growing form of social media, analyzing 15,117 podcast episodes. We analyze correlations between gender and discourse words -- discovered via LDA and BERTopic -- to automatically form gendered discourse word lists. We then study the prevalence of these gendered discourse words in domain-specific contexts, and find that gendered discourse-based masculine defaults exist in the domains of business, technology/politics, and video games. Next, we study the representation of these gendered discourse words from a state-of-the-art LLM embedding model from OpenAI, and find that the masculine discourse words have a more stable and robust representation than the feminine discourse words, which may result in better system performance on downstream tasks for men. Hence, men are rewarded for their discourse patterns with better system performance by one of the state-of-the-art language models -- and this embedding disparity is a representational harm and a masculine default.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces a two-fold framework, GDCF and D-WEAT, to discover and analyze gendered discourse words in spoken content (specifically podcasts) and measure the associated bias in Large Language Models (LLMs).  GDCF (Gendered Discourse Correlation Framework) identifies gendered discourse words by analyzing correlations between gender and discourse features extracted from podcast audio and transcripts.  D-WEAT (Discourse Word-Embedding Association Test), an extension of WEAT, then measures gender bias in LLM embeddings by analyzing the representation of these gendered discourse words. The authors find that masculine discourse words have a more stable representation in LLMs, which constitutes a representational harm and a masculine default.  They also find masculine defaults in domain-specific contexts like business, technology/politics, and video games.

**Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its focus on *discourse-based* masculine defaults and the development of a framework for their automated discovery and analysis, especially within the context of LLMs. Previous work has explored gender differences and biases in language and LLMs, but the specific angle of how masculine defaults manifest and are reinforced through discourse patterns is less explored.  Extending WEAT to D-WEAT, and focusing on spoken content, adds another layer of novelty.  The observation that masculine discourse has a more robust representation in a state-of-the-art LLM is a new and potentially important finding.

**Significance:** Identifying and quantifying subtle biases in LLMs is crucial as these models are increasingly deployed in real-world applications. The paper's findings suggest that LLMs may inadvertently perpetuate masculine defaults, potentially leading to unfair or discriminatory outcomes. By providing a framework for identifying and measuring these biases, the authors contribute to the development of more equitable and inclusive language technologies.  Demonstrating how seemingly innocuous discourse patterns contribute to wider gender biases offers a practical tool for researchers and practitioners aiming to mitigate algorithmic discrimination.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly defines masculine defaults and articulates the research questions.
*   **Well-defined methodology:** The two-part framework (GDCF and D-WEAT) is well-structured and explained.
*   **Use of established techniques:** The paper leverages existing techniques like LDA, BERTopic, WhisperX, and WEAT, but combines and adapts them in a novel way to address the research questions.
*   **Empirical validation:** The experiments on a large podcast dataset provide empirical evidence to support the claims.
*   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper explores various aspects of gendered discourse, including correlations with domain-specific topics and the impact on LLM embeddings.
*   **Code and results availability:** Making the code and extended results publicly available promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Consideration of limitations and ethical implications:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of the binary gender definition, the potential for misuse of the framework, and other constraints.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplification of gender:** The reliance on a binary gender definition is a significant limitation, as acknowledged by the authors. It doesn't capture the complexity of gender identity and expression.
*   **Limited scope of discourse analysis:** The analysis focuses primarily on a relatively limited set of discourse features.  A more nuanced analysis incorporating other linguistic features, such as sentiment, style, or speech acts, could provide a more comprehensive understanding of gendered discourse.
*   **Causal claims:** While the paper demonstrates correlations, it doesn't establish causal relationships between the presence of masculine discourse and LLM performance or real-world outcomes. It states "better system performance" but doesn't provide the downstream task or metric for evaluating performance change due to robust representation. Further experiments could strengthen claims on how a stable representation of masculine defaults improves task accuracy or speed, and for what tasks.
*   **UMAP reduction:** The BERTopic section is weakened by concluding that UMAP embeddings reduce the BERTopic embeddings to a limited output space. This might be better explored.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and valuable contribution to the field of NLP, particularly in the area of bias detection and mitigation in LLMs. The focus on discourse-based masculine defaults is relatively underexplored, and the proposed framework offers a practical approach for identifying and measuring these biases. The empirical findings and the ethical considerations make the paper a meaningful contribution.

However, the limitations of the binary gender definition and the relatively limited scope of discourse analysis detract from the paper's overall impact. While the authors acknowledge these limitations, they are significant enough to warrant a slightly lower score. Furthermore, claiming performance improvement due to robust masculine defaults has not been empirically demonstrated.

Score: 7. The paper offers a valuable contribution to a relevant problem area and the methodology and data collection methods are rigorous. However, the paper is not without limitations that could be addressed in future iterations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TextArena
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11442v1)
- **Authors**: Leon Guertler, Bobby Cheng, Simon Yu, Bo Liu, Leshem Choshen, Cheston Tan
- **Abstract**: TextArena is an open-source collection of competitive text-based games for training and evaluation of agentic behavior in Large Language Models (LLMs). It spans 57+ unique environments (including single-player, two-player, and multi-player setups) and allows for easy evaluation of model capabilities via an online-play system (against humans and other submitted models) with real-time TrueSkill scores. Traditional benchmarks rarely assess dynamic social skills such as negotiation, theory of mind, and deception, creating a gap that TextArena addresses. Designed with research, community and extensibility in mind, TextArena emphasizes ease of adding new games, adapting the framework, testing models, playing against the models, and training models. Detailed documentation of environments, games, leaderboard, and examples are available on https://github.com/LeonGuertler/TextArena and https://www.textarena.ai/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the TextArena paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TextArena, an open-source collection of competitive text-based games designed for training and evaluating agentic behavior in large language models (LLMs).  It features a diverse set of games (57+ at the initial release, expanding to 74+ since) spanning single-player, two-player, and multi-player setups.  TextArena allows for online play against humans and other submitted models, using a TrueSkill™-based leaderboard for performance tracking. The core motivation is to address the limitations of traditional benchmarks that often fail to assess dynamic social skills like negotiation, theory of mind, and deception.  The framework emphasizes ease of extensibility, making it simple to add new games and adapt the existing framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the *combination* of several existing trends rather than a completely groundbreaking, new technology.  Competitive gameplay for LLM evaluation is not entirely new (e.g., Chatbot Arena), nor are Gym-like environments. The use of TrueSkill™ for LLM performance tracking, while appropriate, is also an established technique. The value comes from the integrated approach: a diverse set of games specifically targeting social skills, an online competition framework, a simple way to add new games, and a dynamic leaderboard. The most valuable aspect of this is the focus on evaluating soft skills.

* **Significance:** The significance of TextArena lies in its potential to drive research in areas currently underserved by standard benchmarks, specifically the dynamic social intelligence of LLMs. The framework's extensibility is crucial, allowing the community to contribute new games that target emerging skills. Furthermore, the availability of a competitive platform with real-time performance tracking can foster innovation and encourage the development of more robust and adaptable AI agents.  The move away from absolute benchmarks to *relative* rankings is a key strength, particularly in the face of rapidly advancing models. The ability to compare models against human performance provides a crucial real-world context. The identification of skill profiles is another important aspect.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Focus on Social Skills:**  Addresses a significant gap in LLM evaluation.
    *   **Extensibility:**  The ease of adding new games promotes community involvement and keeps the benchmark relevant as models evolve.
    *   **Online Competition:**  Enables dynamic evaluation and comparison against both models and humans.
    *   **TrueSkill™ Tracking:**  Provides a robust and reliable performance metric.
    *   **Open-Source:** Fosters accessibility and community contribution.
    * The games are either naively text-based or easily adaptable to text-based.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Text:** Text-based games, while versatile, may not fully capture the complexities of real-world social interactions.
    *   **Game Design Limitations:** The quality and validity of the games themselves are crucial.  The paper doesn't delve deeply into the design principles used to ensure that the games effectively measure the targeted skills.
    *   **Potential for Exploitation:**  As with any competitive environment, there is a risk that models could be specifically trained to exploit the mechanics of certain games, potentially skewing the overall evaluation. More investigation is needed to reduce model overfitting.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Model Behavior:** While the leaderboard provides a ranking, deeper analysis of how models actually play the games and the strategies they employ would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The impact on the field hinges on the adoption and contribution by the research community. If TextArena becomes a widely used platform for evaluating and training LLMs, it could significantly influence the development of more socially intelligent and adaptable AI agents. The project is open-source which allows and even encourages development and participation from other developers.

**Justification for Score:**

TextArena is a valuable contribution because it aggregates existing ideas, is well-structured, open-source, and targets a specific, underserved area (social skills). The easy integration makes it easier for new developers to test models. However, it is not breaking new ground from a technological perspective; it represents more of an *engineering* achievement. While TextArena does not pioneer fundamentally new evaluation strategies, its thoughtful design and community-driven approach makes it valuable.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diffusion Distillation With Direct Preference Optimization For Efficient 3D LiDAR Scene Completion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11447v1)
- **Authors**: An Zhaol, Shengyuan Zhang, Ling Yang, Zejian Li, Jiale Wu, Haoran Xu, AnYang Wei, Perry Pengyun GU Lingyun Sun
- **Abstract**: The application of diffusion models in 3D LiDAR scene completion is limited due to diffusion's slow sampling speed. Score distillation accelerates diffusion sampling but with performance degradation, while post-training with direct policy optimization (DPO) boosts performance using preference data. This paper proposes Distillation-DPO, a novel diffusion distillation framework for LiDAR scene completion with preference aligment. First, the student model generates paired completion scenes with different initial noises. Second, using LiDAR scene evaluation metrics as preference, we construct winning and losing sample pairs. Such construction is reasonable, since most LiDAR scene metrics are informative but non-differentiable to be optimized directly. Third, Distillation-DPO optimizes the student model by exploiting the difference in score functions between the teacher and student models on the paired completion scenes. Such procedure is repeated until convergence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, compared to state-of-the-art LiDAR scene completion diffusion models, Distillation-DPO achieves higher-quality scene completion while accelerating the completion speed by more than 5-fold. Our method is the first to explore adopting preference learning in distillation to the best of our knowledge and provide insights into preference-aligned distillation. Our code is public available on https://github.com/happyw1nd/DistillationDPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Diffusion Distillation With Direct Preference Optimization For Efficient 3D LiDAR Scene Completion":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the slow sampling speed of diffusion models when applied to 3D LiDAR scene completion. The authors propose Distillation-DPO, a novel diffusion distillation framework. This framework uses paired completion scenes generated by a student model with different initial noises. LiDAR scene evaluation metrics act as preference signals to construct win-lose sample pairs.  Distillation-DPO then optimizes the student model by exploiting the difference in score functions between teacher and student models on these paired scenes. Experiments show that the proposed method achieves higher-quality scene completion with more than a 5-fold speedup compared to state-of-the-art LiDAR scene completion diffusion models like LiDiff. The paper explores preference learning in distillation and provides insights into preference-aligned distillation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the application of DPO (Direct Preference Optimization) in the context of diffusion distillation for LiDAR scene completion. Combining these techniques, particularly using LiDAR scene metrics as preference signals, is a novel contribution. The introduction of the win-lose pairs construction using these metrics is a good idea and seems to work in practice. It avoids the need for expensive and difficult-to-obtain human annotations.

*   **Significance:** Speeding up LiDAR scene completion is a practically important problem, as slow inference speeds hinder real-world deployment. The paper demonstrates a significant speedup (5x) while maintaining or even improving completion quality.  This improvement could be significant for applications like autonomous driving and robotics. The study provides the first insights into the area of preference learning during distillation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method is well-motivated, addressing a clear limitation of diffusion models.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a significant performance improvement over existing methods in terms of both speed and quality (CD and JSD metrics).
    *   The ablation studies are thorough, investigating the impact of different inference steps, lambda values (noise scale), and teacher model performance.
    *   The qualitative results visually confirm the improved completion quality, especially in fine details.
    *   The paper clearly outlines the differences and similarities between their approach and related works like Diffusion-DPO and Score Distillation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies on the performance of the pre-trained teacher model (LiDiff). While they address this by fine-tuning the teacher, the student model's ultimate performance is still limited by the teacher.
    *   While the paper significantly improves CD and JSD, there's no improvement (or slightly worse performance) in terms of EMD. The authors don't fully explain this discrepancy.  It's not clear if the EMD differences are statistically significant.
    *   The paper doesn't delve deeply into why some LiDAR scene metrics worked better than others when building win-loss pairs.
    *   The implementation details for teacher model fine-tuning using Diffusion-DPO should be explored to ensure reproduction by external researchers.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a moderate impact on the field. It presents a practical approach for improving the efficiency of diffusion models for LiDAR scene completion. The idea of using scene metrics as preference signals could inspire other researchers to explore similar approaches in other areas. Further work could extend this approach to other 3D scene understanding tasks.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by efficiently utilizing diffusion models in 3D lidar scene completion. It successfully combines distillation and preference learning techniques, which could influence future research in this domain. While there are some weaknesses related to metric selection and dependence on the teacher model, the strengths of the work, including the performance improvements and thorough analysis, outweigh these concerns.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Aligning Generative Denoising with Discriminative Objectives Unleashes Diffusion for Visual Perception
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11457v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqi Pang, Xin Xu, Yu-Xiong Wang
- **Abstract**: With the success of image generation, generative diffusion models are increasingly adopted for discriminative tasks, as pixel generation provides a unified perception interface. However, directly repurposing the generative denoising process for discriminative objectives reveals critical gaps rarely addressed previously. Generative models tolerate intermediate sampling errors if the final distribution remains plausible, but discriminative tasks require rigorous accuracy throughout, as evidenced in challenging multi-modal tasks like referring image segmentation. Motivated by this gap, we analyze and enhance alignment between generative diffusion processes and perception tasks, focusing on how perception quality evolves during denoising. We find: (1) earlier denoising steps contribute disproportionately to perception quality, prompting us to propose tailored learning objectives reflecting varying timestep contributions; (2) later denoising steps show unexpected perception degradation, highlighting sensitivity to training-denoising distribution shifts, addressed by our diffusion-tailored data augmentation; and (3) generative processes uniquely enable interactivity, serving as controllable user interfaces adaptable to correctional prompts in multi-round interactions. Our insights significantly improve diffusion-based perception models without architectural changes, achieving state-of-the-art performance on depth estimation, referring image segmentation, and generalist perception tasks. Code available at https://github.com/ziqipang/ADDP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Aligning Generative Denoising with Discriminative Objectives: Unleashes Diffusion for Visual Perception" addresses the performance gap between generative diffusion models and discriminative tasks, particularly in perception-related areas like referring image segmentation (RIS). The authors identify and tackle key discrepancies between the generative denoising process and discriminative objectives by analyzing how perception quality evolves during denoising. Their contributions, collectively termed "ADDP," include:

1.  A learning objective that weights timesteps based on their contribution to perception quality.
2.  Diffusion-tailored data augmentation to simulate training-denoising distribution shift.
3.  A novel perspective viewing the denoising process as an interactive user interface enabling correctional prompts from users or foundation models.

The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of ADDP by enhancing state-of-the-art diffusion-based depth estimation, improving RIS performance, and boosting the capabilities of perception generalists.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by explicitly addressing the misalignment between generative diffusion processes and discriminative tasks in perception. The insights on uneven timestep contributions and training-denoising distribution shift are well-motivated by empirical observations. The idea of leveraging the denoising process for interactive correction is interesting.  However, individually, several components are not entirely new. Weighting timesteps in diffusion training and using data augmentation are known techniques, but their specific application and tailoring to bridge the generative-discriminative gap is novel.  The user interface concept builds on classifier-free guidance but gives it a specific, task-oriented justification.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of diffusion-based perception. By identifying and mitigating the key limitations, the authors make diffusion models more practical and effective for a wider range of perception tasks, particularly those requiring multi-modal reasoning and interaction. The improvement in RIS is noteworthy, demonstrating that ADDP can push diffusion models closer to discriminative baselines. The interactive user interface perspective opens interesting avenues for human-in-the-loop perception systems. The application to depth estimation and generalist models further strengthens the impact of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated insights.
    *   Thorough empirical evaluation across diverse perception tasks.
    *   Simple and plug-and-play nature of ADDP, facilitating easy adoption.
    *   Novel conceptualization of the denoising process as an interactive interface.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While effective, some aspects of ADDP (e.g., data augmentation) rely on task-specific design choices. More general augmentation strategies could enhance the applicability.
    *   The interactive user interface relies on the quality of correctional prompts from users/foundation models. The automatic generation of prompts with GPT4o may not always be reliable, potentially limiting performance. While ADDP can catch up with some discriminative baselines for the first time on RIS tasks, its performance is still underperforming when compared to the state-of-the-art.
    *   The paper lacks some in-depth theoretical analysis for the suggested approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on diffusion-based perception by:

    *   Inspiring more investigations into the alignment between generative and discriminative objectives.
    *   Providing a practical framework for enhancing diffusion models for perception tasks.
    *   Encouraging the development of interactive perception systems leveraging diffusion's denoising capabilities.

The work provides a clear and practical step to use diffusion models with data augumentations on different stages of diffusion.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by addressing a relevant problem in diffusion-based perception and proposing effective solutions. While some components are not entirely novel, their specific application and tailoring to bridge the generative-discriminative gap adds novelty. The empirical results demonstrate significant improvements across diverse perception tasks, indicating the practical value of the approach. Although the interactive user interface aspect is promising, it is also limited by the quality of external foundation models and a small performance improvement on certain tasks. The work can inspire new studies on diffusion models and their potential in perceptive learning.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
